L45: ## I. Executive Summary
L46: ## 1. Overview
L47: ## 1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L48: Ionis Pharmaceuticals (Applicant) submitted this new drug application (NDA) for olezarsen sodium (tradename Tryngolza), seeking approval of this APOC-III-directed antisense oligonucleotide to reduce triglycerides in adults with familial chylomicronemia syndrome (FCS), a rare genetic disorder. Patients with FCS have very high concentrations of triglycerides (TG) and are at increased risk for acute pancreatitis, a potentially fatal complication of this disease. The NDA was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team. Each discipline recommends approval for adult patients with FCS for the reduction of triglycerides and the signatory authority for this application concurs with those recommendations.
L49: Substantial evidence of effectiveness for olezarsen in patients with FCS was established using data from one adequate and well-controlled trial with confirmatory evidence (CE). A single trial in patients 18 years of age and older with FCS showed a large and clinically significant reduction in triglycerides that was statistically significant compared to placebo along with a numerical decrease in pancreatitis events; all patients were on a background of low-fat diet and optimized triglyceride-lowering therapy. Confirmatory evidence providing strong mechanistic support includes the well-established etiology of the disease, the mechanism of action of the therapy, and trial results in a related condition in patients with moderate to severe hypertriglyceridemia.
L50: The available safety data show that olezarsen is safe for its intended use. Common adverse reactions included injection site reactions, decreased platelet count, and arthralgia. Laboratory test abnormalities included decreases in platelet count and increases in glucose, liver enzymes, and low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C). These laboratory changes were notable but did not lead to clinical adverse events. Elevations in liver enzymes were more frequent in patients with moderate hypertriglyceridemia as compared to those patients with FCS. All the identified safety risks for olezarsen can be adequately mitigated through labeling and further evaluated during routine and enhanced pharmacovigilance.
L51: The NDA includes appropriate preapproval nonclinical studies, and no additional nonclinical studies or clinical pharmacology studies will be conducted as postmarketing requirements. We are requesting a postmarketing requirement to complete the ongoing trials in patients with severe hypertriglyceridemia to evaluate further the potential for drug induced liver injury with olezarsen. Enhanced pharmacovigilance will be requested for cases of liver toxicity.
L52: As described in the Benefit/Risk Framework below, we conclude that the reduction in triglycerides, a surrogate for the reduction in risk for acute pancreatitis, observed with use of olezarsen outweighs the risks when olezarsen is used as recommended in the approved labeling.
L53: ## 1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L54: Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and well-controlled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence.
L55: Familial chylomicronemia syndrome (FCS) is a rare genetic disorder. Individuals have very high concentrations of triglycerides (TG) and are at increased risk for acute pancreatitis, a potentially life-threatening consequence of this condition. Reduction of serum TG levels in patients with severe hypertriglyceridemia (TG \ge 500 \text{ mg/dL}) is considered a surrogate for reduction of the risk of acute pancreatitis. Olezarsen is a new molecular entity and would be first-in-class for the treatment of FCS, for which there are no FDA-approved therapies.
L56: Primary evidence of olezarsen's efficacy on TG reduction in adult patients with FCS was demonstrated by a single trial, ISIS 678354-CS3. In this 53-week, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized trial of adult subjects with FCS, olezarsen 80 mg as an add-on to background TG-lowering therapy decreased TG at Month 6 (average of Weeks 23, 25, and 27) by 44.5% (95% confidence interval [CI]: -72, -17; p=0.0015 compared to placebo)1. The treatment effect with olezarsen was seen in adult subjects with fasting TG levels \ge 880 \text{ mg/dL} despite low-fat diet (\le 20 \text{ grams fat per day}) and optimized lipid lowering therapies and was similar across important subgroups. Over the 12-month treatment period, the numerical incidence of pancreatitis in subjects treated with olezarsen 80 mg was lower compared with placebo (one subject reported 1 event of adjudicated acute pancreatitis (AP) in the olezarsen 80 mg group compared with 11 events experienced by seven subjects in the placebo group). The results of this trial are not typical for the clinical trajectory of FCS and support the efficacy of olezarsen in this population.
L57: The approval decision for olezarsen also relies on confirmatory evidence from a related indication from Trial ISIS 678354-CS8. Trial CS8 demonstrated efficacy on TG lowering for this same dose, 80 mg administered Q4W as a subcutaneous (SC) injection for 1 year, in adults with hypertriglyceridemia (HTG) and atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (established or at increased risk for), and/or with severe HTG (sHTG). The results from Trial CS8 are similar to those observed in Trial CS3. In Trial CS8, the difference between the olezarsen 80 mg group and the placebo group in percent change in fasting TG from baseline to Month 6 (average of Weeks 25 and 27) was -56.5% (95% CI: -67.4%, -45.6%; p&lt;0.0001)2. No subjects in any treatment group had an adjudicated AP event during the treatment period in this trial. At Month 6, placebo-adjusted mean percent change in fasting non-high-density lipoprotein cholesterol (non-HDL-C) levels from baseline for olezarsen 80 mg was -24.5% (95% CI: -37.6, -11.5) and for apolipoprotein B (ApoB) was -19.3% (95% CI: -29.8, -8.8)3. This randomized, controlled trial

L61: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L62: was highly persuasive, having demonstrated a large treatment effect on TG, a highly statistically significant result, and reassuring changes on secondary lipid endpoints.
L63: Typically, two adequate and well-controlled trials are required to achieve the statutory definition of substantial evidence. However, in the case of FCS, given the rarity of the condition, the seriousness of the disease, the unmet medical need to treat this disease, the persuasiveness of the results of Trial ISIS 678354-CS3, and the robustness of the confirmatory evidence in subjects with moderate or severe HTG, the Division of Diabetes Lipid Disorders and Obesity concludes that the Applicant has provided the SEE required by law [see 21 CFR 314.126(a)(b)] to support approval.
L64: Although this application relies primarily on a single, blinded, placebo-controlled trial, the results constitute SEE in this serious, rare condition with a well-characterized natural history. The application supports approval of olezarsen, administered SC 80 mg monthly, as an adjunct to low-fat diet for the treatment of adults with FCS.
L65: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L66: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L67: Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
L68: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	Familial chylomicronemia syndrome (FCS) is a rare autosomal recessive condition leading to a deficiency in lipoprotein lipase (LPL) enzyme activity from inactivation of the LPL gene or genes encoding its co-factors (such as apolipoprotein CII).
Prevalence of FCS is 1 to 10 in 1,000,000 individuals.
Characterized by severely elevated chylomicrons and plasma triglycerides.
The most serious complication is acute pancreatitis, which can be life-threatening.
Other clinical manifestations include eruptive cutaneous xanthomas, lipemia retinalis, episodic abdominal pain, and hepatosplenomegaly.
FCS is a rare genetic disorder of lipid metabolism characterized by excessive chylomicronemia and severe hypertriglyceridemia.
The most serious complication is acute pancreatitis. Other severe and debilitating symptoms include abdominal pain, fatigue, and depression.

Current treatment options	Restrictive low-fat diet (<20 grams daily) is the mainstay of therapy that can lower triglycerides, but adherence is difficult.
Other triglyceride-lowering agents, such fibrates, niacin, or omega-3 fatty acids, are generally not effective in FCS.
No approved drug therapies
Current management of the disease is severe dietary restriction. There is no effective drug therapy, and there exists a high unmet need for effective and safe treatment.
L69: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L70: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	The efficacy of olezarsen in patients with FCS was evaluated in a single phase 3 trial (CS3).
Randomized (1:1 ratio to Cohort A [50 mg] or Cohort B [80 mg]; each cohort was further randomized 2:1 to groups receiving either the olezarsen dose for the cohort or a matching volume of placebo, respectively), double blind, placebo control, 53-week trial of 66 subjects with FCS, enriched for history of acute pancreatitis or symptoms suggestive of pancreatitis (80%), evaluating olezarsen 50 or 80 mg administered subcutaneously once every 4 weeks versus placebo.Primary endpoint of mean percent change in fasting TG levels from baseline to Month 6 for olezarsen 80 mg compared with placebo: 33% decrease in TG versus 11% increase for pooled placebo; placebo-adjusted mean percent decrease of 44.5% (p=0.0015; 95% CI: -72.0, -17.0).
Treatment difference persisted over time: placebo adjusted percent change in TG levels from baseline was -61.4% (95% CI: -101.9, -20.8) at Month 12 in the olezarsen 80 mg treated group.
At Month 6, placebo-adjusted mean percent decreases in fasting non-HDL-C levels from baseline for olezarsen 80 mg was 25.7% (95% CI: -44.3, -7.1)
During the Treatment Period, 1 subject had 1 event of adjudicated acute pancreatitis in each of the olezarsen treatment groups (total n = 2) compared with 11 events by 7 subjects in the placebo group.
Approximately 88% or 38/43 subjects in the pooled olezarsen treatment group completed the one-year trial. Three (14%) subjects in the olezarsen 80 mg group compared to 1 (4%) subject in the placebo group discontinued study drug.

Olezarsen has demonstrated benefit to patients with FCS characterized by lower TG levels which are expected to reduce the risk of pancreatitis.
Olezarsen 80 mg was tolerated by the majority of subjects.
The triglyceride results were compelling, the reduction in acute pancreatitis events was reassuring, and the discontinuation rate was acceptable.
L71: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L72: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Confirmatory mechanistic evidence from Study CS8, randomized (3:1 allocation within 50 mg and 80 mg Cohorts), double-blind, placebo-controlled, 53-week trial of olezarsen 80 mg or olezarsen 50 mg once every 4 weeks in 154 subjects with hypertriglyceridemia (HTG) and established or increased risk of atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD), or with severe HTG (sHTG). Approximately 90% had baseline TG levels < 500 mg/dL.Primary endpoint of mean percent change in fasting TG levels from baseline to Month 6 compared with placebo: 64% decrease in TG with olezarsen 80mg versus 8% for placebo; placebo-adjusted mean percent decrease of 56.5% (p<0.0001; 95% CI: -67.4, -45.6).a
Treatment difference persisted over time: Placebo adjusted percent change in TG levels from baseline was -54.1% (95% CI: -65.8, -42.52)b at Month 12 in the olezarsen 80 mg treated group.
At Month 6, placebo-adjusted mean percent decrease in fasting non-HDL-C levels from baseline for olezarsen 80 mg was 24.5% (95% CI: -37.6, -11.5) and for apoB was 19.3% (95% CI: -29.8, -8.8).c
No subjects in any treatment group had an adjudicated acute pancreatitis event during the Treatment Period.
Approximately 82% or 94/115 subjects in the pooled olezarsen treatment group completed the one-year trial. Seven subjects (12%) in the olezarsen 80 mg group and 3 subjects (8%) in the placebo group discontinued study drug.
L73: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L74: Risk and risk management	In CS3, 43 subjects with FCS were exposed to olezarsen. Twenty-two subjects were treated with olezarsen 80 mg every 4 weeks with a median exposure of 52 weeks (mean, 46 weeks).
In CS8, 57 subjects were treated with olezarsen 80 mg every 4 weeks with a median exposure of 52 weeks (mean, 48 weeks).
Supportive data include CS13, an open-label extension of CS3 in 54 subjects with FCS treated with olezarsen 80 mg every 4 weeks with a median exposure of 33 weeks (mean, 35 weeks).
Supportive data include CS7, an open-label study in 24 subjects with FCS previously treated with volanesorsen, switched to olezarsen 80 mg every 4 weeks. The median duration of exposure was 52 weeks (mean 47 weeks).
No data in pediatric patients.
Hypersensitivity reactions: In CS3, 2 (9%) subjects in the olezarsen 80 mg group discontinued because of hypersensitivity or flu-like reactions compared to none in placebo. There were no serious hypersensitivity reactions in CS3 or CS8 although 3 serious reactions were seen in the open-label extension trials. The onset of symptoms usually occur within minutes or same day of the injection.
Decreases in Platelet Count: Olezarsen treatment causes reductions in platelet counts. Platelet counts remain on average 5 to 15% below baseline. The incidence of bleeding events was low and similar across treatment groups. No olezarsen-treated subject in the FCS trial CS3 paused, reduced, or discontinued olezarsen dosing due to a bleeding AE. No olezarsen-treated subjects, to date, have reported a platelet count below 50 \times 10^9/L.
The most concerning risk is serious hypersensitivity reactions, including symptoms of bronchospasm, diffuse erythema, chills, and myalgias. This risk is clinically monitorable by the healthcare provider. Olezarsen should be discontinued if serious hypersensitivity reactions occur.
The clinical impact of the drug-induced LDL-C and apoB increases in the FCS population is uncertain, but likely minimal in this population, because despite increases in LDL-C, the value remains low (i.e., <70 \text{ mg/dL}) for most study subjects. LDL-C is monitorable and can be mitigated with lipid-modifying therapy.
Small increases in liver enzymes occurred but the incidence of hepatic events was low and similar across treatment groups. This is described in Section 6.1 of the prescribing information (PI). A postmarketing requirement will assess further this potential liver signal in patients with severe hypertriglyceridemia along with enhanced pharmacovigilance for liver toxicity in the postmarket setting in patients with FCS.
The 80 mg dose of olezarsen, administered subcutaneously every month, was reasonably well-tolerated throughout these trials. Approximately 90% of patients with FCS in trial CS3 completed the one year trial.
All risks associated with olezarsen can be adequately addressed through labeling and no specific monitoring, beyond that necessary for the treatment and management of FCS, is recommended. Olezarsen is contraindicated in patients who have experienced serious hypersensitivity reactions with olezarsens. This finding is described in sections 4 and 5 of the PI. Common adverse reactions included injection site reactions, decreased platelet count, and arthralgia. Laboratory test abnormalities included decreases in platelet count and increases in glucose, liver enzymes, and low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C). These laboratory changes were notable but did not lead to clinical adverse events and resolved with discontinuation of the drug.
L75: - **Increases in Liver Enzymes:** The incidence of hepatic events was low and similar across treatment groups. Olezarsen was associated with mean increases in ALT, AST, GGT within the first 3 months which stabilized, and remained within normal range. Liver enzymes returned towards baseline with discontinuation of olezarsen. In some subjects, increases in liver enzymes lead to discontinuation or interruption of study drug. There were no cases of Hy's Law, an indicator of drug-induced liver injury.
- **Dysglycemia:** In trial CS3, average glucose and HbA1c values increased slightly more in the olezarsen treatment groups than placebo. Using an algorithmic FMQ for hyperglycemia consisting of adverse events, new antidiabetic medication, and laboratory values, a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects without a medical history of diabetes at baseline (52%) versus placebo-treated subjects (35%) fulfilled at least one of the FMQ criterion, suggesting a safety signal for worsening glycemic control.
- **Injection site reactions (ISRs):** ISRs, characterized primarily by erythema and discoloration, occur with olezarsen treatment. In CS3, ISRs occurred in 3 (14%) subjects in both the olezarsen 80 mg and 50 mg groups vs. 2 (9%) subjects in placebo group. None of the ISRs were serious, no subjects in FCS trials reduced study drug, withdrew from the trial, or required treatment of the ISR.
- **Changes in LDL-C and Apo B:** Increases in LDL-C and apoB of approximately 50% from baseline were noted in the FCS population treated with olezarsen compared to a &lt;1% increase in those treated with placebo. In contrast, in trial CS8 of subjects with moderate to high levels of triglycerides, LDL-C and apoB changes were smaller and were decreased in the olezarsen groups as compared to placebo.
- **Immunogenicity:** Treatment with olezarsen results in development of treatment-emergent anti-drug antibodies in approximately 40% of treated subjects. No relationship between adverse events, including hypersensitivity events, and ADA status was seen in CS3 and CS8.
L76: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L77: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Adverse events in CS3: Two (9%) subjects in the olezarsen 80 mg group compared to none in the placebo group discontinued treatment due to an AE. Adverse events that were increased in the 80 mg olezarsen treatment groups versus placebo were related to reductions in platelet count, injection site reactions, nasopharyngitis, erythema, and arthralgia.
Tolerability in CS3: Approximately 88% of subjects in the pooled olezarsen treatment group completed the one-year trial.

L80: c FDA results from Table 16, Table 35
L81: Abbreviations: ADA, antidrug antibody; AE, adverse event; ALT, alanine aminotransferase; apoB, apolipoprotein B; ASCVD, atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; CI, confidence interval; FCS, familial chylomicronemia syndrome; FMQ, FDA medical query; GGT, gamma-glutamyltransferase; HDL-C, high-density lipoprotein cholesterol; HTG, hypertriglyceridemia; ISR, injection site reaction; LDL-C, low-density lipoprotein cholesterol; LPL, lipoprotein lipase; sHTG, severe hypertriglyceridemia; TG, triglycerides
L82: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L83: The benefit of TG lowering, with the expectation of a reduction in risk for acute pancreatitis, in patients with FCS outweighs the potential risks in this population.
L84: FCS is a rare genetic disorder of lipid metabolism characterized by excessive chylomicronemia and severe hypertriglyceridemia. The most serious complication is acute pancreatitis. Common, and sometimes debilitating, symptoms include episodic abdominal pain, fatigue, and depression. Other clinical manifestations include eruptive cutaneous xanthomas, lipemia retinalis, and hepatosplenomegaly. Current management of the disease is severe dietary restriction. There is no effective drug therapy, and there exists a high unmet need.
L85: Olezarsen 80 mg once monthly effectively lowers TG levels in patients with FCS. TG lowering is considered a surrogate for reduction in the risk of acute pancreatitis. Numerically lower incidence of pancreatitis in patients assigned to FCS is supportive of the expected clinical benefit.
L86: Trial CS3 was a 53-week, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomized trial of adult subjects with FCS with fasting TG levels \ge 880 mg/dL despite very low-fat (\le 20 grams per day) diet and optimized lipid-lowering therapies. Olezarsen 80 mg administered every 4 weeks (as an add-on to background TG-lowering therapy) decreased TG at Month 6 by 44.5% compared to placebo. Over the 12-month treatment period, the numerical incidence of pancreatitis in subjects randomized to olezarsen 80 mg (one event experienced by one subject) was lower compared with the incidence in patients assigned to placebo (11 events experienced by seven subjects). Treatment discontinuation at one year was acceptable (14% in the olezarsen group versus 4% in the placebo group).
L87: Even considering uncertainty around the magnitude of the benefit on acute pancreatitis based on reliance on a surrogate endpoint, the anticipated benefit clearly outweighs the risk in the FCS population.
L88: The safety of olezarsen in patients with FCS was established by the clinical program. The most concerning risk is serious hypersensitivity reactions, including symptoms of bronchospasm, diffuse erythema, chills, and myalgias. This risk is clinically monitorable and may be adequately communicated in the Contraindications and Warnings and Precautions section of the prescribing information. The risk for drug-induced liver injury was primarily seen in patients with moderate hypertriglyceridemia. The risk in patients with FCS is very low but an additional evaluation of this potential risk will be assessed further in postmarket requirements.
L89: The clinical impact of the drug-induced LDL-C and apoB increases in the FCS population is uncertain but does not outweigh the benefit. Although LDL-C increased by approximately 50% and ApoB by approximately 12% compared to placebo, baseline values were very low (mean baseline LDL-C was 17 mg/dL in placebo and to 27 mg/dL in the 80 mg arms, while mean baseline total ApoB was 59.7 mg/dL and 58.4 mg/dL, respectively), thus the impact of these percentage increases is likely minimal. The absolute increase in these parameters was relatively low magnitude and remained within the normal range for most subjects. LDL-C is monitorable, and elevations can be mitigated with lipid-modifying therapy. Notably, LDL-C and ApoB decreased with olezarsen 80 mg versus placebo in the moderate and severe HTG populations in CS8 compared to placebo.
L90: NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L91: Other adverse events, including decreases in platelet count, increases in liver enzymes, and increase in glucose are monitorable and may be communicated in the Adverse Reactions section of labeling.
L92: Although the safety database is small, strengths include the placebo-controlled design, use of two doses to assess dose-response, the 1-year treatment duration, with additional longer-term exposure in the OLE, the low study drop-out rate, and small amount of missing data.
L93: Modeling and simulation were used to bridge the Q4W regimen evaluated in clinical trials to the once monthly regimen proposed for the marketed product. There are not expected to be clinically meaningful differences between the to-be-marketed one monthly dosage and the Q4W regimen used in trials.
L94: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L95: ## 3. Introduction
L96: This review serves as the interdisciplinary assessment for NDA 218614, olezarsen sodium (Tryngolza) injection.
L97: The Applicant (Ionis Pharmaceuticals, Inc.) is developing olezarsen as an adjunct to diet to reduce triglycerides (TG) in adults with familial chylomicronemia syndrome (FCS). The recommended dosage of olezarsen is 80 mg administered by subcutaneous (SC) injection once monthly.
L98: FCS is a rare, autosomal recessive condition affecting 1 to 10 individuals in a million. Individuals with FCS have persistent elevated levels of TG, which increases their risk of pancreatitis, a potentially life-threatening consequence of this condition. The onset of symptoms in individuals with FCS usually occurs in childhood or adolescence and includes eruptive xanthomas, lipemia retinalis, episodic abdominal pain, and hepatosplenomegaly. A restrictive low-fat diet is the mainstay of therapy and is effective, but adherence is difficult. TG-lowering drugs, such as fibrates and fish oils, are largely ineffective in reducing TG in this population. There are no FDA-approved products for the treatment of FCS.
L99: Olezarsen is an antisense oligonucleotide (ASO) inhibitor of apolipoprotein C-III (ApoC-III) production, covalently linked to a ligand containing three N-acetyl galactosamine (GalNAc) residues to enable delivery of the ASO to hepatocytes. Olezarsen causes degradation of *apoC-III* mRNA through binding to *apoC-III* mRNA, which results in a reduction of serum ApoC-III protein. ApoC-III is a key regulator of TG metabolism via lipoprotein lipase (LPL) dependent and independent mechanisms. By reducing ApoC-III production, olezarsen decreases the negative effect of ApoC-III on TG clearance, thereby lowering TG levels. The lipid biomarker TG has historically been used as a surrogate for reduction in risk of pancreatitis and the basis of full approval for TG-lowering drugs in patients with hypertriglyceridemia (HTG) (TG \ge 500 mg/dL).
L100: Olezarsen shares the same nucleotide sequence as its parent drug, volanesorsen. Volanesorsen received a conditional marketing authorization for patients with FCS at high risk for pancreatitis by the European Medical Agency in 2019 but received a complete response letter from the Agency in 2018, (b)(4)
L101: (EMA 2019). Olezarsen is differentiated from volanesorsen by its conjugation to GalNAc, which permits a different therapeutic dosing regimen of olezarsen (80 mg administered SC every 4 weeks [Q4W]) compared to volanesorsen (300 mg administered SC once per week [Q1W]).
L102: The IND application for olezarsen was opened in December 2017, with a phase 2 trial of individuals with moderately elevated TG (TG 200 to 500 mg/dL) with established coronary artery disease (Trial ISIS 678354-CS2). Following the primary analysis of the phase 2 trial, the Applicant elected to focus on the study populations of FCS and severe hypertriglyceridemia (sHTG) (TG \ge 500 mg/dL). Multiple interactions between the Applicant and Division have occurred to discuss the Applicant's development plans for FCS, including Type C meetings in
L103: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L104: July 2020 and August 2022, advice letters following review of phase 3 protocols, statistical analysis plan (SAP), integrated summary of safety, and a pre-NDA meeting in December 2023.
L105: Olezarsen is a new molecular entity and is not currently marketed in the United States. Olezarsen would be first-in-class for the treatment of FCS, for which there are no FDA-approved therapies. Olezarsen received fast track designation in January 2023, breakthrough therapy designation in February 2024, and priority review for this NDA submission. Refer to Section 12 for a detailed summary of the regulatory history.
L106: Trial ISIS 678354-CS3, the pivotal trial in this submission, was conducted to evaluate the efficacy and safety of olezarsen as compared to placebo in 66 adults with genetically confirmed FCS. The trial was designed as a double-blind, placebo-controlled, 53-week trial. Eligible study subjects were randomized to either olezarsen 50 mg, olezarsen 80 mg, or placebo administered SC Q4W. The primary efficacy endpoint was percentage change in fasting TG from baseline to Month 6 (average of Week 23, 25, and 27 assessments). Other evaluations included assessment of the effect of olezarsen on non-HDL-C concentrations and adjudicated acute pancreatitis (AP) event rates. Safety and tolerability assessments included adverse events (AEs), clinical laboratory tests, electrocardiogram (ECG), use of concomitant medications, and independently adjudicated event rate of major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE) for olezarsen compared with placebo.
L107: The safety and confirmatory evidence of effectiveness of olezarsen in patients with HTG was provided with data from phase 2b Trial ISIS 678354-CS8, and other trials in the olezarsen clinical development program. Trial CS8 was a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial evaluating olezarsen 50 mg or olezarsen 80 mg administered Q4W as an SC injection for 1 year in 154 subjects with HTG and atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (established or at increased risk for), and/or with sHTG. The primary efficacy endpoint was the percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 6, which was defined as the average of Weeks 25 and 27, compared with the placebo group. Safety assessments were similar to those of Trial CS3.
L108: The review team identified three review issues relevant to the evaluation of benefit (Section 6.3)
L109: - Confirmation of efficacy of TG reduction of the 80 mg dose in Trial CS3
- (b) (4)
- Confirmation of the reduction in pancreatitis events in Trial CS3
L110: At the start of the review process, several potential risks were identified for olezarsen based on prior knowledge of both olezarsen and volanesorsen. These risks (or potential risks) were multidisciplinary and included concerns such as hypersensitivity reactions, thrombocytopenia, hepatic-related or renal-related events, dysglycemia, and the bridging of the vial and autoinjector (commercial product), as the 90% confidence interval (CI) for maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}) for the 80 mg dose is outside the standard upper CI limit of 80 to 125%.
L111: NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L112: At the conclusion of this NDA review, the team identified the following risks that were important to highlight in the overall benefit/risk assessment (please refer to Section 7.7):
L113: - Hypersensitivity reactions
- Platelet reductions and bleeding events
- Hepatic-related AEs
- Dysglycemia
- Injection site reactions (ISRs)
- Changes in low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C) and apolipoprotein B (ApoB)
L114: ## 3.1. Review Issue List
L115: ## 3.1.1. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L116: The review team identified the key review issues, listed in Sections 3.1.1 and 3.1.2 below, relevant to the evaluation of benefit and risk, respectively. A detailed assessment of these benefit and risk issues can be found in Sections 6.3 and 7.7, respectively.
L117: ## 3.1.1.1. Efficacy of Triglyceride Reduction of 80 mg Olezarsen in Trial CS3 in the FCS Population
L118: The review team concluded that the results of the primary endpoint of TG reduction of olezarsen 80 mg dose was statistically significant compared to placebo and was sufficient to establish the efficacy of olezarsen 80 mg dose for TG reduction in the FCS population.
L119: (b) (4)
L120: ## 3.1.1.2.
L121: (b) (4)
L122: ## 3.1.1.3. Acceptability of the Secondary Endpoint of Acute Pancreatitis Risk Reduction
L123: Trial CS3 assessed key secondary endpoints of a reduction in prospectively adjudicated AP; it is the first outcome trial submitted to the FDA to measure adjudicated AP in an HTG population. The nominal finding of a reduction in the rate of AP is consistent with the pathophysiology that links elevated levels of TG with AP and the reduction of TG with a subsequent reduction in AP.
L124: ## 3.1.1.4. Adequacy of the Confirmatory Evidence in Trial CS8 in the Severe and Moderate Hypertriglyceridemic Populations
L125: The following lines of evidence constitute adequate confirmatory evidence of treatment benefit: (1) well-understood underlying disease pathophysiology; (2) targeted mechanism of action of olezarsen; (3) evidence of drug effect on a pharmacologically relevant biomarker; and (4) evidence of exposure-response on the biomarker.
L126: The review team established that there is adequate confirmatory evidence to generate substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) with Trial CS8, a phase 2 trial in severe and moderate HTG populations. For a detailed discussion, refer to Section 6.3.4.
L127: ## 3.1.1.5. Confirmation of Other Lipoproteins and Parameters in Trial CS3 in the FCS Population
L128: The lipid parameters LDL-C, non-HDL-C, total ApoB and ApoC-III levels were confirmed by the statistical team. These changes in the lipid profile (LDL-C, non-HDL-C, and total ApoB) are expected based on the lipid metabolism pathway and do not alter the favorable benefit-risk profile of olezarsen 80 mg of reducing TG and the risk of AP. The reduction of ApoC-III confirmed the mechanism of action of olezarsen.
L129: ## 3.1.2. Key Safety Review Issues
L130: ## 3.1.2.1. Hypersensitivity Reactions
L131: Hypersensitivity reactions, including symptoms of bronchospasm, diffuse erythema, chills, and myalgias, requiring medical treatment have occurred in patients treated with olezarsen.
L132: ## 3.1.2.2. Platelet Reductions and Bleeding Events
L133: Olezarsen can cause reductions in platelet count, including reductions below the lower limit of normal. No olezarsen-treated patient with FCS had severe thrombocytopenia, defined as a platelet count &lt;50,000/\text{mm}^3. There were no major bleeding events associated with a low platelet count. Overall, the proportion of patients experiencing a bleeding adverse event was similar across treatment groups.
L134: ## 3.1.2.3. Increases in Liver Enzymes
L135: Increases from baseline in liver enzymes within the normal range were observed with olezarsen treatment in the FCS population. The incidence of hepatic events was low and similar across treatment groups. In some subjects, increases in liver enzymes lead to discontinuation or interruption of study drug. There were no cases of Hy's Law, an indicator of drug-induced liver injury.
L136: ## 3.1.2.4. Dysglycemia
L137: Small increases in average values in fasting glucose and HbA1c over time were observed with olezarsen treatment in the FCS population. Using an algorithmic FDA Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities Query (FMQ) for hyperglycemia consisting of adverse events, new antidiabetic medication, and laboratory values, a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects without a medical history of diabetes at baseline (52%) versus placebo-treated subjects (35%) fulfilled at least one of the FMQ criterion, suggesting a safety signal for worsening glucose metabolism.
L138: ## 3.1.2.5. Injection Site Reactions
L139: Injection site reactions (ISRs), characterized primarily by erythema and discoloration, occur with olezarsen treatment. In CS3, ISRs occurred in 3 (14%) subjects in both the olezarsen 80 mg and 50 mg groups vs. 2 (9%) subjects in placebo group. None of the ISRs were serious, no subjects in FCS trials reduced study drug, withdrew from the trial, or required treatment of the injection site reaction (ISR).
L140: ## 3.1.2.6. Increase in LDL-C
L141: Increases in LDL-C and total apolipoprotein B (apoB) were observed in the FCS population treated with olezarsen compared to those treated with placebo. Despite increases in LDL-C, the value remained low (i.e., &lt;70 mg/dL) for most patients.
L142: ## 3.1.2.7. Imbalance in Cardiovascular Events
L143: In the olezarsen clinical development program there was a higher number of deaths, serious cardiac events, and adjudicated MACE with olezarsen treatment compared to placebo treatment. Most of the serious cardiac events and MACE occurred in a non-FCS population with underlying cardiovascular comorbidities. The number of MACE and duration of exposure is too small to provide a definitive causality assessment for olezarsen. At this time there is no clear signal of cardiovascular harm in the FCS population.
L144: ## 3.1.2.8. Immunogenicity
L145: Treatment with olezarsen results in development of treatment-emergent antidrug antibodies (ADA) in approximately 40% of treated subjects. No relationship between adverse events, including hypersensitivity events, and ADA status was seen in CS3 and CS8.
L146: ## 3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
L147: The primary evidence of efficacy and safety for olezarsen for the proposed indication of an adjunct to diet to reduce TG in adults with FCS is provided by a single adequate and well-controlled trial conducted in the United States, Canada, the United Kingdom, and Europe (Trial CS3; ISIS 678354-CS3). CS3 is a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled phase 3 trial. Refer to Sections 6.2.2 and 7.6.2 for additional details. In addition to Trial CS3, a trial conducted
L148: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L149: in the United States and Canada (Trial CS8; ISIS 678354-CS8) provided confirmatory evidence of olezarsen effectiveness to substantiate the results of CS3 and provided supportive safety data. CS8 is a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled phase 2b trial. Refer to Sections 6.2.3 and 7.6.3 for additional details. Recommendations from FDA during protocol development were, for the most part, incorporated into the trial design for CS3 and CS8.
L150: Additional supportive information was provided by the open-label extension (OLE) of the CS3 pivotal trial (ISIS 678354-CS13) and the OLE of the CS7 trial (ISIS 678354-CS7); the latter trial in subjects previously administered volanesorsen. These four trials provide the basis of the benefit-risk assessment for olezarsen in patients diagnosed with FCS. In general, these trials included similar subject populations (FCS in CS3, CS13, and CS7; moderate/severe HTG in CS8), dosage regimens for olezarsen, as well as primary endpoint definitions and timing of assessments in CS3 and CS8. The Applicant asserts that all of the trials were conducted according to the International Council for Harmonisation tripartite guideline on Good Clinical Practice. Refer to Section 7.5 for additional details.
L151: Additional supportive safety and efficacy information from Trial CS2 (ISIS 678354-CS2), which enrolled subjects with HTG with or at high risk for cardiovascular disease (CVD), was also submitted. This trial used smaller doses than the proposed marketed dosage of 80 mg monthly. The Applicant provided additional information to support the applicability of the data from the non-U.S. sites to the U.S. population and provided full clinical study reports and data sets for all five trials. Although olezarsen is not yet commercially available in any country, volanesorsen shares the identical oligonucleotide sequence as olezarsen and is approved outside of the United States. Given their similarities, the safety for volanesorsen was reviewed during the previous FDA review for volanesorsen (NDA 210645) as well as the global marketed experience of volanesorsen.

L171: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L172: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L173: Olezarsen (ISIS 678354) is an *apoC-III* mRNA-directed ASO proposed for an indication as an adjunct to diet to reduce TG in adults with FCS. Olezarsen is comprised of the active ASO component (identical to volanesorsen, which is marketed outside the United States) that is covalently attached to triantennary N-acetyl galactosamine (GalNAc3) via a (b) (4)
L174: linker to form an ASO-GalNAc conjugate. The ASO portion binds to *apoC-III* mRNA and the GalNAc3 portion targets the hepatocyte-specific asialoglycoprotein receptor, which is expressed abundantly on the hepatocyte cell surface. After internalization, the GalNAc3 and linker are cleaved to release the free ASO. The ASO portion of olezarsen binds to the 3' untranslated region of *apoC-III* mRNA and results in ribonuclease H1-mediated degradation, preventing the production of ApoC-III protein.
L175: FCS is a rare, serious, autosomal recessive inherited disease of lipid metabolism characterized by severely impaired LPL activity and extremely high levels of plasma TG carried primarily by chylomicrons (resulting in HTG and hyperchylomicronemia). Olezarsen targets *apoC-III* mRNA and reduces expression of ApoC-III protein, a key regulator of plasma TG levels (Figure 1), leading to increased TG LPL activity, hepatic uptake of TG-rich lipoproteins, and hepatic lipase activity. The increased clearance of plasma TG (as chylomicrons and very low-density lipoprotein particles) would be expected to significantly reduce plasma TG and chylomicron levels in individuals with FCS.
L176: Figure 1. A Schematic of ApoC-III Inhibition of Lipoprotein Transport
L177: Source: Figure modified from (Ward et al. 2022)
Abbreviations: ANGPTL3, angiotensin-like protein 3; apoB, apolipoprotein B; apoC-III, apolipoprotein C-III; CM, chylomicron; CMR, CM remnant; IDL, intermediate density lipoprotein; LDL, low-density lipoprotein; LPL, lipoprotein lipase; VLDL, very low-density lipoprotein.
L178: The pharmacological activity of olezarsen was characterized in vitro in primary human hepatocytes (HepatoPac®) and primary hepatocytes of transgenic mice expressing the human *apoC-III* gene. Olezarsen produced a concentration-dependent reduction of *apoC-III* mRNA with an IC50 of 0.18 µM and a maximal efficacy of 82% in human hepatocytes, and reduced *apoC-III* mRNA levels in transgenic mouse hepatocytes with an IC50 of 0.012 µM and maximal efficacy of 89%.
L179: Studies with olezarsen or a mouse-specific surrogate were conducted in several animal models. In wild-type mice, a mouse-specific *apoC-III* surrogate ASO was used (ISIS 838707, a murine
L180: GalNac3-conjugated version), due to the divergence in sequence between mouse and human *apoC-III* mRNA. In wild-type mice, using the surrogate, liver *apoC-III* mRNA expression and plasma TG levels were reduced by up to 88% and 76%, respectively, compared to control mice following 6-week treatment with olezarsen. Studies were also conducted in human *apoC-III* transgenic mice. In the transgenic mice, a single dose of olezarsen up to 10 mg/kg resulted in dose-dependent reductions in liver *apoC-III* mRNA by up to 77%, and reduced plasma ApoC-III protein and plasma TG levels by up to 87%. Olezarsen exhibits full complementarity to the *apoC-III* mRNA transcript of humans and cynomolgus monkeys. In monkeys administered olezarsen once weekly for up to 39 weeks, there were dose-dependent reductions in liver *apoC-III* mRNA of up to 90% and reductions in plasma ApoC-III protein of up to 77%. Minimal effects on plasma TG levels were seen in monkeys given olezarsen, which was likely because chow-fed monkeys have very low plasma TG levels at baseline. Overall, these animal models demonstrated dose and time-dependent reductions in hepatic *apoC-III* mRNA, plasma ApoC-III protein, and (in rodents) plasma TG levels (see Section 13 for further details). The observed effects were consistent with the expected pharmacological properties of the compound. Results from these animal models provide reasonable proof-of-concept that supported the initiation of human trials as assessed using a clinically translatable and monitorable pharmacological endpoint, plasma TG.
L181: Of note, a robust series of nonclinical pharmacology studies were previously conducted with volanesorsen, the unconjugated active antisense oligonucleotide component of olezarsen, which lacks the GalNac moiety but is otherwise structurally identical. Volanesorsen has not received marketing approval in the United States, but the nonclinical studies with the active component are considered relevant and were cross-referenced by the Applicant to the current NDA. Olezarsen and volanesorsen demonstrate a similar pharmacologic profile (sequence identical and specific knockdown of *apoC-III* mRNA in monkeys and humans) and differ primarily in their pharmacokinetic and tissue distribution profiles. Olezarsen is specifically distributed to the liver and therefore a more rapid liver uptake and lower nominal doses of olezarsen were theoretically achievable compared to volanesorsen, which lacks GalNAc.
L182: There was no evidence of off-target activity against other mRNA or downstream protein targets. Olezarsen is not an inhibitor or an inducer of major drug-metabolizing cytochrome P450 enzymes nor is it a substrate or inhibitor of various drug transporters. The established pharmacologic class for olezarsen should be consistent with prior members of the same drug class, i.e., “Olezarsen is an APOC-III-directed ASO indicated as an adjunct to diet to reduce triglycerides in adults with familial chylomicronemia syndrome (FCS)”.
L183: ## 5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
L184: **Table 5. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics**
L185: Characteristic	Drug Information
Pharmacologic Activity	
Established pharmacologic class (EPC)	APOC-III-directed antisense oligonucleotide (ASO)
Mechanism of action	Olezarsen is a 2'-O-methoxyethyl (2'-MOE) chimeric ASO covalently bound to triantennary N-acetyl galactosamine (GalNAc3), a high-affinity ligand for the hepatocyte-specific asialoglycoprotein receptor. The ASO portion of olezarsen
L186: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L187: Characteristic	Drug Information
is the parent drug, volanesorsen, and is designed to bind to the human apoC-III messenger ribonucleic acid (mRNA). Olezarsen causes degradation of apoC-III mRNA through selective binding to apoC-III mRNA, which results in a reduction of serum apoC-III protein. Studies suggest that apoC-III regulates both triglyceride metabolism and hepatic clearance of chylomicrons and other triglyceride-rich lipoproteins.
Active moieties	Olezarsen
QT prolongation	At a dose 1.5 times the maximum recommended dose for Tryngolza, clinically significant QTc interval prolongation was not observed.
General Information
Bioanalysis	Plasma and urine concentrations of olezarsen were determined by validated hybridization electrochemiluminescence (ECL) and partially validated enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA) methods. These methods assess the total concentration of full-length ASOs, including fully conjugated, partially conjugated (i.e., olezarsen with 1, 2, or 3 GalNAc sugar deletions), and unconjugated olezarsen (volanesorsen). The performance of bioanalytical methods and the results of quantifications were acceptable.
Healthy subjects versus patients	No apparent differences in the PK parameters were observed following multiple subcutaneous administrations of 80 mg olezarsen once every 4 weeks between patients with FCS and healthy volunteers.

Range of effective dose(s) or exposure	The 80 mg Q4W was shown to be effective in reducing TG levels in subjects with FCS in the Phase 3 Study ISIS 678354-CS3.
Maximally tolerated dose or exposure	A maximum tolerated dose was not determined. A supratherapeutic dose of 120 mg was studied as a single dose in Phase 1 Study ISIS-678354-CS1.
Dose proportionality	In Study ISIS 678354-CS1, olezarsen exposure (Cmax and AUC) increased in a dose-proportional manner at doses ranging from 10 to 120 mg (slope of 1.35 for Cmax and 1.27 for AUC using linear regression analyses of the log-transformed data).
Accumulation	No accumulation of olezarsen Cmax and AUC was observed in repeated dosing (once every 4 weeks). Accumulation in Ctrough was observed with once every 4 weeks administration in both healthy volunteers and patients with FCS. Based on the population PK model, the predicted accumulation in Ctrough is 2.3-fold.
Time to achieve steady state	Steady-state trough in patients with FCS appeared to be reached by Day 169 consistent with the apparent terminal elimination half-life of approximately 30 days in plasma
Bridge between to-be marketed and clinical trial formulations	The intended commercial drug product presentation of prefilled syringe with an autoinjector is supported by the BE study (Study ISIS 678354-CS20)
Absorption
Bioavailability	The absolute bioavailability after subcutaneous administration of olezarsen was not determined.
Tmax	Following subcutaneous administration, the Tmax for olezarsen is approximately 2 hours post dose, based on population estimates.
Distribution
Volume of distribution	The population estimates for the apparent central volume of distribution is 91.9 L and the apparent peripheral volume of distribution is 2960 L.
Plasma protein binding	Protein binding of olezarsen > 99%
Drug as substrate of transporters	Olezarsen was not a substrate for major drug transporters in vitro.
L188: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L189: Characteristic	Drug Information
Elimination
Mass balance results	A mass balance study was not conducted due to the risk of prolonged residence time in liver. Urinary excretion of total full-length olezarsen within the first 24 hours represented a small fraction of the administered dose (< 1% of administered dose) in healthy volunteers. The low renal clearance over the first 24 hours supports the hypothesis that the initial plasma clearance is the result of rapid tissue uptake, not excretion. The ultimate elimination of unconjugated olezarsen is likely a combination of slow nuclease metabolism within tissues and subsequent excretion of shortened metabolites (as well as any remaining full-length oligonucleotide) into urine.
Clearance	Population PK model estimated apparent clearance was 13.5 L/h.
Half-life	The terminal elimination half-life is approximately 30 days.
Metabolic pathway(s)	The ASO-moiety of olezarsen (volanesorsen) is metabolized by endo- and exonucleases in tissues to shorter oligonucleotide metabolites. Oligonucleotides like olezarsen are not substrates for the classic CYP metabolic pathways.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight	While there was a clear trend of declining exposure with increasing body weight for Cmax,ss and AUCtau,ss, no such apparent trend was identified for Ctrough,ss. Considering steady-state apoC-III and TG reductions (percent change-from-baseline) were similar across different body weight groups, the impact of body weight on olezarsen PK was not considered clinically meaningful, and a fixed dose of 80 mg monthly is appropriate, regardless of body weight.
Age	Age (adults aged < 65 years [N = 223], 65 to 74 years [N = 41], and ≥ 75 years [N = 8]) was not identified as a statistically significant covariate in the population PK analysis.
Renal impairment	No dosage adjustment is needed for patients with mild or moderate impairment of renal function. Olezarsen was not studied in patients with severe renal impairment.
Hepatic impairment	No dosage adjustment is needed for patients with mild hepatic impairment. Olezarsen was not studied in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment.
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of metabolism	Olezarsen did not show any direct or time-dependent inhibition of any of the major CYP enzymes (CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1, and CYP3A4) in vitro at clinically relevant concentrations. Olezarsen did not induce CYP1A2, 2B6 or 3A4 at clinically relevant concentrations.
Inhibition/induction of transporter systems	Olezarsen was not a substrate or an inhibitor of OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BSEP, BCRP, or P-gp.
Immunogenicity
Bioanalysis	The ADA assay's ability to detect anti-olezarsen antibodies in the presence of circulating olezarsen (drug tolerance) was determined to be 39.1 ng/mL of antibody in the presence of up to 4000 ng/mL of olezarsen which is found to be acceptable. OPQR reviewed the Applicant's ADA assay and confirmed that it is suitable to detect ADAs against olezarsen.
Incidence	With duration of treatment up to 53 weeks, treatment-emergent ADAs against olezarsen were detected in 38.1% (8/21) of patients in the 50 mg olezarsen group, 45.5% (10/22) of patients in the 80 mg olezarsen group in Study ISIS 678354-CS3. ADAs to olezarsen were not characterized for neutralizing activity.
L190: Characteristic	Drug Information
Clinical impact	The presence of ADAs did not affect olezarsen plasma Cmax but increased AUC and Ctrough. Although anti-drug antibody development was not found to affect the pharmacodynamics, safety, or efficacy of Tryngolza in these patients, the available data are limited to make definitive conclusions.

L193: ## 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L194: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L195: ## 6.1.1. Proposed Olezarsen Dosage Regimen
L196: The Applicant's proposed dosage of Tryngolza for the proposed indication is 80 mg administered by SC injection once monthly (Q1M).
L197: ## Missed Dose
L198: Administer Tryngolza as soon as possible after a missed dose. Resume dosing at monthly intervals from the date of the most recently administered dose.
L199: ## 6.1.2. Dose Selection Rationale for Clinical Trials
L200: The 50 mg and 80 mg doses of olezarsen Q4W were selected for both the pivotal Trial ISIS 678354-CS3 and the confirmatory efficacy Trial ISIS 678354-CS8 based on the pre-clinical data and the pharmacokinetic (PK), pharmacodynamic (PD), and safety analyses from the Phase 1 study in healthy volunteers with HTG (ISIS 678354-CS1) and the results of the Phase 2 study in patients with atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD) and HTG (ISIS 678354-CS2).
L201: A pooled analysis of safety for the Phase 1 and Phase 2 clinical studies did not demonstrate any clear difference in safety or tolerability between the different doses tested (Phase 1: 10 to 120 mg single doses, 15 or 30 mg per week for 6 weeks, and 60 mg Q4W for 13 weeks; Phase 2: 10 mg or 50 mg Q4W, 15 mg Q2W, and 10 mg Q1W, all for at least 6 months and up to 1 year).
L202: Considering that patients with FCS have extremely high TG levels (often &gt;2000 mg/dL), and that the targeted TG range for these patients was &lt;880 mg/dL, a maximal TG reduction over 70% was considered optimal for olezarsen to prevent the risk of AP. Population PK/PD model-based simulations for a population of patients with FCS predicted a 70% reduction in TG levels and an 86% reduction in ApoC-III levels with a dosing regimen of 80 mg Q4W, which was selected as the highest dose tested in this study. The 50 mg Q4W dose of olezarsen was expected
L203: to produce a 64% TG lowering and a 79% ApoC-III lowering from baseline and was included in the study to evaluate dose-response.
L204: Patients with FCS require chronic treatment, and therefore a Q4W dosing regimen may provide advantages over weekly dosing in terms of convenience and compliance for patients.
L205: ## 6.1.3. Exposure-Response Relationship for Efficacy and Safety
L206: ## Evaluation of the Proposed Dosage Regimen
L207: The dosing regimen is supported by the favorable benefit-risk profiles in patients with FCS (Trial ISIS 678354-CS3) and efficacy and safety profiles in patients with HTG and ASCVD/high-risk for ASCVD or with sHTG (Trial ISIS 678354-CS8, see Section 6.2). In ISIS 678354-CS3, the olezarsen 80 mg group had a greater placebo-adjusted mean percent decrease in fasting TG from baseline compared with the 50 mg group at Month 6 (44.5% for the 80 mg group vs. 21.5% for the 50 mg group) and at Month 12 (61.4% vs. 43.2%, see Section 6.2.2.6 for details). For the primary endpoint of mean percent decrease in fasting TG levels from baseline at Month 6, the decrease for the 80 mg group compared with the placebo group was statistically significant (p=0.0015), but the same comparison for the 50 mg group and the placebo group did not reach statistical significance (p=0.1153). Consistent with the mechanism of action of olezarsen, the 80 mg group had a greater placebo-adjusted mean percent decrease in ApoC-III levels from baseline compared with the olezarsen 50 mg group at Month 6 (73.69% vs. 65.48%), and at Month 12 (81.28% vs. 77.06%). Consistent with the results observed in ISIS 678354-CS3, a greater mean percent decrease from baseline in TG levels and ApoC-III levels in the 80 mg group compared with the 50 mg group was also observed in ISIS 678354-CS8. The adjudicated AP event rates were the same for the 80 mg group (one [4.5%] patient experienced one event) and 50 mg group (one [4.8%] patient experienced one event).
L208: In addition, population PK and exposure-response modeling and simulation were conducted to support the proposed dosing regimen. According to the exposure-ApoC-III and exposure-TG modeling and simulation, 50 mg and 80 mg olezarsen administered Q4W are estimated to achieve, at steady-state exposure and the end of the dosing interval, a median percent reduction from baseline in ApoC-III of 64.7% versus 73.5% (for 50 mg versus 80 mg), and a median percent reduction from baseline in TG of 23.4 versus 29.4%, in patients with FCS. Figure 2 shows a trend towards a marginally higher percentage change from baseline (CFB) in ApoC-III and TG for the 80 mg compared to the 50 mg doses, with overlapping 95% CIs for the percent CFB in TG concentrations.
L209: **Figure 2. Predicted Olezarsen, ApoC-III, and TG Concentration-Time Profiles for 50 mg and 80 mg Q4W**

L213: The relationship between olezarsen exposure metrics and the maximum reduction in platelet count (from merged studies ISIS 678354-CS3 and -CS8) is relatively shallow, with most patients experiencing less than grade 1 thrombocytopenia, and most patients with highest maximum decrease in platelets counts having no ADA or maximum ADA titer lower or equal to 800 (Figure 65).
L214: ## Dosing Regimen Comparison of Once Monthly and Once Every 4 Weeks
L215: To bridge from the Q4W dosing regimen used in the clinical program to the once-monthly regimen proposed for commercialization, simulations were performed using the final population PK/PD model and applying the covariate distribution from Study ISIS 678354-CS3 in patients with FCS. The simulated olezarsen PK and PD profiles following the administration of olezarsen 80 mg Q4W for 13 doses or once monthly for 12 doses overlapped on Day 1 and at steady-state with only marginal difference in median (and 80% prediction interval) olezarsen concentrations and ApoC-III or TG concentrations (Figure 3 and Figure 31).
L216: The comparison of Q4W with a once-monthly dosing regimen showed no meaningful differences in the simulated PK exposure (C_{max,ss}, C_{trough,ss}, and AUC_{tau,ss}) (Figure 4) and ApoC-III or TG profiles when compared for different dose levels (30, 50, 80, and 120 mg) (Figure 5).
L217: Overall, there do not appear to be any clinically relevant differences in PK, ApoC-III, or TG between the dosing regimen of Q4W and Q1M.

L229: ## **Bridging Between Phase 3 and To-Be-Marketed Drug Product Presentations**
L230: The bioequivalence (BE) Trial ISIS 678354-CS20, in conjunction with the exposure-response analyses for efficacy and safety, support that intended commercial 80 mg olezarsen administered
L231: as a 100 mg/mL formulation via autoinjector was therapeutically equivalent to 80 mg olezarsen administered as a 100 mg/mL formulation via vial with syringe, as used in the clinical program.

L234: Source: Reviewer's Analysis
L235: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; CI, confidence interval; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; Geo, geometric; PK, pharmacokinetic
L236: The primary endpoint of BE between the autoinjector and the vial presentations based on AUC0-336h was achieved but the geometric mean ratio 90% CI for Cmax was slightly above the standard upper CI limit for BE (80.00% to 125.00%), by 4.42% (Table 6). However, this small difference is not clinically relevant based on the following considerations: (1) the short duration around Cmax, during which plasma concentration versus time profiles showed a small difference, and the rest of the concentration-time profiles were nearly superimposable; (2) the percent changes in ApoC-III and TG levels from baseline on any of the sampling days were similar between the two treatments (autoinjector and vial). These data demonstrate that the small difference in Cmax at the 80 mg dose level between the two treatments had no clinically meaningful effect on PD; and (3) there was no clinical meaningful difference in safety profiles and ISRs between the two presentations for olezarsen. Of note, as detailed in Section 17, the autoinjector was introduced into the open-label clinical trials, CS7 and CS13, and the exposure to the autoinjector in these trials was small.
L237: OSIS has determined that an inspection is not needed for bioanalytical site because they conducted a Remote Regulatory Assessment for this site in (b)(4) under a different NDA and concluded that data from the reviewed studies were reliable.
L238: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To Demonstrate Efficacy
L239: ## 6.2.1. Integrated Analysis, Efficacy
L240: The review team concluded that an integrated analysis of efficacy was not informative given the FCS population investigated in Trial CS3 and the sHTG and moderate HTG population with increased risk/or established CVD investigated in Trial CS8 were too different in terms of baseline levels of TG and risk of pancreatitis to make comparative analyses.
L241: ## 6.2.2. Trial ISIS 678354-CS3
L242: ## 6.2.2.1. Design, Trial CS3
L243: The pivotal Trial ISIS 678354-CS3 (Trial CS3) was a randomized, double blind, placebo-controlled trial in patients with FCS with and without a prior history of pancreatitis. The primary objective of this trial was to demonstrate the efficacy of olezarsen (two different doses) on the percent change in fasting TG over a 53-week period compared to placebo. Subjects were randomized in a 1:1 ratio to either cohort A (50 mg) or Cohort B (80 mg). Each cohort was further randomized 2:1 to receive olezarsen or placebo. Randomization was stratified by prior history of pancreatitis (within 10 years prior to screening) and previous treatment with volanesorsen. The study design is shown below (Figure 6).
L244: Figure 6. Study Design, Trial CS3
L245: Abbreviations: ET = Early Termination; PD = pharmacodynamic; PK = pharmacokinetic; SC = subcutaneous.
L246: Source: Complete Study Report, Trial CS3.
L247: The primary efficacy endpoint was change in fasting TG from baseline to Month 6 (defined as the average of Weeks 23, 25, and 27). The primary endpoint family included analysis of the percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 6 first for the olezarsen 80 mg group, and, if the comparison with placebo was statistically significant for the olezarsen 80 mg group, then
L248: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L249: superiority over placebo of the percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 6 for the olezarsen 50 mg group was to be determined.
L250: The effect on AP events was also evaluated in this trial. Out of a total of 28 secondary endpoints, a comparison of adjudicated AP between the olezarsen treatment arms and placebo was listed in endpoints #13-16, and #25-26 (see below).
L251: Other FCS-related symptoms were assessed as exploratory endpoints.

L263: ## 6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial CS3
L264: ## Main Inclusion Criteria
L265: - A diagnosis of FCS (type 1 hyperlipoproteinemia) by documentation of confirmed homozygote, compound heterozygote, or double heterozygote for loss-of-function mutations in type 1-causing genes, such as LPL, glycosylphosphatidylinositol-anchored high-density lipoprotein-binding protein 1 (GPIHBP1), apolipoprotein A5 (ApoA5), apolipoprotein C-II (ApoC2), glycerol-3 phosphate dehydrogenase 1 (GPD1), or lipase maturation factor 1 (LMF1).
- Fasting TG \ge880 mg/dL at screening. If the fasting TG was &lt;880 mg/dL, up to two additional tests may have been performed, with any single test used to qualify
- Age \ge18 years at the time of informed consent
- History of pancreatitis (defined as a recorded diagnosis of AP or hospitalization or ER visit for severe abdominal pain consistent with AP and for which no alternate diagnosis was made) within 10 years prior to screening. Patients without a recorded history of pancreatitis,
L266: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L267: or no recorded history within 10 years prior to screening, were also eligible but their enrollment was capped at 35% (i.e., \le 21) of the 60 planned patients.
L268: - Willing to follow a diet comprising \le 20 g of fat per day during the study
- The following concomitant medications were allowed if on a stable dose for at least 4 weeks prior to screening:
L269: - Statins, omega-3 fatty acids, prescription and over-the-counter (OTC), fibrates, or other lipid-lowering medications
- Antidiabetic medications
- Antihypertensive medications
- Oral anticoagulants
- Tamoxifen, estrogens or progestins
L270: ## **Main Exclusion Criteria**
L271: - Clinically significant abnormalities in medical history (e.g., previous acute coronary syndrome within 6 months of screening, major surgery within 3 months of screening) or physical examination
- AP within 4 weeks prior to screening
- Screening laboratory results as follows, or any other clinically significant abnormalities in screening laboratory values that would have rendered a patient unsuitable for inclusion:Platelet count &lt;100,000/\text{mm}^3 at Screening or Qualification. If the platelet count was &lt;100,000/\text{mm}^3, up to two additional tests may have been performed to qualify at both screening and qualification
- Alanine aminotransferase (ALT) or aspartate aminotransferase (AST) &gt;3.0 \times upper limit of normal (ULN)
- Total bilirubin &gt; ULN unless due to Gilbert's syndrome
- Estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) &lt;45 mL/min/1.73 \text{ m}^2 (as determined by the Chronic Kidney Disease Epidemiology formula for creatinine clearance)
- Urine protein/creatinine ratio (UPCR) \ge 500 mg/g, or urine albumin/creatinine (UACR) ratio \ge 300 mg/g
- Uncontrolled arterial hypertension (blood pressure [BP] &gt;160/100 mm Hg)
- History of bleeding diathesis or coagulopathy or clinically significant abnormality in coagulation parameters at screening
- Malignancy within 5 years, except for basal or squamous cell carcinoma of the skin or carcinoma in situ of the cervix that had been successfully treated
- Active infection with HIV, hepatitis C or hepatitis B diagnosed by initial serological testing and confirmed with RNA testing, or prior treatment for hepatitis C.
- Treatment with another investigational drug (non-oligonucleotide), biological agent, or device within 1 month of screening, or five half-lives of investigational agent, whichever was longer
L272: The above eligibility criteria are acceptable.
L273: ## 6.2.2.3. Patient Screening and Disposition, Trial CS3
L274: A total of 144 subjects were screened for the trial. Of those screened, approximately 54% failed screening due mostly to failure to meet randomization criteria (69), other (6), physician decision (2), and withdrawal by patient (1). The reason “Other” for screen failure was further investigated (Listing 16.2.1.2). In most cases (5 out of 6) it was due to subject not able to meet according to visit schedule.

L277: Source: Clinical Analyst; ds.xpt and Clinical Study Report; Software: R
L278: A total of 66 subjects were randomized to receive olezarsen (olezarsen 80 mg, n=22; olezarsen 50 mg, n=21) or placebo (n=23). The number of patients in each treatment group with prior history of pancreatitis within 10 years of Screening and with previous treatment with volanesorsen were similar, due to stratified randomization.
L279: Approximately 88% or 38/43 subjects in the pooled olezarsen treatment group completed the study. Table 8 below summarizes the disposition of subjects by treatment arm.
L280: Overall, five subjects (5/43 or 11.6%) discontinued pooled olezarsen treatment (three subjects in the olezarsen 80 mg group, two subjects in the olezarsen 50 mg group) compared to one subject in the placebo group.
L281: Overall, three subjects (3/43 or 7.0%) in the pooled olezarsen group discontinued study drug due to an adverse event (AE); no patients in the placebo group discontinued treatment due to an AE. One of the discontinuations due to an AE was a sudden death in the olezarsen 50 mg group which is further examined in the safety review.
L282: Across the treatment groups, the percentage of subjects that completed the trial was balanced and allowed for a meaningful assessment of efficacy and safety.

L304: ## 6.2.2.5. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial CS3
L305: ## Analysis Population
L306: The FAS was defined as all patients who were randomly assigned to treatment and received any amount of olezarsen or placebo. The primary efficacy analysis was performed using FAS. The safety set was also defined as all patients who were randomized to treatment and received any amount of olezarsen or placebo. The safety analysis was performed using the safety set.
L307: ## Primary Efficacy Analysis
L308: The primary analysis time point was at Month 6. The value of the primary endpoint was defined as the average of Week 23, 25, and 27 fasting assessments. The Applicant's prespecified primary analysis of the primary endpoint, the mean percent change in fasting TG from baseline to the primary analysis time point, was performed using an analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) model with missing values imputed using the retrieved dropout method (patients who discontinue treatment before Week 23 but still have TG assessments at Weeks 23, 25, or 27). The model included treatment group (olezarsen 80 mg, olezarsen 50 mg, pooled placebo) and the two randomization stratification factors (prior history of pancreatitis within 10 years prior to screening (yes vs. no) and previous treatment with volanesorsen (yes vs. no) as the fixed effects and natural log-transformed baseline fasting TG as a covariate. To account for potential heterogeneity in variance, the 95% CIs were calculated using the robust variance estimator based on the Bell and McCaffrey method. The Applicant stated that any intercurrent events occurring
L309: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L310: before Week 23 (e.g., treatment discontinuation, use of additional medication, changes in background or concomitant treatments) were implemented using the treatment policy strategy where the patients' assessments post intercurrent events were collected and used in the analysis. The Applicant conducted a post hoc test for normality based on the observed data using a Shapiro-Wilk Normality Test. The FDA results shown are different from the Applicant's results, but the conclusions concur.
L311: ## **Missing Data**
L312: There was about 7.5% missing data at Month 6 (one patient in olezarsen 50 mg, three patients in olezarsen 80 mg, and one patient in the placebo group). There was not a sufficient number of retrieved dropouts, thus the Applicant used a washout multiple imputation approach (Jump to Reference, J2R) to impute missing TG data. The Applicant stated that patients treated with olezarsen who had missing TG values after the last dosing date multiply imputed based on baseline fasting TG, the two stratification factors of their own treatment group, and the imputation model for the placebo group. Patients in the placebo group had missing TG values multiply imputed based on baseline fasting TG, fasting TG values at postbaseline visits and the two stratification factors from the placebo group.
L313: There was some concern over how the missing data were imputed. We were unable to replicate the results. An information request was sent to the Applicant seeking clarification on whether their imputation procedure had taken into account all uncertainties of the estimated imputation model based on placebo data. It seems that the Applicant selected the first 100 imputed datasets with the imputed values falling into feasible range of the imputed endpoints among 500 imputations. This procedure may underestimate the uncertainty of the imputed values. In response to the information request, the Applicant performed alternative imputation by directly restricting the imputed values to be within the feasible range instead of discarding out of range imputations. FDA conducted a washout imputation in the primary analysis to impute missing data without truncating any imputed values. FDA results are shown in Section 6.2.2.6. The results are different from the Applicants, but the conclusion remained the same. FDA's analyses used only placebo completer data for the placebo-washout imputation whereas the Applicant's imputation used all observed placebo data, which also contributed to the difference in results.
L314: The Applicant conducted some sensitivity analyses in the FAS population to evaluate the impact of missing data on the analysis conclusion for the primary endpoint. A pattern mixture model with ANCOVA was conducted, as well as a nonparametric analysis with Wilcoxon rank-sum test, a two-dimensional tipping-point analysis, and an ANCOVA model using log-transformed data. The results of the nonparametric analysis with Wilcoxon rank-sum will be shown in this review.
L315: ## **Secondary Endpoints**
L316: There are many secondary endpoints (see Section 6.2.2.1 and Section III). This review will present results of
L317: - Percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 12
- Percent change in fasting ApoC-III from baseline at Month 6 and Month 12
- Percent change in fasting ApoB-48 from baseline at Month 6 and Month 12
- Percent change in fasting non-HDL-C from baseline at Month 6 and Month 12
- Adjudicated AP event rate.
L318: - Adjudicated AP event rate in patients with a prior history of pancreatitis within 10 years prior to screening
- Adjudicated AP event rate between pooled olezarsen and pooled placebo in the subset of patients with two or more adjudicated AP events in 5 years prior to enrollment (FAS).
L319: There are some endpoints of interest to the FDA that were not included in the main clinical study report by the Applicant that are also shown in this review:
L320: - Percent change in baseline in LDL-C ultracentrifugation (mg/dL)
- Percent change in baseline in fasting total ApoB (mg/dL)
- Percent change in baseline in fasting high density lipoprotein cholesterol (mg/dL).
L321: *Note: Month 12 value was defined as the average of Weeks 51 and 53 values of an endpoint.*
L322: The continuous secondary endpoints were analyzed similarly to the primary endpoint with the same missing data imputation implemented. FDA conducted a washout imputation to impute missing data without truncating any imputed values.
L323: The pancreatitis endpoints were analyzed using a negative binomial regression model with treatment group and previous treatment with volanesorsen (yes/no) as the factors, and number of adjudicated AP events in 5 years prior to the enrollment, i.e., events occurred within 1,826 days (calculated by 365.25 times 5 and rounded to integer) before the first dose date and up to the first dose date and time, as a covariate. The logarithm of time in year that each patient was observed from Week 1 to 53 was used as an offset variable.
L324: ## **Multiplicity**
L325: A step-down procedure to account for multiplicity across treatment comparisons and key endpoints was used. For the primary endpoint family, the two treatment arms (olezarsen 80 mg and olezarsen 50 mg) were compared against pooled placebo using the hierarchical testing procedure, in which the olezarsen 80 mg treatment group was first compared to the pooled placebo at the 2-sided alpha level of 0.05. If the comparison was statistically significant (p&lt;0.05), then the olezarsen 50 mg treatment group was compared against the pooled placebo at the alpha level of 0.05. If the comparison of the olezarsen 80 mg treatment group was not
L326: statistically significant, then the comparison of the olezarsen 50 mg treatment group against pooled placebo and all secondary endpoints were considered exploratory.
L327: Testing of the secondary endpoint family was performed only if the comparison of the primary endpoint was statistically significant (p&lt;0.05) for both the olezarsen 80 mg and 50 mg treatment groups. If the comparison of the first secondary endpoint was statistically significant (p&lt;0.05), then the second secondary endpoint was tested. However, if the comparison of the first secondary endpoint was not statistically significant, then all endpoints of lower rank were considered exploratory. This process was repeated with each subsequent secondary endpoint.
L328: ## 6.2.2.6. Results of Analyses, Trial CS3
L329: The primary analysis results for the mean percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 6 are shown in Table 11. A statistically significantly greater mean percent decrease in fasting TG levels from baseline at Month 6 was observed in the olezarsen 80 mg group compared with the placebo group; the placebo-subtracted treatment difference was -44.5%, p=0.0015. Statistical significance was not achieved for the olezarsen 50 mg group compared with the pooled placebo group; the placebo-subtracted treatment difference was -21.5%, p=0.1153.
