L427: ## **Acute Pancreatitis**
L428: In Study CS3, an independent blinded committee prospectively adjudicated all AEs suggestive of AP for which the subject visited the emergency room or was hospitalized. Of note, the committee also adjudicated retrospective AP events which occurred within 5 years of randomization using the same definitions; see Section **III** for more discussion of retrospective AP adjudication.
L429: To prospectively adjudicated AP, the committee used the revised Atlanta Diagnostic Criteria (Banks et al. 2013) which required two of the following for the diagnosis of documented AP:
L430: - Abdominal pain consistent with AP
- Serum amylase or lipase levels that were at least 3x ULN
- Computed tomography (CT)/magnetic resonance imaging findings consistent with AP
L431: However, since clinicians do not always obtain a complete workup for AP, and to ensure that AP events were not missed, the committee defined additional categories for the diagnosis of AP:
L432: - Probable pancreatitis- required all of the following: abdominal pain consistent with AP; typical supportive treatment for pancreatitis (such as the patient being NPO, receiving intravenous [IV] fluids, and on analgesics); amylase and lipase had to be elevated but did not have to be above 3x ULN; radiology investigations were not performed or not diagnostic; discharge diagnosis was AP with no alternative diagnosis.
- Possible pancreatitis- required all of the following: a previous medical diagnosis of AP; abdominal pain strongly consistent with AP; use of typical supportive measures for AP; serum amylase/lipase were normal or not determined or missing; radiology investigations were not performed or not diagnostic; discharge diagnosis was AP with no alternative diagnosis.
- Unable to adjudicate- insufficient source documentation.
- No diagnosis of AP- sufficient evidence provided to conclude there was no diagnosis of AP.
L433: For data analysis, events that were decided as documented, probable, or possible were considered to be positively adjudicated AP.
L434: The adjudication workflow for AP was such that a triggering event was randomly assigned to two adjudicators to be adjudicated independently; if there was consensus between the reviewers, the decision was complete. If there was disagreement, or if additional information was required, the event was assigned to a meeting for final adjudication. At the meeting of three reviewers, the chair would render the final decision.
L435: In Study CS3, there were 31 prospective events adjudicated by the committee. Of the 31 events, 13 were determined to be confirmed AP events. Of the 13 events, 2 occurred in the olezarsen treatment arms (one each in the olezarsen 80 mg group and 50 mg group) and 11 events occurred in the placebo arm; the mean rate ratio (pooled olezarsen groups vs. placebo) was 0.12 (95% CI, 0.02 to 0.66, nominal p-value 0.0144) (Table 20). All subjects who had a positively confirmed AP event had a history of two or more previous episodes before the trial.

L441: The Applicant prespecified a number of secondary efficacy analyses related to adjudicated AP. All the endpoints were of nominal significance. Note that the treatment period was “Week 1 to Week 53”. The Applicant created a second interval “Week 13-Week 53” to reflect the near steady-state (at approximately three half-lives) of olezarsen. The “Week 13-Week 53” interval is considered exploratory by the FDA.

There were two events that occurred in the pooled olezarsen group and 11 events in the pooled placebo group from Week 1 to 53. There was a 90% decrease in the AP event rate for the pooled olezarsen group compared to the pooled placebo group from Week 1 to 53, the estimated reference range (RR) was 0.10 with 95% CI (0.02, 0.51).

L492: ## 6.2.3. Trial ISIS-678354-CS8
L493: ## 6.2.3.1. Design, Trial CS8
L494: The confirmatory phase 2 Trial ISIS 678354-CS8 (Trial CS8) was a randomized, double blind, placebo-controlled trial in patients with sHTG and moderate HTG with increased risk for or established CVD. The primary objective of this trial was to demonstrate the efficacy of olezarsen (two different doses) on the percent change in fasting TG over a 53-week period compared to placebo.
L495: Figure 10. Study Design, Trial CS8
L496: Source: Complete Study Report, Trial CS8
L497: Abbreviations: PD, pharmacodynamic; PK, pharmacokinetic; SC, subcutaneous
L498: The primary efficacy endpoint was the change from baseline to Month 6 (defined as the average of Weeks 25 and 27). The primary endpoint family included analysis of the percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 6 first for the olezarsen 80 mg group, and, if the comparison with placebo was statistically significant for the olezarsen 80 mg group, then analysis of the percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 6 for the olezarsen 50 mg group was to be determined.
L499: The first 10 secondary endpoints are listed in order of hierarchy by priority of analysis. Refer to the Section **III** for the complete list.
L500: - Secondary endpoint 1: Comparison of percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 12 between olezarsen 80 mg and pooled placebo in FAS
- Secondary endpoint 2: Comparison of percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 12 between olezarsen 50 mg and pooled placebo in FAS
- Secondary endpoint 3: Comparison of percent change in fasting ApoC-III from baseline at Month 6 between olezarsen 80 mg and pooled placebo in FAS
- Secondary endpoint 4: Comparison of percent change in fasting ApoC-III from baseline at Month 12 between olezarsen 80 mg and pooled placebo in FAS
- Secondary endpoint 5: Comparison of percent change in fasting ApoC-III from baseline at Month 6 between olezarsen 50 mg and pooled placebo in FAS
- Secondary endpoint 6: Comparison of percent change in fasting ApoC-III from baseline at Month 12 between olezarsen 50 mg and pooled placebo in FAS
- Secondary endpoint 7: Comparison of proportion of patients who achieve fasting TG &lt;150 mg/dL at Month 6 between olezarsen 80 mg and pooled placebo in the subgroup of FAS with baseline fasting TG &lt;500 mg/dL
- Secondary endpoint 8: Comparison of proportion of patients who achieve fasting TG &lt;150 mg/dL at Month 12 between olezarsen 80 mg and pooled placebo in the subgroup of FAS with baseline fasting TG &lt;500 mg/dL
- Secondary endpoint 9: Comparison of percent change in fasting very low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (VLDL-C) from baseline at Month 6 between olezarsen 80 mg and pooled placebo in FAS
- Secondary endpoint 10: Comparison of percent change in fasting VLDL-C from baseline at Month 12 between olezarsen 80 mg and pooled placebo in FAS
L501: Comparison of adjudicated AP event rate between pooled olezarsen treatment group and placebo in FAS was listed as secondary endpoint #47. Acute pancreatitis was adjudicated by a blinded independent committee according to the Atlanta Classification and as outlined in the Pancreatitis Charter. Additional exploratory/safety endpoint for MACE was also explored.
L502: ## 6.2.3.2. Eligibility Criteria, Study Trial CS8
L503: ## Main Inclusion Criteria
L504: - Aged \ge 18 years at the time of informed consent
- Was included in at least one of the following groups (a or b):Hypertriglyceridemia with fasting TG \ge 150 mg/dL and &lt;500 mg/dL with eitherClinical diagnosis of ASCVD defined as documented coronary artery disease, cerebrovascular disease, or peripheral artery disease
- At increased risk of developing ASCVD defined as the following:Type 2 diabetes mellitus,
- Two (2) or more of the following cardiovascular (CV) risk-enhancing factorsMales \ge 55 years of age or females \ge 65 years of age
- Females with a history of preeclampsia, or premature menopause
- Family history of premature ASCVD
- Current tobacco use (5 cigarettes/day or more for at least 1 year)
- Metabolic syndrome
- Hypertension requiring antihypertensive treatment
- Chronic kidney disease stage 3 (eGFR 30-59 mL/min/m^2)
- LDL-C \ge 160 mg/dL
- LDL-C \ge 100 mg/dL on therapy to lower LDL-C
- Elevated high-sensitivity C-reactive protein (hsCRP) (\ge 2.0 mg/L)
- Elevated lipoprotein (\ge 50 mg/dL)
- Ankle-brachial index &lt;0.9 in asymptomatic patients
- Coronary artery calcium score \ge 100
Severe HTG with fasting TG \ge 500 mg/dL

- Patients were to be on standard of care lipid-lowering medications per local guidelines. Lipid-lowering medications were to be optimized and stabilized for at least 4 weeks prior to screening to minimize changes in these medications during the study.
- Patients were to be willing to comply with diet and lifestyle recommendations
- Satisfied the following:Females could not be pregnant or lactating, and had to be either:

Surgically sterile (e.g., tubal occlusion, hysterectomy, bilateral salpingectomy, bilateral oophorectomy)
- Postmenopausal (defined as 12 months of spontaneous amenorrhea in females &gt;55 years of age or, in females \le 55 years, 12 months of spontaneous amenorrhea without an alternative medical cause and follicle-stimulating hormone levels in the postmenopausal range for the laboratory involved)
Abstinent*

If engaged in sexual relations of childbearing potential, had to agree to use a highly effective contraceptive method from the time of signing the informed consent form until at least 30 weeks after the last dose of study drug (olezarsen or placebo)
L505: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L506: Males had to be surgically sterile, abstinent*, or if engaged in sexual relations with a female of childbearing potential, were to utilize a highly effective contraceptive method from the time of signing the informed consent form until at least 30 weeks after the last dose of study drug (olezarsen or placebo)
L507: * *Abstinence was only acceptable as true abstinence (i.e., when this was in line with the preferred and usual lifestyle of the patient). Periodic abstinence (e.g., calendar, ovulation, symptothermal, post-ovulation methods), declaration of abstinence for the duration of a trial, and withdrawal were not acceptable methods of contraception.*
L508: ## **Key Exclusion Criteria**
L509: - Diabetes with any of the followingNewly diagnosed within 12 weeks of screening
- Glycosylated hemoglobin (HbA1c) \ge 9.5\% at screening
- Change in basal insulin regimen &gt;20\% within 3 months prior to screening
- For patients with type 1 diabetes: episode of diabetic ketoacidosis, or three or more episodes of severe hypoglycemia within 6 months prior to screening
- Acute coronary syndrome or stroke/transient ischemic attack within 6 months prior to screening
- Major surgery, peripheral revascularization, or non-urgent percutaneous coronary intervention within 3 months prior to screening, or upcoming planned major surgery or major procedure (e.g., arterial revascularization) during the course of the study
- Active pancreatitis within 4 weeks prior to screening
- Screening laboratory results as follows, or any other clinically significant abnormalities in screening laboratory values that would have rendered a patient unsuitable for inclusion:ALT or AST &gt;3.0 \times ULN
- Total bilirubin &gt;1.5 \times ULN unless due to Gilbert's syndrome
- eGFR &lt;30 mL/min/1.73 m^2 (as determined by the Chronic Kidney Disease Epidemiology formula for creatinine clearance;
- UPCR \ge 500 mg/g
- Uncontrolled arterial hypertension (BP &gt;180/100 mmHg) despite antihypertensive therapy
- Uncontrolled hypothyroidism, such as thyroid-stimulating hormone &gt; 1.5 \times ULN and free thyroxine &lt; lower limit of normal, clinical evidence of hypothyroidism, or thyroid hormone therapy that had not been stable for \ge 4 weeks prior to screening
- Active infection with human immunodeficiency virus, hepatitis C or hepatitis B (e.g., HBsAg positive) diagnosed by initial serological testing and confirmed with RNA testing, or prior treatment for hepatitis C. Patients at screening who tested positive by serology (for example,
L510: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L511: positive for Hepatitis C antibody), but negative by RNA may have been allowed in consultation with the Sponsor Medical Monitor or designee

- Malignancy within 5 years of screening, except for non-melanoma skin cancers, cervical in situ carcinoma, breast ductal carcinoma in situ, or Stage 1 prostate carcinoma that had been successfully treated.
- Hypersensitivity to the active substance or to any of the excipients (olezarsen or placebo)
- Concomitant medication/procedure restrictions
L512: Systemic corticosteroids or anabolic steroids within 6 weeks prior to screening and during the study unless approved by the Sponsor Medical Monitor or designee

Use of bile acid resins such as colestipol, cholestyramine, or colesevelam within 4 weeks prior to screening or planned during the study

Plasma apheresis within 4 weeks prior to screening or planned during the study

Change in medications known to exacerbate HTG such as certain beta blockers, thiazides, isotretinoin, oral antidiabetic medications, tamoxifen, estrogens, or progestins within 4 weeks prior to screening

Change in atypical antipsychotic medications (e.g., olanzapine and clozapine) within 3 months prior to screening
L513: The eligibility criteria for Trial CS8 were acceptable.

L543: ## 6.2.3.5. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial CS8
L544: ## Analysis Population
L545: The FAS was defined as all patients who were randomly assigned to treatment and received any amount of olezarsen or placebo. The primary efficacy analysis was performed using the FAS. The safety set was also defined as all patients who were randomized to treatment and received any amount of olezarsen or placebo. The safety analysis was performed using the safety set.
L546: ## Primary Efficacy Analysis
L547: The primary analysis time point was at Month 6. The value of the primary endpoint was defined as the average of Weeks 25 and 27 fasting assessments. The Applicant's prespecified primary analysis of the primary endpoint, the mean percent change in fasting TG from baseline to the primary analysis time point, was performed using an ANCOVA model that assumes unequal variance with missing values imputed using the retrieved dropout method (patients who discontinue treatment before Week 25 but still have TG assessments at Weeks 25 or 27). For Month 12 the retrieved dropout method includes patients who discontinue treatment before Week 51 but still have TG assessments at Weeks 51 or 53. The model included treatment group (olezarsen 80 mg, olezarsen 50 mg, pooled placebo) as the fixed effect and natural log-transformed baseline fasting TG as a covariate.
L548: ## Missing Data
L549: There was about 7.1% missing data at Month 6 (five patients in olezarsen 50 mg, five patients in olezarsen 80 mg, and one patient in the placebo group). If there was not a sufficient number of retrieved dropouts, the missing TG data was imputed using a wash-out multiple imputation approach (J2R). The Applicant stated that patients treated with olezarsen who had missing TG values after the last "observed value" multiply imputed based on baseline fasting TG and the imputation model for the placebo group. Patients in the placebo group had missing TG values multiply imputed based on baseline fasting TG and fasting TG values at postbaseline visits from the placebo group.
L550: The Applicant selected the first 100 imputed datasets with the imputed values falling into feasible range of the imputed endpoints among 1000 imputations. This procedure may underestimate the uncertainty of the imputed values. FDA conducted a washout imputation in the primary analysis to impute missing data without truncating any imputed values. FDA results are shown in Section 6.2.3.6. The FDA results shown are different from the Applicant's results, but the outcomes concur.
L551: The Applicant conducted some sensitivity analyses in the FAS population to evaluate the impact of missing data on the analysis conclusion for the primary endpoint. The primary analysis was repeated using the completers set, which is all observed data (no missing data imputation). A nonparametric analysis with Wilcoxon rank-sum test, a 2-dimensional tipping-point analysis, on the FAS population, a pattern mixture models with ANCOVA was conducted, and an ANCOVA model using log-transformed data in the FAS population.
L552: ## **Secondary Endpoints**
L553: There are many secondary endpoints (see Section III). This review will show the secondary endpoints, percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 12, the percent change in fasting non-HDL-C, HDL-C, LDL-C, total Apo from baseline at Month 6 and 12 and adjudicated AP event rate during the treatment period. The Month 12 value was defined as the average of Weeks 51 and 53 values of the endpoint. The Applicant's prespecified continuous secondary endpoints were analyzed using an ANCOVA model that assumes unequal variance with missing values imputed using the retrieved dropout method. The model included treatment group (olezarsen 80 mg, olezarsen 50 mg, pooled placebo) as the fixed effect and natural log-transformed baseline fasting TG as a covariate for mean percent change in fasting TG from baseline to Month 12. For the percent change in fasting non-HDL-C, HDL-C, LDL-C, and total Apo from baseline to Month 6 and 12 were analyzed using an ANCOVA model that assumes unequal variance with missing values imputed using the retrieved dropout method. The model included treatment group (olezarsen 80 mg, olezarsen 50 mg, pooled placebo) baseline stratification factor (qualification fasting TG levels of &lt;500 mg/dL vs. ≥500 mg/dL) as the fixed effect and natural log-transformed baseline fasting associated endpoint as a covariate.
L554: Adjudicated AP event rate: The pancreatitis endpoint was analyzed using a negative binomial regression model or Poisson regression model with treatment group and natural log transformed baseline fasting TG as the factors, and number of adjudicated AP events in 5 years prior to the enrollment as a covariate. The logarithm of time in year that each patient was observed from Week 1 to 53 was used as an offset variable.
L555: ## **Multiplicity**
L556: A step-down procedure to account for multiplicity across treatment comparisons and key endpoints was used. All tests will be conducted at a two-sided alpha level of 0.05. For the primary endpoint family, the two treatment arms (olezarsen 80 mg and olezarsen 50 mg) were compared against pooled placebo using the hierarchical testing procedure, in which the olezarsen 80 mg treatment group was first compared to the pooled placebo at the 2-sided alpha level of 0.05. If the comparison was statistically significant (p&lt;0.05), then the olezarsen 50 mg treatment group was compared against the pooled placebo at the alpha level of 0.05. If the comparison of the olezarsen 80 mg treatment group was not statistically significant, then the comparison of the olezarsen 50 mg treatment group against pooled placebo and all secondary endpoints were considered exploratory.
L557: Testing of the secondary endpoint family was performed only if the comparison of the primary endpoint was statistically significant (p&lt;0.05) for both the olezarsen 80 mg and 50 mg treatment groups.
L558: If the comparison of the first secondary endpoint was statistically significant (p&lt;0.05), then the second secondary endpoint was tested. However, if the comparison of the first secondary endpoint was not statistically significant, then all endpoints of lower rank were considered exploratory. This process was repeated with each subsequent secondary endpoint.

L614: ## 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L615: ## Issue/Background
L616: The pivotal phase 3 trial in the FCS population, Trial CS3, assessed two primary co-endpoints, the mean percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 6 (average of Weeks 23, 25, and 27) for olezarsen 80 mg compared with placebo and the mean percent change in fasting TG from baseline at Month 6 for olezarsen 50 mg compared with placebo.
L617: The primary TG endpoint was statistically significant for olezarsen 80 mg, but not for the olezarsen 50 mg dose. TG as an efficacy endpoint has been the basis of approval for other TG-lowering drugs in patients with severe hypertriglyceridemia (TG \ge 500 \text{ mg/dL}). TG has also been historically accepted by the Division as a surrogate for the reduction in the risk of AP. In addition to TG reduction, other lipid parameters were reviewed to determine whether the overall benefit risk profile remained favorable.
L618: Trial CS3 also assessed key secondary endpoints of a reduction in prospectively adjudicated AP; it is the first outcome trial submitted to the Agency to measure adjudicated AP in an HTG population. The nominal finding of a reduction in the rate of AP is consistent with the pathophysiology that links elevated levels of TG with AP and the reduction of TG with a subsequent reduction in AP. However, the limitations of the trial are its small size and relatively short duration to determine AP outcomes.
L619: The olezarsen FCS program was based on one adequate and well-controlled trial supported by confirmatory evidence generated by Trial CS8, a phase 2 trial in severe and moderate HTG populations.
L620: ## 6.3.1. Efficacy of Triglyceride Reduction of 80 mg Olezarsen in Trial CS3 in the FCS Population
L621: ## Issue
L622: Does the olezarsen 80 mg dose show efficacy in reducing TG levels in subjects with FCS in Trial CS3?
L623: ## Background
L624: ## Familial Chylomicronemia Syndrome
L625: Familial chylomicronemia syndrome is a rare inherited disease of lipid metabolism that causes sHTG and hyperchylomicronemia and is estimated to affect 1 to 2 persons per 1,000,000 in the United States (Warden et al. 2020; Tripathi et al. 2021). FCS is caused by reduced or absent LPL activity which leads to a reduced ability to clear TG leading to their accumulation in plasma. Because patients with FCS lack LPL activity, they are dependent on less efficient hepatic pathways for TG clearance. Genetic testing can confirm the diagnosis of FCS; however, FCS is often diagnosed using clinical signs and symptoms due to barriers to obtain genetic testing (Moulin et al. 2018).
L626: The major complication of FCS is acute and recurrent episodes of pancreatitis. Approximately 85% of patients with FCS report at least one episode of AP, and 40% have multiple recurrences
L627: NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L628: during their lifetime (Davidson et al. 2018). In addition, patients with FCS frequently experience eruptive xanthomas, lipemia retinalis, nausea, vomiting, and recurrent abdominal pain (Baass et al. 2020).
L629: ## Apolipoprotein C-III as a Target for Drug Development in Hypertriglyceridemia
L630: ApoC-III is a small protein that inhibits the activity of LPL, the enzyme responsible for the breakdown and clearance of TG from the blood. ApoC-III also inhibits the uptake of TG-rich particles by the liver. Therefore, ApoC-III is a target for drug development aimed at lowering TG levels. Olezarsen is a GalNAc3-conjugated antisense oligonucleotide that reduces ApoC-III production by binding to *apoC-III* mRNA and inducing its degradation (Alexander et al. 2019).
L631: ## TG Reduction Used in Other Drug Approvals in Hypertriglyceridemic Populations
L632: TG has historically been accepted by the Division as the basis of approval for TG-lowering drugs in patients with sHTG (TG \ge500 mg/dL) and mixed dyslipidemia (TG \ge150-200 mg/dL). Moreover, elevated TG levels (&gt;1,000 mg/dL) increase the risk of pancreatitis. The mechanism for hypertriglyceridemic pancreatitis is postulated to involve hydrolysis of TG by pancreatic lipase and release of free fatty acids that induce free radical damage, inflammation, and damage to the pancreas. Another hypothesis postulates that hyperviscosity due to elevated levels of chylomicrons in pancreatic capillaries leads to ischemia and pancreatitis (Tsuang et al. 2009). Since elevated TG levels are hypothesized to cause pancreatitis by these mechanisms, TG reduction has been assumed to decrease the risk for AP, although there have been no previous outcomes trials for AP in HTG populations.
L633: ## Assessment
L634: In Trial CS3, TG reduction (primary endpoint) was assessed as the difference in mean percent change from baseline to Month 6 in fasting TG between olezarsen 80 mg and pooled placebo in subjects with FCS in the FAS.
L635: When deciding between using the median or the mean to describe changes in TG, the choice may depend on the distribution of the data, the clinical question of interest, and the consideration of influence of extreme large values. The mean is often the common choice as the difference in means estimate the average casual effect. The mean could be sensitive to extreme values (which may or may not be outliers). If there are outliers or if the distribution is highly skewed and the sample size is not large, inference on the mean relying on normal approximation might be suboptimal. If TG levels are highly variable with outliers, the median provides a better representation of the typical changes in TG. Previous studies indicate the typical distribution of TG is skewed. Recent TG lowering drugs (such as Vascepa and Epanova) and statins with TG lowering indications (Crestor, Lipitor) have used median percent TG changes as the primary endpoint as TG tends to have a skewed distribution (at least in non-FCS populations). The Applicant conducted a post hoc test for normality based on the observed data using a Shapiro-Wilk Normality Test. This test indicated that there was not enough evidence to conclude that the percent change in TG did not follow normal distribution. Thus, the Applicant used the difference in mean to describe the percent changes in TG.
L636: A sensitivity analysis using a non-parametric Wilcoxon rank sum test was conducted to explore the impact of distributional assumption on the analysis conclusion. The median percent change in TG from baseline to Month 6 endpoint was -33.6% for olezarsen 80 mg, and +2.8% for
L637: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L638: placebo. The median of the treatment difference between olezarsen 80mg and placebo was -40.8% (95% CI -65.5, -16.1).
L639: Another prespecified sensitivity analysis for the primary endpoint used a log transformation of TG data (Table 37). Testing was conducted to investigate whether the primary analysis was sensitive to violation of the normality assumption. These sensitivity analyses were consistently in favor of olezarsen 80 mg over placebo.

L643: The primary analysis time point (Month 6) was defined as the average of Weeks 23, 25, and 27.
L644: Missing data after last dosing date for patients in the olezarsen arms were imputed using washout MI (J2R) approach. Missing data before the last dosing date or for patients in the placebo group were imputed under the MAR assumption. For each imputed data set, the percent change from Baseline to the primary analysis time point in fasting TG between each olezarsen treatment group (olezarsen 80 mg, olezarsen 50 mg) vs. pooled placebo are compared using ANCOVA model with log(y/x) as dependent variable, where y was the value of TG at primary analysis time point, x was the baseline value of TG; and the two randomization stratification factors and treatment group as factors and log-transformed baseline TG as a covariate. The model provided an estimate of log postbaseline to baseline ratio for each treatment group as well as the ratios relative to the pooled placebo group and the corresponding 95% confidence intervals (CIs).
L645: Normality was tested based on observed data.
L646: Note 1: The estimates from the 100 fitted models were combined to provide an overall estimate with corresponding confidence intervals and p-value.
L647: Abbreviations: ANCOVA, analysis of covariance; CI, confidence interval; J2R, Jump to Reference; LSM, least squares mean; MI, multiple imputation; N, total number of subjects
L648: The mean percent change in TG from baseline to Month 6 endpoint was -33.3% for olezarsen 80 mg, and +11.2% for placebo. The mean of the treatment difference between olezarsen 80 mg and placebo was -44.50% (95% CI -72.0, -17.0, p=0.0015).
L649: Sensitivity analysis of the primary endpoint on the Per Protocol Set further confirmed the robustness of the results. Furthermore, the primary endpoint analysis was repeated in the FAS with no missing TG value at baseline and the primary analysis time point. This analysis also supported the conclusion from the primary analysis.
L650: Additionally, the secondary endpoint of percent change in TG from baseline to Month 12 between olezarsen 80 mg and pooled placebo in subjects in the FAS showed persistence of TG reduction at Month 12. At Month 12, the placebo-subtracted mean percent decrease in TG for olezarsen 80 mg was approximately -61.4% (95% CI -101.9% to -20.8%).
L651: ## **Conclusion**
L652: Yes, the olezarsen 80 mg dose demonstrated efficacy in reducing TG levels in subjects with FCS in Trial CS3.
L653: In the FCS population, who have severely elevated TG (typically &gt;1,000 mg/dL, sometimes exceeding 2,000-10,000 mg/dL), the goal is to reduce TG levels as much as possible to minimize the risk of AP. Although there is no universally defined "target" TG level for patients with FCS, the general goal is to keep TG below 500 mg/dL. However, because FCS patients tend to have persistently high TG due to genetic factors, achieving levels below 1,000 mg/dL is often considered a success in clinical practice.
L654: In Trial CS3, the placebo-subtracted mean percent change in TG (primary endpoint) showed a statistically significant mean TG reduction of -44%. In the olezarsen 80 mg group, the mean TG at baseline was 2613 mg/dL which decreased at Month 6 to a mean TG of 1732 mg/dL; the within treatment arm TG reduction with olezarsen 80 mg from baseline to Month 6 was approximately -33% (95% CI -49.03, -14.94). Therefore, olezarsen 80 mg dose showed both a statistically significant and clinically meaningful reduction in mean TG levels. Furthermore, this TG reduction is associated with a nominal reduction in the rate of AP (see Section 6.3.3).

L656: ## 6.3.2.
L657: (b) (4)
L658: ## 6.3.3. Acceptability of the Secondary Endpoint of Acute Pancreatitis Risk Reduction
L659: ## Issue
L660: Is the secondary endpoint of reduction in the rate of AP acceptable and can it be described in the olezarsen labeling?
L661: ## Background
L662: As stated previously, patients with FCS have a significantly elevated risk of AP due to extremely high TG levels. Thirty to 40% of FCS patients will experience at least one episode of AP, and many will have recurrent, severe episodes that are potentially life-threatening. Estimates of the lifetime incidence of pancreatitis in this population range from approximately 50 to 80% (Stroes et al. 2017). Acute pancreatitis can lead to complications such as pancreatic necrosis, infection, and multiorgan failure.
L663: ## Assessment
L664: Trial CS3 investigated whether treatment with two different doses of olezarsen (80 mg and 50 mg) affected the rate of AP as compared to placebo in several secondary endpoints.
L665: Because of the failure of efficacy (TG reduction) of olezarsen 50 mg dose (co-primary endpoint), all following endpoints, including AP rates, did not meet statistical significance but were considered to be of nominal significance. However, the reduction in the pancreatitis rate can be considered clinically significant. Note that the secondary endpoints summarized below regarding pancreatitis rate reduction considered the pooled treatment arms for both olezarsen 80 mg and olezarsen 50 mg.
L666: **Table 38. Summary of Key Acute Pancreatitis Secondary Efficacy Endpoints, Weeks 1 to 53, Trial CS3**

L670: Since the review team determined that the olezarsen 80 mg dose would be approved, it was decided (b) (4) the AP incidence for olezarsen 80 mg would be listed in the label. Specifically, the numerical incidence of AP in subjects treated with olezarsen 80 mg (n=1, 5%) was lower than placebo (n=7, 30%).
L671: ## Conclusion
L672: Yes, the secondary endpoint of reduction in the rate of AP is acceptable and clinically meaningful and therefore can be described in the olezarsen labeling.
L673: Because of the failure of efficacy in TG reduction of olezarsen 50 mg dose (co-primary endpoint), all following endpoints, including AP did not meet statistical significance but were considered to be of nominal significance. However, the reduction in the AP rate with olezarsen 80 mg can be considered clinically significant. (b) (4)
L674: , future trials in sHTG populations, which are ongoing with olezarsen, may be able to provide additional effectiveness data.
L675: ## **6.3.4. Adequacy of the Confirmatory Evidence in Trial CS8 in the Severe and Moderate Hypertriglyceridemic Populations**
L676: ## Issue
L677: Is Trial CS8, a phase 2 trial conducted in subjects with sHTG and mixed dyslipidemia, adequate to be used as confirmatory evidence for effectiveness of TG lowering in the FCS population?
L678: ## **Background**
L679: ## **Requirements for Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness**
L680: Substantial evidence of effectiveness, the regulatory requirement for approval, generally consists of evidence from at least two adequate and well-controlled trials. In certain circumstances, a single adequate and well-controlled trial demonstrating efficacy may be sufficient to generate SEE if there is adequate confirmatory evidence. Such circumstances are described in the FDA draft guidance for industry *Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness With One Adequate and Well-Controlled Clinical Investigation and Confirmatory Evidence* (September 2023).
L681: For example, clinical trial data from diseases with similarities in their underlying pathophysiology is appropriate for use as confirmatory evidence. Additionally, the degree of similarity between the efficacy endpoints in similar diseases can further support the use of these data as confirmatory evidence.
L682: Other criteria for allowing use of confirmatory evidence includes the persuasiveness of the single investigation; the robustness of the confirmatory evidence; the seriousness of the disease, particularly when there is an unmet medical need; the size of the patient population; and whether it is ethical and practicable to conduct more than one adequate and well-controlled clinical investigation.
L683: These criteria and considerations are all relevant to the olezarsen development program for FCS, a very rare and serious disease with an unmet need. Because the olezarsen FCS program has only one adequate and well-controlled trial, we sought to assess whether there is adequate confirmatory evidence to generate SEE with Trial CS8, a phase 2 trial in severe and moderate HTG populations.
L684: ## **Causes and Classification of Hypertriglyceridemia**
L685: Plasma TG levels represent the TG content within various lipoprotein particles, mainly chylomicrons and VLDL. A fasting TG level below 150 mg/dL is generally considered within the normal range. Non-fasting TG levels of 175 mg/dL fall around the 75th percentile of the normal range, while levels of approximately 400 mg/dL are in the 97th percentile. In the general population, plasma TG levels are skewed to the right with a long tail toward higher values, varying based on population diversity. Due to this skew, a logarithmic transformation is used to define statistical normal ranges for TG levels, which can range from 30 mg/dL to 10,000 mg/dL. TG levels also differ between sexes, being higher in men than women, and they increase with age and the development of conditions like central adiposity, metabolic syndrome, and diabetes (Subramanian 2000).
L686: FCS, sHTG and moderate HTG are all disorders characterized by elevated TG levels, but are differentiated by the degree of TG elevation, with moderate HTG populations generally characterized by TG between 150 mg/dL to 499 mg/dL, sHTG populations characterized by TG between 500 mg/dL to 1,000 mg/dL and FCS characterized by TG \ge 1,000 mg/dL. TG levels can overlap between categories; for example, some patients with sHTG can have TG over 1,000 mg/dL and patients with FCS with TGs in the range of sHTG.
L687: HTG can be caused by various factors. Mild to moderate HTG is a complex and genetically diverse condition, typically polygenic, resulting from the combined effects of common and rare
L688: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L689: variants in over 30 genes, as measured by genetic risk scores. All genetic forms can be worsened by environmental factors. Since these conditions arise from the interaction between multiple susceptibility genes and lifestyle factors such as poor diet, obesity, or diabetes, individuals with moderate to severe HTG should be treated as a single group, regardless of accompanying lipoprotein abnormalities. Due to the complexity of these disorders, routine genetic testing is not recommended (Subramanian 2000).
L690: FCS is a rare genetic disorder characterized by extremely high TG levels, often above 2,000 mg/dL. The elevated TG in FCS are due to the accumulation of chylomicrons, large particles that carry TG from the intestines into the bloodstream. FCS results from mutations in genes involved in the breakdown of chylomicrons, most commonly the LPL gene, which impairs the body's ability to clear TG.
L691: Both FCS and sHTG result from impaired TG metabolism. Both FCS and sHTG are associated with an increased risk of AP. Extremely high TG levels (&gt;1,000 mg/dL) in both conditions can trigger this life-threatening inflammation of the pancreas. However, the risk is particularly high in FCS patients, who may experience recurrent pancreatitis even with modest elevations in TG levels.
L692: ## Assessment
L693: In Trial CS8, the primary efficacy endpoint of mean percent change in TG from baseline at Month 6 compared to placebo was met for both 80 mg olezarsen and 50 mg olezarsen treatment groups. Specifically, the placebo-adjusted mean percent change from baseline in TG was -56.5% for olezarsen 80 mg group (95% CI, -67.4, -45.6) and -51.7% (95% CI, -62.5, -41.0) for the olezarsen 50 mg group; p-value &lt;0.0001 for both olezarsen treatment groups. Results of all sensitivity analyses on the mean percent change from baseline in fasting TG at Month 6 were consistent with the results for the primary efficacy endpoint.
L694: In the 80 mg olezarsen treatment group, the mean baseline TG was 315 mg/dL (SD of 222 mg/dL) which decreased to 102 mg/dL (SD of 65 mg/dL) at Month 6. In the 50 mg olezarsen treatment group, the mean baseline TG was 292 mg/dL (SD of 183 mg/dL) which decreased to 115 mg/dL (SD of 76 mg/dL) at Month 6. In the placebo group, the mean baseline TG was 301 mg/dL (SD of 202 mg/dL) which decreased to 258 mg/dL (SD of 141 mg/dL) at Month 6.
L695: The TG reduction with olezarsen treatment in Trial CS8 in the sHTG and moderate HTG population was consistent with the TG reduction in Trial CS3 in the FCS population. Not only were the results of Trial CS8 consistent, but the results were robust with a p-value of &lt;0.0001. The TG reduction was also clinically meaningful with TG reductions that were below a threshold of 150 mg/dL, considered to be within normal range.
L696: A sensitivity analysis using a non-parametric Wilcoxon rank sum test was conducted to explore the impact of distributional assumption on the analysis conclusion. The median percent change in TG from baseline to Month 6 endpoint was -68.6% for olezarsen 80 mg, and -15.0% for placebo. The median of the treatment difference between olezarsen 80 mg and placebo was -52.8% (95% CI -63.0, -42.6).
L697: Another prespecified sensitivity analysis for the primary endpoint used a log transformation of TG data (Table 39). Testing was conducted to investigate whether the primary analysis was
L698: sensitive to violation of the normality assumption. These sensitivity analyses were consistently in favor of olezarsen 80 mg over placebo.

For each imputed data set, the percent change from baseline to Month 6 in fasting triglycerides (TG) between each Olezarsen treatment group (Olezarsen 80 mg, Olezarsen 50 mg) and placebo are compared using an ANCOVA model with \log(y/x) as the dependent variable, where y is the value of TG at Month 6 and x is the baseline value of TG; treatment group is a factor; and the log-transformed baseline TG as a covariate. L702: In Trial CS8, there were no events of AP.
L703: ## Conclusion
L704: Yes, Trial CS8, a phase 2 trial conducted in subjects with severe and moderate HTG, is acceptable to be used as confirmatory evidence for effectiveness of TG lowering in the FCS population.
L705: FCS and severe and moderate HTG are related through their shared impact on TG metabolism and their elevated risk for pancreatitis. However, while FCS is a rare genetic disorder caused by a single-gene defect, severe and moderate HTG are often a result of both genetic predisposition and environmental influences. Both conditions require careful management to prevent complications, especially pancreatitis.
L706: ## 6.3.5. Confirmation of Other Lipoproteins and Parameters in Trial CS3 in the FCS Population
L707: ## Issue
L708: Are the changes in LDL-C, total ApoB, non-HDL-C and ApoC-III in Trial CS3 confirmed by the statistical team?
L709: ## **Background**
L710: Understanding the changes in the overall lipid and lipoprotein profile is important because it can provide an assessment of the risk for CVD. Because Trial CS3 was of 53 weeks duration, and was not powered to evaluate cardiovascular risk reduction, the confirmation of lipid and lipoprotein changes was conducted to identify the changes that occurred during the trial. The lipid profile can guide healthcare providers to prescribe dietary changes, exercise, or medications (e.g., statins) to improve cholesterol levels as needed for the individual patient.
L711: Elevated TG is a risk factor for AP. Additionally, elevated TG and LDL-C are both risk factors for CVD. TG are mainly transported by VLDL. As VLDL particles release TG into tissues (via lipoprotein lipase), they become smaller, denser particles called LDL, which are richer in cholesterol. Therefore, when TG are removed from the bloodstream, the number of LDL particles can increase, raising LDL-C levels (Feingold 2022).
L712: ApoC-III plays several important roles in lipid metabolism, particularly in the regulation of TG levels. Its main functions include inhibition of lipoprotein lipase, an enzyme responsible for breaking down TG contained within VLDL-C and chylomicrons. By inhibiting LPL, ApoC-III slows the breakdown and clearance of TG from the bloodstream. ApoC-III also impairs the liver's ability to remove TG rich lipoproteins from circulation by inhibiting their uptake by hepatic receptors. For these reasons, ApoC-III has is a target for therapies aimed at reducing TG, such as olezarsen.
L713: ## **Assessment**
L714: Refer to Section 6.2.2.6 to review changes in LDL-C, non-HDL-C, total ApoB, and ApoC-III. Refer also to Section 16 for additional lipid analyses (waterfall plots). In Trial CS3, mean LDL-C increased, mean non-HDL-C decreased, and there were slight increases in mean total ApoB with olezarsen 80 mg as compared to placebo. ApoC-III levels were also decreased with olezarsen treatment versus placebo.
L715: The observed increases in LDL-C are consistent with the effects of other TG-lowering medications in patients with sHTG. The mean LDL-C values remained below 100 mg/dL for olezarsen 80 mg/dL. On average, non-HDL-C levels decreased with olezarsen treatment, although total ApoB increased slightly compared to placebo. The net effect on atherosclerotic risk is unknown. One (1.8%) and three (5.2%) patients in the olezarsen 80 mg and 50 mg groups, respectively, experienced MACE treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) during the study, while no patients in the placebo group experienced such an event. MACE events are discussed further in Section 7.
L716: Non-HDL-C includes all forms of atherogenic cholesterol such as LDL-C, VLDL-C, and intermediate-density lipoprotein. Non-HDL-C may be a more accurate predictor of CVD risk than LDL-C alone, especially in people with moderately high TG or metabolic disorders. In the FCS population, non-HDL-C levels decreased with olezarsen 80 mg to a greater degree than placebo. The clinical significance of the change in non-HDL-C in the FCS population is unknown.
L717: Total ApoB is a measure of the total number of atherogenic lipoproteins in the blood. ApoB is a protein found on the surface of certain lipoproteins, including LDL-C, VLDL-C, intermediate-
L718: NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L719: density lipoprotein and lipoprotein(a). The increase in total ApoB may reflect the increase in LDL-C seen with olezarsen treatment.
L720: ApoC-III slows down the clearance of TG from the blood and inhibits the action of LPL, leading to HTG. ApoC-III levels were decreased with olezarsen treatment confirming the mechanism of action of olezarsen.
L721: ## Conclusion
L722: Yes, the lipid parameters LDL-C, non-HDL-C, total ApoB and ApoC-III levels were confirmed by the statistical team. These changes in the lipid profile (LDL-C, non-HDL-C, and total ApoB) are expected based on the lipid metabolism pathway and do not alter the favorable benefit-risk profile of olezarsen 80 mg of reducing TG and the risk of AP. The reduction of ApoC-III confirmed the mechanism of action of olezarsen. The results of these lipid and lipoprotein parameters will be shown in the olezarsen label.
L723: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L724: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L725: The nonclinical evaluation of the safety of olezarsen included repeat-dose general toxicology studies of up to 26 and 39-weeks in duration with 13 and 26-week recovery periods in mice and monkeys, respectively, an evaluation of effects on male and female fertility and early embryonic development in mice, a battery of genotoxicity studies in vitro (bacterial reverse mutation assay and chromosomal aberration assay) and in vivo (micronucleus assay in mouse), and an impurity qualification toxicity study. Carcinogenicity, embryofetal development, and pre-/postnatal development toxicity studies were not scientifically justified for olezarsen because studies conducted with the unconjugated active ASO (volanesorsen) provided acceptable assessments of carcinogenic, embryo-fetal development, and pre-/postnatal risk.
L726: Potentially clinically relevant observations from repeat-dose toxicity studies with olezarsen (also observed with volanesorsen, the unconjugated ASO) included thrombocytopenia, effects on the liver, injection site reactions, and hypersensitivity/inflammatory responses, which are summarized below.
L727: ## Thrombocytopenia
L728: Reductions in platelet counts were noted in mice and monkeys following SC administration of olezarsen, which was anticipated based on similar findings with volanesorsen. In a 13-week mouse toxicity study that included a 13-week recovery period, a 25% reduction in platelet count was reported in females administered 24 mg/kg/wk olezarsen (~6x maximum recommended human dose based on body surface area [BSA]) that was reversible. However, reductions in platelet counts were not observed in the 26-week mouse study at doses up to 20 mg/kg/wk (~5x MRHDBSA).
L729: In a 39-week monkey toxicity study that included a 26-week recovery period, olezarsen induced a marked reduction in platelet count (&gt;98% decrease) in a single male animal at the 12 mg/kg/wk
L730: dose (12\times \text{MRHD}_{\text{BSA}}) on Day 65, resulting in petechia and oral mucosal bleeding. The platelet count recovered following treatment with methylprednisolone but was again reduced (\sim 95\%) at the scheduled termination of this animal at Week 13.
L731: These findings were considered treatment-related and adverse considering prior observations of thrombocytopenia with volanesorsen in both mice and monkeys at clinically relevant doses. Thrombocytopenia and bleeding events were monitored during clinical development (Section 7.2)
L732: ## **Hepatic Findings**
L733: Drug-related histopathology findings were reported in the liver of both mice and monkeys following SC administration of olezarsen. In the 26-week mouse toxicity study with olezarsen, dose-related increases in the incidence and severity of hepatocellular hypertrophy, cytoplasmic alteration, karyomegaly, increased mitotic figures, and vacuolated/granular macrophages were noted. These microscopic findings correlated with increased absolute (by up to \sim 60\%) and relative liver weights at \ge 6 \text{ mg/kg/wk} (\sim 1.5\times \text{MRHD}_{\text{BSA}}) and increases in liver enzymes (approximately 3-fold AST, 7-fold ALT and 2-fold alkaline phosphatase) at 20 \text{ mg/kg/wk} dose (\sim 5\times \text{MRHD}_{\text{BSA}}) in both sexes compared to control. These effects were reversible.
L734: Hepatic-related events were also observed in a 39-week monkey toxicity study with olezarsen. Liver findings included increases (over baseline) in ALT (4-fold increase), AST (1.6-fold increase) and glutamate dehydrogenase (\sim 5-fold) at \ge 6 \text{ mg/kg/wk} (\sim 6\times \text{MRHD}_{\text{BSA}}). The increases in liver enzymes correlated with microscopic observations of basophilic granules in hepatocytes and Kupffer cells (along with Kupffer cell hypertrophy). At \ge 12 \text{ mg/kg/wk} (\sim 12\times \text{MRHD}_{\text{BSA}}), increases (up to 2-fold) in absolute and relative liver weights occurred. The effects on liver enzymes and liver weight were reversible.
L735: From a nonclinical perspective, the liver is considered a target organ but with uncertain translatability to humans. These observations in animals are known to be associated with ASO uptake and accumulation in the liver, leading to hepatocellular vacuolation, hepatocellular single-cell necrosis, and Kupffer cell hypertrophy resulting from the presence of basophilic granules in the liver of both mouse and monkey (Frazier 2015). Vacuolated or granular macrophages in the liver are also associated with cellular activation and cytokine production by the proinflammatory properties of ASOs (Engelhardt et al. 2015). These effects were associated with liver weight increases and with increases in liver enzymes. The effects on the liver were generally reversible as the incidence/severity was lessened following a recovery period. Humans are thought to be less sensitive to these effects, but liver safety was carefully monitored during clinical development (Section 7.2).
L736: ## **Injection Site Reactions**
L737: Injection site reactions were observed in the 26-week mouse and 39-week monkey toxicity studies. In mice, vacuolated/granular macrophages were observed in the skin and injection sites at all dose levels (\ge 2 \text{ mg/kg/wk}, \sim 0.5\times \text{MRHD}_{\text{BSA}}). Mononuclear cell infiltration and basophilic granules in histiocytes were also found in both sexes at the injection site. In monkeys, minimal to marked mononuclear cell infiltration or slight acute inflammation was observed at the injection sites at \ge 6 \text{ mg/kg/wk} (\sim 6\times \text{MRHD}_{\text{BSA}}). These microscopic changes are consistent with the pro-
L738: NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L739: inflammatory effects seen in animals with ASOs. Injection-site reactions were monitored during clinical development (Section 7.2).
L740: ## Hypersensitivity/Inflammatory Reactions
L741: Changes in biomarkers indicative of hypersensitivity reactions were observed in the mouse and monkey toxicity studies.
L742: In the 26-week mouse study, an increase in the potent inflammatory cytokine, monocyte chemoattractant protein-1, which suggests an inflammatory response. In addition, IgM decreased in males at \ge mid dose (up to 40% lower) and in females at \ge low dose (up to 58%). This effect was reversible in the 13-week recovery group. In the 39-week monkey study, complement activation was observed. Dose-dependent increases in complement Bb were seen at all dosages. Up to 3-fold (females) and up to 21-fold (males) at 12 and 30 mg/kg/wk and up to 3.6-fold (males) and 5.5-fold (females) at \ge2 mg/kg/wk. Increases in complement Bb was accompanied by decreases in complement C3 at all dosages in males and at 12 and 30 mg/kg/wk in females. Effects indicative of generalized inflammation occurred primarily at dosages &gt;12 mg/kg and included increases in C-reactive peptide, monocyte chemoattractant protein-1 (MCP-1), and/or gamma interferon (IFN\gamma) levels.
L743: Phosphorothioate oligonucleotides are reported to cause the release cytokines in rodents and nonhuman primates secondary to complement activation (Henry et al. 2002), but humans are generally considered less sensitive than these animal models. Monkeys are generally more sensitive to 2'-O-methoxyethyl (2' MOE) ASO-mediated complement activation than humans (Shen et al. 2014). Monitoring for hypersensitivity reactions was conducted during clinical development.
L744: ## Mutagenicity/Carcinogenicity
L745: Olezarsen was not mutagenic in vitro or in vivo. Carcinogenicity studies were not conducted with olezarsen, but lifetime carcinogenicity studies were conducted for the active unconjugated ASO, volanesorsen, in both mice and rats. These carcinogenicity studies identified increased incidences of neoplasms in both mice and rats. Increases in hepatocellular adenomas and carcinomas, hemangiomas and hemangiosarcomas, histiocytic sarcomas, and pituitary gland adenomas were observed in mice, while malignant fibrous histiocytomas at the injection site were observed in rats. These findings are likely related to the increased sensitivity of rodents to the proinflammatory effects of phosphorothioate oligonucleotides and/or the method of administration and have also been reported in carcinogenicity studies with other ASOs. Therefore, the clinical relevance of these tumors is uncertain but may be limited.
L746: ## Reproductive Safety
L747: No significant risk for pregnancy or embryo-fetal development was detected in studies with volanesorsen in mice and rats. Refer to Section 8.4 of this review for labeling recommendations.
L748: ## Conclusion
L749: Overall, administration of olezarsen in animals at clinically relevant exposures was well-tolerated. Observations identified with olezarsen in the 26-week mouse and 39-week monkey studies of potential clinical relevance were thrombocytopenia, effects in liver, injection site
L750: reactions, and hypersensitivity/inflammatory responses. Most of the effects are consistent with those reported for other ASOs, and indeed some of these effects were also noted clinically (see Section 7.7). The clinical significance of the changes in complement activation observed in cynomolgus monkeys is unclear, but monkeys are generally thought to have increased sensitivity compared to humans. The safety margins and multiples for these observations from these pivotal studies are summarized below in Table 40.

L758: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L759: The potential safety concerns for olezarsen were initially based on the clinical experiences with volanesorsen and information known about anti-sense oligonucleotides in general. The safety profile of volanesorsen and/or antisense oligonucleotides is characterized by hypersensitivity reactions/flu-like reactions (Section 7.7.1), injection site reactions (Section 7.7.5), thrombocytopenia and bleeding events (Section 7.7.2), hepatic-related events (Section 7.7.3), dysglycemia (Section 7.7.4), changes in LDL-C and ApoB (Section 7.7.6), and immunogenicity (Section 7.7.8). These topics informed the key safety review issues. For further discussion of these issues, refer to the relevant sections hyperlinked above.
L760: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L761: ## 7.3.1. Adverse Events Identified in Postmarket Experiences
L762: Although olezarsen is not yet commercially available in any country, volanesorsen shares the same identical oligonucleotide sequence as olezarsen and is approved outside of the United States. Given their similarities, the Applicant was asked to provide a summary of safety for volanesorsen based on the global experience of volanesorsen exposure. Volanesorsen was granted conditional marketing approval in the European Economic Area member states in 2019 and in Great Britain in 2021. Marketing authorization was received in Brazil in 2021, (b) (4) Volanesorsen is indicated in all of these countries as an adjunct to diet in adult patients with FCS and at high risk for pancreatitis, in whom response to diet and triglyceride lowering therapy has been inadequate.
L763: As of November 2, 2023, the estimated cumulative postmarketing exposure is 226.6 patient years and (b) (4) individuals have been exposed to commercial volanesorsen. The important identified and potential risk are thrombocytopenia, injection site reactions, immunogenicity, hepatotoxicity, and nephrotoxicity. There have been no new safety signals identified for volanesorsen in the postmarket setting. A search of medical literature using PubMed and the search term 'volanesorsen', did not yield reports of new safety findings.
L764: ## 7.3.2. Expectations on Safety
L765: It is anticipated that olezarsen's safety profile in the postmarket setting, when used in the indicated treatment population, will be similar to the safety experience in the clinical trial population. (b) (4)
L766: Given that this application seeks a treatment indication in a rare disease population, one would expect the number of subjects exposed to olezarsen in the postmarket setting to be small. A higher-than-expected amount of olezarsen patient exposure could suggest off-label treatment in other HTG populations. Because the clinical trials of olezarsen in other HTG populations are blinded and ongoing, the benefits and risks in an off-label population are unknown.
L767: ## 7.3.3. Additional Safety Issues From Other Disciplines
L768: None.
L769: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L770: Clinical study data were analyzed using JMP and JMP Clinical software. Clinical data scientists (CDSs) from the Division of Biomedical Informatics generated the safety tables and figures,
L771: unless otherwise noted. These include safety tables and figures for patient disposition, exposure data, AEs, and serious adverse events (SAEs).
L772: Phase 1 and 2 trials were screened for safety signals that may not have been identified in the phase 3 program.
L773: The safety population in all clinical trials was defined as randomized subjects who received at least one dose of study drug. Subjects were analyzed in the treatment group according to the actual treatment they received.
L774: The primary data for review of olezarsen safety was from CS3, the pivotal, 53-week, placebo-controlled trial in adults with FCS. Supportive safety data was provided in CS8, the 53-week placebo-controlled trial in adults with moderately elevated TG at risk for ASCVD and adults with high TG. Two open-label trials in adults with FCS, CS13 and CS7, provide additional supportive safety data. All eligible subjects completing treatment in CS3 could elect to enroll in an OLE study, CS13, and Trial CS7 provides safety information from adults with FCS that had been previously treated with volanesorsen.
L775: The Applicant provided several pooled analyses using different combinations of the above trials including a pool of the phase 3 placebo-controlled trials, CS3 and CS8, a pool of studies of individuals with FCS – CS3, CS7, CS13, and a pool of all phase 2/3 trials – CS2, CS3, CS8, CS7, and CS13. Although the phase 3 trial designs share a placebo-controlled period and dosing regimen of olezarsen, both trials had key differences that may impact the types and frequency of events, including different patient populations and treatment allocation (1:1 vs. 2:1). Open-label trials have inherent biases given treatment assignment is known which may impact both trial participants and individuals involved in the study conduct. Therefore, pooling an open-label trial with a placebo-controlled blinded trial may be limited in its ability to interpret a drug's true effect. For this safety review, longitudinal data on subjects participating in CS3 and CS13 was evaluated such as platelet count changes over time. However, in general, in this clinical reviewer's opinion, pooling did not provide additional information that cannot be gleaned from review of the individual trial safety data. Therefore, this review does not present pooled safety data.
L776: In Trials CS3 and CS8, a TEAE was defined as an event present prior to receiving the first dose of study drug and subsequently worsens, or it was not present prior to receiving the first dose of study drug but subsequently appeared.
L777: SAEs were defined as any AE that resulted in death, was life-threatening, required inpatient hospitalization or prolonged a current hospitalization, cause persistent or significant disability/incapacity, or was a congenital anomaly/birth defect in the offspring of a subject who received study drug. In addition, any event that required intervention to prevent one of these outcomes could be considered a serious adverse event (SAE) (i.e., allergic bronchospasm requiring intensive treatment in an emergency department or home), but this was at the judgment of the investigator.
L778: Following discussion between the clinical reviewers and clinical data scientist teams, the following were implemented in the safety analyses, first because adjudicated pancreatitis events were considered clinical endpoints of efficacy, these events were excluded from the safety tables and figures. Second, the Applicant translated verbatim terms to Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) preferred terms for the events reported in the trial. The
L779: translations were reviewed, and several verbatim terms noted events occurring during or shortly after administration of study drug which were recoded with the preferred term 'injection related reaction'. For example, a verbatim term 'IP INJECTION REACTION DIARREA (sp) AFTER 15 MINUTES' was coded by the Applicant with the preferred term of 'Diarrhoea'. This event was considered more consistent with a hypersensitivity reaction and was recoded as 'injection related reaction' by the review team.
L780: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L781: The safety database for this application is small, due in part to the rarity of the FCS population. The Applicant was strongly encouraged, before phase 3 was initiated, to bolster the safety database by increasing the size of studies investigating olezarsen in other patient populations, especially since the Applicant was contemplating development of olezarsen for the more common severe hypertriglyceridemia population (i.e., non-FCS, TG \ge 500 \text{ mg/dL}) at the time. In response, the Applicant conducted a small (n=154), 53-week, placebo-controlled trial (CS8) in patients with moderate HTG with or at risk for ASCVD and patients with sHTG.
L782: Limitations of this safety database include differences in the CS3 and CS8 populations in regard to comorbidities, concomitant medications, and treatment objectives (i.e., the majority of subjects in CS8 had moderate HTG with risk for ASCVD and therefore the primary objective is to reduce the frequency of MACE versus pancreatitis). These factors may limit the generalizability of the safety findings in Trial CS8 to the FCS population. Another gap in the safety database is the absence of safety data available to evaluate the effect of olezarsen in pediatric patients with FCS, a condition that presents during childhood. The Applicant has an agreed Pediatric Investigation Plan with the European Medicine Agency that is deferred (b) (4) to determine pediatric dosing (EMA 2023). Therefore, it is possible, especially given the size of the database, that additional safety signals may emerge or be more clearly characterized with additional exposure in patients with FCS.
L783: However, the strengths of the safety database include the placebo-controlled design, use of two doses to assess dose-response, and the 1-year treatment duration in this rare disease population, with additional longer-term exposure in the OLE. In addition, there were very few study dropouts and the amount of missing data is small. Trial CS8, despite its limitations provides additional supportive safety information from a placebo-controlled trial in an HTG population.
L784: Overall, the safety database is adequate to assess the safety of olezarsen for the proposed indication, dosage regimen, and patient population.
L785: At the time of the various data-cutoffs, a total of 202 subjects were exposed to olezarsen in the phase 2/3 program, including 87 subjects with FCS. Of these 87 subjects, 54 had at least 12 months exposure to olezarsen (35 subjects at 80 mg and 19 subjects at 50 mg or 80 mg). Table 41 below provides the number of subjects exposed to either olezarsen or placebo in the phase 2/3 program. Treatment exposure for Trials CS3 and CS8 follow the table.

L818: ## 7.6.2.2. Deaths, Trial CS3
L819: There was one death in the olezarsen 50 mg group in Trial CS3. A summary is included here.
L820: - Subject #CS3-(b) (6); CS3 – Sudden death (unknown cause), olezarsen 50 mg Q4W – 74-year-old female with a medical history of pancreatitis and hypertension. The patient was receiving the concomitant medications calcifediol, calcium carbonate, cholecalciferol, denosumab, gemfibrozil, hydrochlorothiazide, losartan, manidipine, and omeprazole. She had been previously treated with volanesorsen in Trial CS7 OLE study for 1.5 years. She discontinued volanesorsen due to a nonserious treatment-emergent adverse event (TEAE) of polymyalgia rheumatica and had been off volanesorsen for 2.5 years. The subject had received two doses of olezarsen 50 mg prior to her death. The subject died suddenly while she was making dinner on Study Day 42 (12 days post last dose of olezarsen). There were no symptoms in the minutes or hours prior to the event. No TEAEs, clinically meaningful laboratory results, or electrocardiogram (ECG) abnormalities were reported for the patient prior to the death. The cause of death remains unknown. No autopsy details or death certificate were available.
L821: Following review of the narratives and case report forms, it is difficult to discern olezarsen's contribution, if any, to this death given the limited information available.
L822: ## 7.6.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial CS3
L823: Narratives, as well as relevant datasets, and case report forms were reviewed for these events. These events are further described, where applicable, in the key safety review topics (i.e., serious hepatic-related event of cirrhosis is discussed in Section 7.7.3)
L824: The number and percentage of subjects with a treatment-emergent SAE were lower in the olezarsen-treatment groups. A review of the SAEs did not yield a safety signal for olezarsen due to the low number of events and alternative causative etiologies (e.g., SAE of intestinal perforation secondary to a foreign body ingestion; SAE of bacterial gastroenteritis confounded by recent travel history; SAE of intentional overdose confounded by concurrent medical history of post-traumatic stress disorder treated with antidepressant medication).

L842: ## 7.6.2.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial CS3
L843: Results of the FDA analysis of TEAEs by preferred term and by FMQ that occurred in ≥5% of subjects in any treatment arm for CS3 are shown in Table 47 and Table 48. A higher proportion of subjects in the placebo group compared to the olezarsen treatment groups reported a TEAE. FMQs that had a larger risk difference in the 80 mg olezarsen treatment groups versus placebo were related to reductions in platelet count, injection site reactions, nasopharyngitis, anemia,
L844: vomiting, dyspnea, arthralgia, urinary tract infections. Discussion of the key safety review topics regarding reduction in platelet count, injection site reactions, are discussed in Sections 7.7.
L845: It was noted that the preferred term 'anxiety' occurred in three (14.3%) subjects and the FMQ anxiety occurred in four (19%) subjects treated with olezarsen 50 mg and none in either the olezarsen 80 mg or placebo treatment groups. Review of the anxiety AEs at subject level did not suggest a relatedness to study drug. There was also no imbalance for anxiety or other psychiatric FMQs for olezarsen 80 mg. Note that the FMQ 'Study agent abuse potential' is populated by the preferred term, 'somnolence' in the 80 mg olezarsen group, which on its own does not suggest a safety signal for potential abuse.

L874: ## 7.6.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Trial CS3
L875: Results of the FDA analysis of laboratory value abnormalities for Trial CS3 are shown in Table 49. There was an imbalance unfavorable to olezarsen in the proportion of subjects with elevations in fasting plasma glucose Level 1 \ge 100 mg/dL to 125 mg/dL and Level 2 \ge 126 mg/dL, as well as low glucose levels &lt;54 mg/dL and &lt;40 mg/dL. This risk is further discussed in Section 7.7.4.

L903: ## Hematology
L904: Thrombocytopenia leading to bleeding events is a potential antisense oligonucleotide class effect; therefore, analyses evaluated coagulation parameters and platelet levels. Additional hematopoietic parameters were also evaluated. A greater proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects compared to placebo-treated subjects met lower platelet thresholds. This risk is further discussed in Section 7.7.2. Another difference of note was a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects had an HbA1c \ge 5.7\% compared to placebo-treated subjects. Dysglycemia is discussed in Section 7.7.4.

L940: ## 7.6.3. Safety Results, Trial ISIS 678354-CS8
L941: ## 7.6.3.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Summary, Trial CS8
L942: In Trial CS8, there was a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects experiencing an SAE or discontinuation of study drug due to an AE compared to the placebo-treated subjects (Table 56). Subjects treated with olezarsen 50 mg reported a higher percentage of study drug discontinuations or modifications due to AEs than the olezarsen 80 mg and placebo treatment groups. However, overall TEAEs were higher in the placebo treatment group (76.9%) versus the olezarsen treatment groups (olezarsen 50 mg, 74.1%; olezarsen 80 mg, 66.7%).

L952: ## 7.6.3.2. Deaths, Trial CS8
L953: There was one death in a subject randomized to olezarsen 50 mg in Trial CS8; a brief summary is included here:
L954: - Subject #CS8-(b) (6); CS8 – Death (unknown cause); olezarsen 50 mg Q4W - 76-year-old male in the olezarsen 50 mg group, experienced a severe serious TEAE leading to death (unknown cause) on Study Day 206, approximately 5 weeks after the last dose of olezarsen (Study Day 169). The patient had ongoing coronary artery disease, a cardiac pacemaker insertion, hypertension, hyperlipidemia requiring statin treatment, Type 2 diabetes, and was a former smoker. The patient experienced no liver-related or renal-related laboratory abnormalities with olezarsen treatment during the study. The MACE adjudication committee were unable to determine if the event was a myocardial infarction or heart failure.
L955: Note that another death occurred in Trial CS2, which enrolled a similar population with moderate HTG, with established CVD or at high risk for CVD and is described below:
L956: - Subject #CS2-(b) (6); CS2 – Cardiac arrest, olezarsen 15 mg every 2 weeks – 63-year-old male randomized to olezarsen 15 mg every 2 weeks, experienced a fatal cardiac arrest on Study Day 90, 5 days after the last dose of olezarsen. He had received seven doses of olezarsen before his death. No autopsy was performed. Relevant medical history included type 2 diabetes, acute myocardial infarction, coronary artery bypass graft, coronary revascularization, heart failure, hypertension, and valvular heart disease. At screening, his treated blood pressure was 123/65, and body mass index was 36.6 kg/m2 (Class 2 obesity). Concomitant medications included rosuvastatin 40 mg, Vascepa, fenofibrate, Lasix, carvedilol, aspirin, losartan, insulin, canagliflozin, and clopidogrel.
L957: It is difficult to discern olezarsen's contribution, if any, to these deaths especially given the study subjects' age, significant cardiovascular history, and underlying comorbidities.
L958: ## 7.6.3.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial CS8
L959: In Trial CS8, SAEs occurred in a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects. The biggest treatment difference between olezarsen and placebo-treatment groups were SAEs occurring within the cardiac disorder system organ class (SOC). This risk is discussed further in Section 7.7.7. Four subjects in the olezarsen 80 mg group compared to one subject in the placebo group reported an oncological AE. There is temporal plausibility given the time to diagnosis for some of these cases, however, causality cannot be easily determined given the small number of cases and underlying comorbidities – remote smoking history and type 2 diabetes that confound the causality assessment.

L970: ## 7.6.3.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Trial CS8
L971: FDA analysis of AEs and FMQ leading to treatment discontinuation was done. The analysis by SOC and preferred term is shown in Table 58. FMQ analysis is not shown as it did not yield additional relevant information. Eight (13.8%) subjects treated with olezarsen 50 mg and three (5.3%) subjects treated with olezarsen 80 mg discontinued due to an AE compared to no placebo-treated subjects. Half (n=4) of the subjects in the olezarsen 50 mg group discontinued treatment due to events related to the injection site, and one of the three subjects treated with olezarsen 80 mg who discontinued due to an AE did so because of an injection-related reaction (verbatim term 'post-dose flushing - face, neck, chest, lower body'). Further discussion of injection site reactions and hypersensitivity events are found in Section 7.7.5 and Section 7.7.1.

L980: ## 7.6.3.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial CS8
L981: Overall, there was a smaller proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects compared to placebo-treated subjects with any TEAE. TEAEs occurring at \ge 5\% frequency are shown in Table 59 below. There were imbalances not favoring both olezarsen treatment groups compared to the placebo group for the following preferred terms that occurred in \ge 5\% of subjects: 'alanine aminotransferase increased', 'diabetes mellitus', 'injection site erythema', 'COVID-19', and 'liver function test increased'. Related topics such as injection site reactions, hepatic-related events, and dysglycemia events are further discussed in Section 7.7.4. Please note that there were imbalances not favoring the placebo treatment group compared to the olezarsen treatment groups such as 'type 2 diabetes', 'sinusitis', 'fall', 'hypertension', and 'urinary tract infection'.

L992: ## 7.6.3.6. Laboratory Findings, Trial CS8
L993: ## Chemistry
L994: Review of the chemistry laboratory parameters demonstrated an imbalance in the number of subjects with an elevated fasting glucose (Level 1 \ge100 to 125 mg/dL). Further discussion of dysglycemia is in Section 7.7.4. No other clinically meaningful differences were observed. See Section 17 for details.

L1051: ## 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L1052: ## 7.7.1. Hypersensitivity Reactions
L1053: ## Issue
L1054: Serious hypersensitivity reactions have occurred with olezarsen treatment, it is unclear whether there is a relationship to the development of anti-olezarsen antibodies or previous volanesorsen treatment.
L1055: ## **Background**
L1056: Hypersensitivity reactions is a known and labeled adverse drug reaction for several FDA-approved ASOs. As of 2023, there have been 10 ASOs approved for a variety of medical diseases. Hypersensitivity reactions are listed in the Warnings &amp; Precautions section of labeling for four of these drugs – defibrotide, inotersen, eteplirsen, and golodirsen. The types of reactions observed include rash, urticaria, skin exfoliation, chills, flushing, dysphagia, tachycardia, and bronchospasm. Serious hypersensitivity reactions were noted to occur in the clinical development program for volanesorsen, including an anaphylactic-like reaction in a study subject without FCS within 5 minutes of dosing which required emergency treatment (EMDAC 2018).
L1057: ## **Assessment**
L1058: In this review, hypersensitivity events were evaluated in categories defined as flu-like reactions and hypersensitivity reactions using a variety of company, FDA, and MedDRA queries.
L1059: Flu-like reactions (FLR) were defined as the following:
L1060: - FLR 1 – influenza-like illness, pyrexia, feeling hot, body temperature increased, chills, myalgia, or arthralgia starting on day of injection or next day.
- FLR 2 – (A) influenza-like illness or (B) pyrexia, feeling hot, or body temperature increased, plus two of the following: chills, myalgia, or arthralgia starting on day of injection or next day.
L1061: Hypersensitivity reactions were defined using the following:
L1062: - Hypersensitivity Standardised MedDRA Query (SMQ) (narrow),
- Hypersensitivity FMQ (narrow, broad, algorithmic4) and
- Anaphylactic FMQ (narrow).
L1063: An AE of special interest was defined as a requirement of any use of medications as pretreatment to avoid a hypersensitivity reaction or recurrence of a previous hypersensitivity reaction.
L1064: Table 66 is an overview of the phase 2/3 trials which combines the hypersensitivity events and flu-like reactions, followed by a breakdown of the types of each event in Trials CS3 and CS8. Serious events or events that led to study drug discontinuation from these trials are discussed.
