L1084: In the ongoing open-label trials, CS7 and CS13, there were additional hypersensitivity and FLR events observed (hypersensitivity SMQ [narrow] CS7 [n=3; 12.5%] and CS13 [n=7; 13%]; FLR CS7 [n=4, 16.7%] and CS13 [n=2; 3.7%]).
L1085: Among Trials CS3, CS8, CS13, and CS13, there were seven subjects who discontinued due to hypersensitivity or FLR. Three of these discontinuations were considered serious TEAEs, including one serious 'anaphylactic reaction'. The narratives of these subjects were reviewed and are summarized in tabular form in Section 17. Summaries of the serious hypersensitivity events are included below.
L1086: ## **Serious Hypersensitivity Events**
L1087: - Subject (b) (6), Trial CS7 – ‘Hypersensitivity Reaction’: 62-year-old female with FCS. She had been previously treated with volanesorsen for 5 years. Her last dose of volanesorsen was approximately 9 months before starting olezarsen 80 mg Q4W in the open-label trial, CS7. Within 10 hours of administration of her 2nd dose of olezarsen, she reported an itchy
L1088: rash on her back. Two days later she reported flu-like symptoms (runny nose, headache, sore throat, and fatigue), progressive whole-body rash, wheezing, stiff neck, swelling of the eyes and face, and dyspnea. She did not seek any additional medical attention and reported treatment with loratadine 10 mg and fexofenadine hydrochloride 180 mg. Approximately 10 days after the start of the event, the swelling resolved and treatment was permanently discontinued. Her ADA titers remained negative.
L1089: - Subject [REDACTED](b) (6), Trial CS13 – ‘Hypersensitivity Reaction’: 56-year-old male with FCS, previously treated with volanesorsen for 4 months and reported an immunization reaction – with injection site discoloration, injection site erythema with occurrence of positive ADA (peak titer 6000). No further volanesorsen treatment was received.
L1090: This subject's first dose of olezarsen occurred in the OLE study (randomized to placebo in parent study CS3). ADA titers at the beginning of the OLE were 800. Approximately two minutes after the administration of the second dose of olezarsen, the subject exhibited an intense redness of his face and trunk, which was accompanied by an intense headache. He did not present with dyspnea or fainting at this time. The subject's vital parameters were immediately checked and were as follows: BP of 107/70 mmHg, heart rate (HR) of 72 bpm, and no dyspnea on room air with 88% oxygen saturation (SpO2). The patient did not go to the emergency room or hospital but was treated at the study site with IV saline and 4 mg betamethasone intramuscularly. Monitoring of the patient continued. Resolution of the skin rash was noted after approximately 10 minutes post onset of symptoms. The investigator noted the appearance of a wheal reaction with redness in correspondence to the patient's abdomen area (periumbilical region and under the right rib) which was associated with intense itching. Approximately 20 minutes after symptoms began, the patient's SpO2 (98%) and BP (127/85 mmHg) had returned to normal. Treatment with chlorpheniramine maleate was administered intravenously to treat the itching. An hour after the onset of hypersensitivity, the patient experienced bronchospasm and shaking chills. Treatment included a single dose of methylprednisolone with a combination of beclomethasone dipropionate, ipratropium bromide and salbutamol which resulted in an improvement of symptoms. An ECG showed no abnormalities. Laboratory values of hsCRP, C3, C4, absolute eosinophil count and tryptase before and 1.5 hours after the event were normal. ADA following second dose increased to 6400. The subject's condition improved, and he was discharged from the study site on the same day in good clinical condition.
L1091: - Subject [REDACTED](b) (6), Trial CS13 – ‘Anaphylactic reaction’: 26-year-old male with FCS, previously treated with volanesorsen in Trial CS7, an open-label extension study. In CS7-OLE, he received 24 doses of volanesorsen 300 mg weekly and had a positive ADA titer in [REDACTED](b) (6) (last assessment) of 400. He enrolled in Trial CS3 (baseline ADA positive titer 400) and was treated with olezarsen 80 mg for 1 year and rolled into the open-label study, CS13 (ADA titer at baseline 25,600). Forty-five minutes after each of the first two injections of olezarsen in the OLE trial, he experienced chills, headache, and diffuse abdominal discomfort lasting 30 minutes to 1 hour, and approximately 2 to 3 hours of diffuse muscle tenderness. Because of these symptoms, the subject was pre-treated with 40 mg of prednisolone and 500 mg of Tylenol for subsequent injections without further difficulty until the 7th dose. Following his dose of prednisolone and olezarsen, he left the study site in stable condition, but returned within 5 minutes because of acute onset of profuse diaphoresis, facial edema, diffuse erythema, and severe low back pain. He was brought to the emergency department and administered IV Benadryl, solumedrol 125 mg, dexamethasone 10 mg,
L1092: NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L1093: famotidine 10 mg, and fluids. Vitals at the time were BP of 140 to 160 mmHg (note at screening for CS13 – the subject's BP was 167/107 mmHg), HR 110 bpm, and oxygen saturation of 94%. ECG showed sinus tachycardia with non-specific ST-T abnormalities. Tryptase was 5.0 before dose, 4 hours later 10.7 (normal &lt;11). On the same day, he recovered from the event and was discharged from the ER with a dexamethasone taper. The study drug was discontinued due to this event. His ADA titers remained positive. At the time of this event, his ADA titers were 6400 and, in the weeks, following increased to 12,800. Approximately 2 months after his last dose ADA titers were 200.
L1094: ## Reviewer Comment
L1095: This event was submitted to the IND as a 7-day suspected unexpected serious adverse reaction. At that time, an internal consult with the Division of Pulmonology, Allergy, and Critical Care was requested for review of this event. Following review of the information, the Division of Pulmonology, Allergy, and Critical Care review team felt this event was more consistent with an acute injection-related reaction versus anaphylaxis (Document Archiving, Reporting and Regulatory Tracking System [DARRTS] ID 5100318).
L1096: ## Conclusion
L1097: Hypersensitivity reactions, including serious events, occur in patients with FCS treated with olezarsen. The onset of symptoms usually occurs within minutes or same day of the injection. There is a variable length of treatment duration before the event (first couple of doses versus years of exposure). The numbers of hypersensitivity and FLR events overall are very small, so their dependence on ADA is unclear. Of the three serious cases, ADA values appear to increase in two cases before the event. There were only two cases of subjects receiving pre-treatment to avoid a hypersensitivity reaction, and both times this did not prevent events. Pre-treatment is not recommended given no systematic protocol for pre-treatment was provided or prospectively tested. It is anticipated that additional potentially serious hypersensitivity reactions will occur in individuals with FCS treated with olezarsen. Serious hypersensitivity reactions are a known adverse drug reaction for this drug class. Management of this risk through labeling with revisions to include the types of signs and symptoms observed is a reasonable mitigation.
L1098: ## 7.7.2. Platelet Reductions and Bleeding Events
L1099: ## Issue
L1100: In the clinical trials, olezarsen-treated subjects experienced a higher frequency of platelet reductions compared to placebo-treated subjects, although none were severe (defined as &lt;50 x109/L). However, because of the exclusion of study subjects with baseline platelet counts below 100 x 109/L, it is unknown whether serious thrombocytopenia events will occur in patients with FCS, a patient population with reported platelet variability who may have below normal platelet counts at the beginning of treatment in the postapproval setting.
L1101: ## Background
L1102: Anti-sense oligonucleotides are associated with thrombocytopenia and risk of serious bleeding, including fatal hemorrhage complications (Benson et al. 2018; Dominguez Senin et al. 2022). The primary safety concern for volanesorsen (which shares the same oligonucleotide sequence as
L1103: olezarsen) was severe thrombocytopenia and risk of serious bleeding (EMDAC 2018). Although no deaths or serious bleeding events were noted in the volanesorsen clinical development program, there was a higher proportion of volanesorsen-treated subjects with clinical bleeding events (excluding bleeding at the injection site) than placebo-treated subjects (36% versus 6%, respectively). Reductions in platelet counts associated with volanesorsen were characterized by gradual declines over time of approximately 30% from baseline as well as large precipitous drops in platelet count below 50 \times 10^9/L. The mechanism triggering these events is unknown but may be a result of drug-induced immune thrombocytopenia leading to increased destruction of platelets.
L1104: Given the concern for thrombocytopenia, the pivotal FCS trial excluded study subjects that had a baseline platelet count below 100 \times 10^9/L (&gt;140 \times 10^9/L was considered the lower bound of a normal platelet count) and instituted frequent platelet monitoring (every 2 weeks). Note, no subject was excluded during the screening process due to a platelet count &lt;100 \times 10^9/L. In CS8, platelet levels were obtained Q4W; in CS7 initially every 2 weeks for first 13 weeks then Q4W, and in CS13 Q4W, 8 weeks, and 12 weeks in the 1st, 2nd, and 3rd years, respectively. All protocols had pre-specified actions for platelet counts below different platelet count thresholds below 100 \times 10^9/L. An adverse event of special interest was defined as any case of a platelet count reduction below 50 \times 10^9/L accompanied by a major or clinically relevant bleeding event, or platelet count below 25 \times 10^9/L.
L1105: ## Assessment
L1106: ## Baseline Platelet Values
L1107: Given the reported platelet variability in individuals with FCS, the baseline platelet count was defined as the average of all assessments before the first dose of study drug. The average baseline level of platelets in CS3 was 200, 188, and 215 \times 10^9/L in the olezarsen 50 mg, 80 mg, and placebo groups, respectively. The mean baseline level of platelets in CS8 was 244, 249, and 252 \times 10^9/L in the olezarsen 50 mg, 80 mg, and placebo groups, respectively.
L1108: ## Platelet Values Over Time
L1109: As shown in the Figure 11 and Figure 12 below, platelets decreased over time for the subjects in the olezarsen groups compared to those in the placebo group during CS3. Average declines in platelet count of approximately 5 to 15% from baseline were noted in the olezarsen treatment groups. There were 11 (25.6%) olezarsen-treated subjects and 5 (21.7%) placebo-treated subjects that had platelet counts that went below 100 \times 10^9/L. In CS3, there were 4 (18.2%) subjects treated with olezarsen 80 mg and 1 (4.3%) placebo-treated subject with dose interruptions due to low platelet count.
L1110: By visual inspection of Figure 11 and Figure 13 from CS3 and CS8, respectively, of mean platelet count over time, it appears that platelet counts gradually decline over the first 9 months and then appear to stabilize, although there is significant variability at some timepoints in the FCS population.

L1121: ## Categorical Changes in Platelet Values
L1122: A higher proportion of subjects treated with olezarsen exhibited categorical absolute or relative reductions in platelet count compared to subjects treated with placebo in both CS3 and CS8. In CS3, almost 50% of subjects treated with olezarsen versus 30% of placebo-treated subjects had a 30% reduction in platelet counts from baseline. In CS8, roughly 20% of olezarsen versus 5% of placebo-treated subjects had a 30% reduction in platelet counts from baseline. However, the percentage of subjects with 50% reductions from baseline were similar across treatment groups in both trials.
L1123: The empirical cumulative distribution functions for percent change from baseline to nadir platelet count are shown in Figure 15 and Figure 16 for CS3 and CS8, respectively, allowing one to note the proportion of patients in each arm of each trial that had a percent reduction in platelets of any amount of interest (or greater). For example, it can be easily discerned that ~50% of patients assigned to olezarsen in CS3 had \ge 30\% reduction in platelet count from baseline to on-study nadir.

L1135: ## Adverse Events Related to Platelet Count or Bleeding Events
L1136: ## Reduced Platelet Count Event Terms
L1137: In CS3 and CS8, a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated compared to placebo-treated subjects had AEs reported related to a reduced platelet count. Not all cases of thrombocytopenia or platelet count reductions were reported as AEs, and it is unclear whether investigators employed a systematic approach to determining whether a reduction in platelet count qualified as an “adverse event” and whether such an event should be reported as a decrease in platelet count or thrombocytopenia. Therefore, the number of events portrayed in Table 69 is lower than the actual number of cases that occurred in the trials, and a distinction between the event terms should not be overinterpreted. In CS3 and CS8, no subjects discontinued due to a thrombocytopenia-related AE.

L1142: ## Bleeding Event Terms
L1143: Bleeding events were tabulated below using the MedDRA Hemorrhage SMQ. SMQs are validated sets of MedDRA terms used to investigate specific safety topics. The Hemorrhage SMQ includes preferred terms that refer not only to clinical bleeding events but also to abnormal laboratory values (e.g., decreased hemoglobin), which may or may not be caused by bleeding. Additional analyses were done to exclude events at the injection site and/or related to a laboratory value, as well as evaluation of events in subjects with concomitant anticoagulant or antiplatelet medications.
L1144: Using the Hemorrhage SMQ, the proportion of subjects with an event was similar across treatment groups in Trials CS3 and CS8. After excluding events that were localized to the injection site or represent a laboratory value, there was no meaningful differences were noted in “clinical” bleeding events across treatment groups.
L1145: No olezarsen-treated subject in Trial CS3 paused, reduced, or discontinued olezarsen dosing due to a bleeding TEAE. Most of the subjects in Trial CS3 were not on concomitant anticoagulant or antiplatelet medications at the time of a clinical bleeding event. In contrast, with the exception of the clinical bleeding TEAE of epistaxis in a subject in the olezarsen 80 mg group, all “clinical” bleeding TEAEs in Trial CS8 were experienced by subjects concomitantly receiving anticoagulant or antiplatelet medications in Trial CS8. There were three olezarsen-treated subjects in Trial CS8 who discontinued, paused, or reduced study medication due to a bleeding AE at the injection site – ‘injection site bruising’ (n=1); ‘injection site hemorrhage’ (n=2) - none
L1146: were serious. In CS13, there was one SAE of bleeding, 'lower gastrointestinal hemorrhage' in a treatment-naïve subject while taking 80 mg olezarsen. In CS7, there was a serious bleeding TEAE of 'hematemesis'. Summaries of the SAEs due to bleeding TEAEs in subjects taking olezarsen follow Table 70.

L1152: - Subject ID (b) (6) (CS3/CS13) – 'lower gastrointestinal hemorrhage' - 32-year-old male with FCS and history of pancreatitis, initially enrolled in index Trial CS3 (assigned to placebo) and then continued in Trial CS13. The subject recovered and began receiving olezarsen 80 mg Q4W. On Study Day 44 of CS13, the subject experienced cholangitis secondary to an abdominal drain installation. The subject was on the anticoagulant dalteparin during this time due to bile duct stent placement. The subject received a dose of olezarsen on Day 57. On Study Day 71, the subject presented to the emergency department due to two dark stools. The patient experienced both a serious TEAE of lower gastrointestinal hemorrhage and anemia related to the gastrointestinal hemorrhage. Laboratory values noted anemia (hemoglobin 6.8 g/dL reference range 14.0-18.0) and platelet count of 431 x 109/L. The subject was subsequently
L1153: hospitalized, anticoagulant therapy stopped (narrative states dalteparin and warfarin stopped), and blood transfusion was received. Gastrosocopy revealed the presence of small esophageal varices without red signs. However, near the anastomosis, there was a presence of edematous mucosa, which had very tortuous folds and there were indelible clots in one of the folds. An echo-endoscopy was conducted to clarify the origin of the clot, and the result revealed no gastric varices. Hemoglobin on Day 75 was 8.0 g/dL. While hospitalized the subject experienced an adjudicated case of mild pancreatitis. The subject was discharged on Study Day 76. He continued to receive olezarsen. In the 90-day safety update, it was noted that the subject's platelet count during the OLE fell to 68 x109/L on OLE Study Day 421/Week 61. A gross hemolysis was noted for the patient's blood sample. Laboratory tests were repeated on the same day (OLE Study Day 421) which showed platelet counts of 151 x109/L (central lab) and 127 x109/L (local lab). Subsequent platelet count on OLE Study Day 449 (Week 65) was 142 x109/L. No concurrent bleeding TEAE was reported and the treatment with the Study Drug continued without interruption. No other platelet counts of &lt;140 x109/L were observed for this patient during the study.
L1154: ## **Reviewer Comment**
L1155: This subject had persistent complications secondary to necrotizing pancreatitis and biliary disease which began during placebo treatment in CS3 requiring abdominal drains and anticoagulant therapy which may have contributed to this subject's lower gastrointestinal bleed and resulting anemia. This subject had low levels of hemoglobin prior to this event and during placebo treatment in Trial CS3 (Day 383 of CS3/Day -35 from first olezarsen dose: hemoglobin 9.0 g/dL, Day 39 post-olezarsen hemoglobin of 8.4 g/dL). The subject has continued with olezarsen treatment – hemoglobin levels are below normal (Day 295, hemoglobin 10.1 g/dL) but no additional bleeding events have been reported. Platelet count fell 50% from baseline but had remained within normal limits, apart from one sample that had hemolysis and did not have a confirmed low value with repeat testing on the same day.
L1156: - Subject ID # [REDACTED](b) (6) (CS7) - 'hematemesis' 50-year-old female with FCS previously treated with volanesorsen for approximately 2.5 years, enrolled in the open-label study, CS7 and received olezarsen 80 mg Q4W. At the qualification visit, hemoglobin was 11.9 g/dL (12.0-16.0 g/dL), platelet count was 205 x109/L On Study Day 155, the subject was hospitalized due to abdominal pain, bloody vomiting (~30 to 40 times), and diarrhea, but no melena. Abnormal test results included: MCV of 78 fL, red blood cells of 5.37 x 1012/L, RDW of 19.5%, protein of 87 g/L, ALT of 103 U/L, AST of 95 U/L, gamma-glutamyl transferase (GGT) of 41 U/L, and high sensitivity C-reactive protein of 9.3 mg/L (no reference ranges, hemoglobin, or platelet count provided). On Day 162, hemoglobin was 10.7 g/dL and platelet levels were within reference range. The primary diagnosis was hematemesis, possibly secondary to a Mallory-Weiss tear from vomiting. The Pancreatitis Adjudication Committee did not adjudicate this event as pancreatitis. The subject was discharged from the hospital and considered recovered on Day 162. No action was taken with olezarsen, and the subject continued to receive olezarsen.
L1157: ## **Reviewer Comment**
L1158: This reviewer concurs with the assessment that hematemesis most likely due to persistent vomiting and unlikely to be related to olezarsen treatment.
L1159: ## Conclusion
L1160: Olezarsen treatment causes reductions in platelet count but there is no evidence of an increased clinical risk of bleeding. The decrease in platelet count occurs within the first weeks of treatment and is characterized by a gradual decline over time. Platelet counts tend to remain on average 5 to 15% below baseline. Overall, the proportion of subjects experiencing a clinical bleeding AE, defined as those not occurring at the injection site or associated with a laboratory term, were similar across treatment groups. No olezarsen-treated subjects, to date, have reported a platelet count below 50 x109/L. There were two serious clinical bleeding events in olezarsen-treated subjects in the OLE studies, CS13 and CS7. Although temporally associated with olezarsen treatment, it is this reviewer's assessment that concomitant medications and/or underlying comorbidities offer a plausible alternate etiology, especially given continued treatment with olezarsen in these two subjects without further bleeding events.
L1161: ## 7.7.3. Increases in Liver Enzymes
L1162: ## Issue
L1163: Serious hepatic AEs in subjects treated with olezarsen were observed. Mean increases in liver enzymes associated with olezarsen treatment and cases of categorical increases in liver enzymes greater than the ULN in olezarsen-treated subjects were also observed.
L1164: ## Background
L1165: Risk of hepatotoxicity with elevation of ALT and AST and hepatic steatosis has been indicated in mipomersen and inotersen, which are ASOs targeting hepatic gene expression. Inotersen labeling recommends baseline and monthly monitoring of liver enzymes (Severi et al. 2023; Akcea Therapeutics 2024a). Vupanorsen, an ASO targeting ANGPTL3, which is a key regulator in TG metabolism, caused a dose-dependent increase in hepatic fat fraction (Zimerman et al. 2024). In contrast, volanesorsen, in clinical trials of different patient populations of HTG including FCS, reduced hepatic fat fraction by 24 to 53% placebo-corrected from baseline (Prohaska et al. 2023). Olezarsen includes the Gal-NAc receptor which targets it to the liver, its therapeutic site for its mechanism of action. The olezarsen clinical development program for the FCS treatment indication did not include assessments of hepatic fat.
L1166: ## Assessment
L1167: ## Adverse Events
L1168: TEAEs related to abnormal hepatic function were evaluated by the following queries – Drug Related Hepatic Disorders Comprehensive Search SMQ (narrow and broad), Hepatic Injury FMQ (narrow) and Hepatic Failure FMQ (narrow), and review of the hepatobiliary and investigations SOC. There were no events related to Hepatic Failure FMQ (narrow) in any of the trials (including the open-label extension studies). The Hepatic Injury FMQ and Drug Related Hepatic Disorders SMQ, yielded similar results given the significant overlap between the terms comprising each query. Table 71 lists the adverse terms related to hepatic function in Trials CS3 and CS8.
L1169: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L1170: There was one SAE of ‘hepatic cirrhosis’ in an olezarsen-treated subject and two SAEs, ‘gastric varices’ and ‘gastric varices hemorrhage’, in a placebo-treated subject in Trial CS3. One SAE of ‘cholangitis’ was reported in an olezarsen-treated subject in CS13. The subject with cholangitis has been discussed previously (Subject ID [REDACTED](b)(6)) in the context of an SAE of lower gastrointestinal hemorrhage. Cholangitis appeared secondary to complications related to pancreatitis and subsequent biliary stents and drains.
L1171: Two olezarsen-treated subjects (CS8-[REDACTED](b)(6); CS8-[REDACTED](b)(6)) in CS8 discontinued treatment due to a hepatic-related AE ‘liver function enzyme increased’ and ‘ALT increased’. There were additional subjects that had their study drug interrupted in CS8 due to liver enzyme increases. There were no hepatic-related SAEs in CS8. Brief summaries of the SAE of ‘cirrhosis’ and discontinuations due to AE are summarized below.

L1180: - Subject # [REDACTED](b)(6) Trial CS3 ‘cirrhosis’ SAE: 71-year-old man with FCS previously treated with volanesorsen for 2 years (last dose [REDACTED](b)(6)), with a history of hepatic steatosis ([REDACTED](b)(6)) randomized to treatment of 80 mg olezarsen experienced TEAE of hepatic cirrhosis on Study Day 295 after the 10th dose of olezarsen (Week 41). The subject reported “dealing with edema and abdominal distension over the past year”. The subject was
L1181: sent to the Emergency Department; the subject was considered hemodynamically stable. Total bilirubin and international normalized ratio (INR) values were slightly above normal 1.7 mg/dL (reference range 0.2-1.3 mg/dL), and 1.7 (reference range 0.8-1.1), respectively. Hemoglobin and platelet count values were below normal at 10.7 g/dL (reference range 14-18 g/dL) and 106 x 109/L (reference range 150-400 x 109/L). Upper gastrointestinal (GI) endoscopy noted esophageal varices and four bands were placed to deflate the varices. The subject was discharged from the hospital the next day. The subject continued to take olezarsen and is currently enrolled in CS13. ALT and AST remain in normal range. Direct bilirubin levels have ranged mostly within the normal range – no values have been 2x above the ULN.
L1182: ## **Reviewer Comment**
L1183: Underlying comorbidity of hepatic steatosis confounds an assessment of causality with olezarsen.
L1184: - Subject #CS8-[REDACTED](b)(6) Trial CS8 'liver function test increased' DAE: 73-year-old woman with HTG at risk for ASCVD, who was randomized to olezarsen 50 mg Q4W. Baseline ALT and AST were 23 U/L and 27 U/L, respectively (reference range ALT 10-53 U/L; AST 14-43 U/L) On Study Day 89, the subject received her 4th dose of olezarsen. Laboratory values on that day were notable for ALT and AST &gt;3x ULN (ALT 199 U/L, AST 134 U/L) leading to an AE of 'liver function test increased'. On Study Day 94 she reported 'fatigue'. As a result of these events, study drug administration was paused per stopping rules for elevated liver enzymes and symptoms, and ultimately led to the subject permanently discontinuing study drug. The subject's ALT and AST peaked at 352 U/L and 240 U/L on Study Day 117, respectively. Bilirubin and alkaline phosphatase remained within normal limits. The ALT and AST returned toward normal range over the next 2 months while off study drug. The last laboratory values for ALT and AST were 53 U/L and 44 U/L on Study Day 145.

L1273: ## **Consultation with the Division of Hepatology and Nutrition (DHN) and the Division of Applied Regulatory Science (DARS)**
L1274: Consultation with DHN and subsequently DARS was sought to further explore these hepatic transaminase increases. DARS concluded that there was (1) no suggestion of olezarsen on-target or non-specific off-target sequence effects on mRNAs in the liver that may be related to hepatic/hepatobiliary dysfunction and toxicity, (2) the olezarsen (b) (4)-linker is unlikely to be directly associated with hepatobiliary injury, (3) clinical data has shown that APOC3 inhibition by volanesorsen resulted in reduced hepatic fat fraction (HFF) in patients with diverse etiologies of severe hypertriglyceridemia (hypertriglyceridemia, familial partial lipodystrophy, and FCS), and (4) there is no evidence linking high uptake of GalNAc-conjugated-ASOs and slow/delayed release from hepatic endosomes with changes in liver function/injury or whether ASO on-target effects on the lipodome can influence endosomal function or endosomal-lysozyme systems in hepatic cells.
L1275: However, the relationship between APOC3 and liver steatosis or metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD) is conflicting across different studies. Information on the impact of chronic administration of olezarsen in subjects with impaired liver function is not available and has not been assessed for volanesorsen (which is an approved FCS therapy outside of the US). DARS suggests that because olezarsen and volanesorsen differ only in the (b) (4) GalNAc3-conjugate, an exploratory comparison of these ASOs in a suitable in vitro cell system (e.g., hepatic microphysiological systems, 3D-spheroids, and/or organ-on-chip approaches) may provide further information on the relationship between potential liver changes or injury and endosomal trafficking and release, and reduced APOC3-mediated changes in the transcriptome and lipodome following ASO degradation. The review team is considering this as a possible post marketing commitment.
L1276: The DHN consult, in DARRTS dated December 16, 2024, concludes that the concerning adverse events of hepatic failure or cirrhosis that occurred in FCS patients do not appear to be directly attributable to olezarsen. However, the consequence of lifetime treatment with olezarsen in FCS is unknown, particularly in those patients with underlying liver disease. DHN recommends considering a PMR or PMC assessing the potential for drug induced liver injury with Tryngolza with evaluation including baseline and periodic laboratory tests and hepatic imaging over 3 to 5 years. Description of these modest aminotransferase elevations should be included in labelling.
L1277: ## **Conclusion**
L1278: In general, the incidence of hepatic events was low and similar across treatment groups. However, olezarsen was associated with average increases in ALT, AST, GGT within the first 3 months which stabilized, and remained within reference range. There were a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects in whom serious hepatic events occurred and increases in liver enzymes led to discontinuation or interruption of study drug. Liver enzymes returned towards baseline with discontinuation of olezarsen. There were no cases of Hy's Law, an indicator of
L1279: drug-induced liver injury, noted with olezarsen treatment. However, to evaluate further this potential for chronic drug-induced liver injury, we plan longer term follow-up with laboratory tests and hepatic imaging in patients with severe hypertriglyceridemia in dedicated clinical trials along with enhanced pharmacovigilance for hepatic toxicity events in patients with FCS.
L1280: ## 7.7.4. Dysglycemia
L1281: ## Issue
L1282: Treatment with olezarsen did not have consistent salutary effects on glycemic control parameters in clinical trials.
L1283: ## Background
L1284: Volanesorsen showed a worsening in glycemic control in clinical studies. In a clinical trial of subjects with multifactorial chylomicronemia, in those with type 2 diabetes, the mean percent changes from baseline in HbA1c were 0.4% in the volanesorsen group and -0.2% in the placebo group; these were statistically significant (P=0.0301) (Gouni-Berthold et al. 2021). Per the authors of this trial, a mildly elevated baseline HbA1c in both groups and an imbalance in baseline antidiabetic therapy with 70% of subjects in the placebo group being on insulin compared to 27% of patients in the volanesorsen group, may have contributed to the observed differences. However, it is not reassuring that another ASO that also targets ApoC-III reported worsening glycemic control with drug therapy (Gaudet et al. 2024).
L1285: ## Assessment
L1286: In Trial CS3, average glucose and HbA1c values increased more in the olezarsen treatment groups than placebo. At baseline, mean glucose values were approximately 91 mg/dL, 96 mg/dL, and 103 mg/dL in the olezarsen 50 mg, olezarsen 80 mg, and placebo groups, respectively. At Week 53, there was an approximately 20% increase in the average glucose value in the olezarsen treatment groups (absolute mean change from baseline of 15 mg/dL in the olezarsen 50 mg group and 18 mg/dL in the olezarsen 80 mg group compared to 13% (4.5 mg/dL) increase in the placebo group (see Figure 24).
L1287: In Trial CS3, the mean HbA1c at baseline was 5.6%, 5.6%, and 5.8% in the olezarsen 50 mg, olezarsen 80 mg, and placebo treatment groups. Over time, the olezarsen treatment groups had higher average HbA1c values compared to the placebo group. At Week 53, the olezarsen 50 mg group was 0.19 percentage points higher, the olezarsen 80 mg group was 0.14 percentage points higher, and the placebo group was 0.08 percentage points higher (see Figure 25).

L1303: To further assess the effect of olezarsen on glucose metabolism, a hyperglycemia algorithmic FMQ was evaluated for CS3 and CS8 overall and by medical history of diabetes (see Table 74).
L1304: In CS3, overall and by diabetic status more olezarsen-treated subjects fulfilled at least one of the hyperglycemia criteria compared to placebo-treated subjects. In subjects with no history of diabetes, the proportion of subjects fulfilling \ge 1 FMQ criterion was greater in both the olezarsen 50 mg (50%) and olezarsen 80 mg (53.3%) groups compared with the placebo group (35.3%). The most fulfilled criteria in this subgroup included “change from baseline fasting glucose \ge 20 mg/dL with postbaseline fasting glucose &gt; 100 mg/dL” (44.4%, 53.3%, and 23.5% in the olezarsen 50 mg, olezarsen 80 mg, and placebo groups, respectively) and “HbA1c Increase \ge 0.3\% with Post-Baseline HbA1c \ge 5.7\%” (27.8%, 26.7%, and 11.8%, respectively).
L1305: In CS8, a less consistent pattern was observed. In subjects without a history of diabetes, proportionally fewer subjects fulfilled \ge 1 FMQ criterion in the olezarsen 80 mg group (31.6%) compared with the olezarsen 50 mg (57.1%) and the placebo (55.6%) group.
L1306: It is uncertain whether the differences in degree of HTG or proportion of subjects with type 2 diabetes at baseline impacts the effect of olezarsen on glucose control and contributes to the differences observed between Trials CS3 and CS8.

L1345: ## **Conclusion**
L1346: Small increases in average values in fasting glucose and HbA1c over time were observed with olezarsen treatment in the FCS population. In an ad-hoc analysis using an algorithmic FMQ for hyperglycemia consisting of AEs, new antidiabetic medication, and laboratory values, a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects without a medical history of diabetes at baseline versus placebo-treated subjects fulfilled at least one of the FMQ criteria, suggesting a safety signal for worsening glycemic control. However, it is uncertain whether this represents a true risk with olezarsen treatment. Furthermore, glucose metabolism is monitorable and treatable, and these factors should be considered when evaluating the benefits and risks associated with olezarsen.
L1347: ## **7.7.5. Injection Site Reactions**
L1348: ## **Issue**
L1349: Treatment with olezarsen is associated with ISRs.
L1350: ## **Background**
L1351: Subcutaneously administered oligonucleotides are associated with localized reactions around the injection site, such as erythema, itching, and pain, and less commonly severe ulceration and necrosis. The reported incidence of ISRs in various oligonucleotide clinical trials ranged from 22% to 100% (van Meer et al. 2016).
L1352: ## **Assessment**
L1353: An injection site reaction was defined as a TEAE with a preferred term or verbatim term containing 'injection site' that persisted for at least 2 days (i.e., event resolution date not on the day of the event onset date or the day after) or was ongoing. A separate definition was also used to describe a more severe reaction defined as local cutaneous reaction at the injection site (LCRIS), which was defined as: (A) moderate or severe AEs which included the preferred terms of injection site erythema, injection site swelling, injection site pruritus, or injection site pain that started on the day of injection, persisted for at least 2 days or were ongoing; or (B) any AE at the study drug injection site, regardless of severity, that led to discontinuation of study drug, where the AE at the study drug injection site was the principal reason for discontinuation or if the AE at the injection site led to discontinuation of study drug. The severity of AEs was graded based on the Toxicity Grading Scale for Healthy Adult and Adolescent Volunteers Enrolled in Preventive Vaccine Clinical Trials, which rates injection site reactions based on pain, tenderness, size of erythema/redness, and size of induration/swelling.
L1354: Using the ISR definition, in Trial CS3, a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects reported an ISR compared to placebo-treated subjects (see Table 81). None of the ISRs were serious and no subjects reduced study drug, withdrew from the trial, or required treatment of the ISR. The median number of injections received before the first ISR was 2 in the olezarsen 50 mg group, 3 in the olezarsen 80 mg group, and 3 in the placebo group. The median duration of the ISR was 42 days in the 50 mg olezarsen group, 6 days in the 80 mg olezarsen group, and 133 days in the placebo group. Two of the eight subjects' injection site reactions (one placebo 'IS discoloration', and one olezarsen 50 mg 'IS erythema') had not recovered or resolved at the data cut-off.
L1355: There were no events in Trial CS3 that met the definition of an LCRIS.
L1356: In the OLE study, CS13, five subjects previously treated with placebo who started olezarsen reported 40 ISR events. One subject in the olezarsen-naïve treatment group reported 33 of these events. For this individual (# [b] (6)), the events were ‘injection site discoloration’ that was reported as not resolved, ‘injection site erythema’, ‘injection site swelling’, and ‘injection site hematoma’. This study subject also experienced a TEAE of hepatic enzyme increased that led to permanent discontinuation of study drug. The study site noted that the ISR discoloration event had resolved 1 month after the last follow-up post-treatment visit.
L1357: Individuals who had been treated with olezarsen in the index trial and continued with olezarsen in the open-label extension, reported fewer events – five olezarsen-treated subjects in CS3 reported seven events in CS13. No events were reported as serious, none met the LCRIS definition, and no subjects discontinued treatment due an ISR.

L1370: In the open-label study, CS7, four (16.7%) subjects reported 56 ISR events, 32 of these were ‘injection site erythema’ and 14 were ‘injection site discoloration’. Of these four subjects, one reported injection sites of moderate severity, thus qualifying as an LCRIS. This subject (# (b) (6)) reported ISRs starting with the first dose and subsequent doses. The day of the 8th dose of study drug, the subject reported injection site erythema, swelling, and pain of moderate severity. No action was taken with regard to study drug, and 4 days later the events were recovered/resolved. The subject continued to report ISRs, for example on Study Days 226, 254, 282, and 308 (last day before data cutoff) the subject reported ‘injection site discoloration’ while continuing olezarsen treatment. In CS7, the mean duration of injection site discoloration was 261 days.
L1371: ## Conclusion
L1372: Injection site reactions occur with olezarsen treatment and are characterized primarily by erythema and discoloration. The median number of injections before the first ISR ranged from 1 to 3. In the FCS controlled trial, CS3, the number of injection reaction site events was small (total events in olezarsen-treated [50 and 80 mg combined] group was 9 in 6 [14%] subjects). This may be an underestimation of the number of events given that in the open-label studies of FCS, 10 (19%) subjects in CS13 reported a total of 47 events, and in CS7, 4 (17%) subjects reported 56 events. None of the injection site reactions were serious, and no subjects in FCS trials reduced study drug, withdrew from the trial, or required treatment of the ISR. The majority of injection site reactions resolved, however the duration of ISRs of discoloration and erythema was variable (several days to months before resolution). The incidence of injection site reactions will be described in section 6.1 of the PI.
L1373: ## 7.7.6. Increase in LDL-C
L1374: ## Issue
L1375: In the FCS trial, CS3, treatment with olezarsen was associated with increases in LDL-C and total ApoB. These directional shifts are conventionally considered adverse in assessments of
L1376: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L1377: cardiovascular risk. The clinical impact of olezarsen's effect on lipids in this population is uncertain.
L1378: ## Background
L1379: Several drug classes induce lipid abnormalities including anti-hypertensives (thiazide diuretics [increase cholesterol and TG], certain beta blockers [increase TG, decrease HDL-C]), immunosuppressants (cyclosporine and tacrolimus), protease inhibitors (increase LDL-C), antipsychotic drugs (increase TG), SGTL2 inhibitors (increase LDL-C), and fish oil derived prescription products (increase LDL-C). Volanesorsen in clinical trials increases LDL-C. This may be due to increased lipolysis of TG, although the mechanism has not been conclusively determined.
L1380: ## Assessment
L1381: In CS3, treatment with olezarsen resulted in elevations in LDL-C. At Month 12, the least square mean percent change in LDL-C from baseline increased in the olezarsen 50 mg and olezarsen 80 mg groups by 53% and 47%, respectively versus -0.1% in the placebo group. However, because individuals with FCS have very low LDL-C at baseline, the mean LDL-C values at Month 12 were 27 mg/dL, 29 mg/dL and 16 mg/dL in the olezarsen 50 mg, olezarsen 80 mg, and placebo groups, respectively (see Figure 28).

L1387: All subjects at baseline had an LDL-C &lt;70 mg/dL. No placebo-treated subjects had a postbaseline LDL-C over 70 mg/dL compared to four (19%) olezarsen 50 mg treated subjects and six (27%) olezarsen 80 mg treated subjects. No subject in the olezarsen 50 mg group, but three subjects in the olezarsen 80 mg group experienced an adverse shift of \ge 2 severity categories in LDL-C; two subjects shifted from below 70 mg/dL to the LDL-C category of 100 to &lt;130 mg/dL and one subject shifted from below to 70 mg/dL to the LDL-C category of 130 to &lt;160 mg/dL. These subjects were not on LDL-C lowering medications (e.g., statins) before the trial and they were not started on LDL-C lowering medications for these elevations. LDL-C values in these subjects trended down over time but remained above baseline values.
L1388: ApoB also increased with olezarsen treatment. At Month 12, a 0.6% least squares (LS) mean change from baseline was noted in the placebo group, and a 1.6% and 7.8% increase from baseline in the olezarsen 50 mg and olezarsen 80 mg groups, respectively. A higher percentage of subjects in the olezarsen treatment groups exceeded the ApoB threshold of 130 mg/dL at any time postbaseline (10% olezarsen 50 mg; 38% olezarsen 80 mg; 4% placebo).

L1402: In Trial CS7, 22 out of 24 subjects at baseline had LDL-C below 70 mg/dL, and 4 subjects shifted to a higher LDL-C category. One (4.2%) subject had a shift from &lt;70 mg/dL at baseline to 130 to &lt;160 mg/dL postbaseline, 1 (4.2%) subject had a shift from &lt;70 mg/dL to 160 to &lt;190 mg/dL postbaseline, and 1 (4.2%) subject had a shift from 100 mg to &lt;130 /dL to 160 to &lt;190 mg/dL postbaseline – this last subject was started on rosuvastatin 10 mg and ezetimibe 10 mg during the study.
L1403: ## Conclusion
L1404: LS mean increases in LDL-C and ApoB of approximately 50% and 8% from baseline, respectively, were noted in the FCS population treated with olezarsen 80 mg compared to a &lt;1% LS mean increase in those treated with placebo. In contrast, in Trial CS8 of subjects with moderate to high levels of TG, LDL-C increases were smaller and similar across treatment groups, notably ApoB had a moderate decrease. It is uncertain, if the difference in LDL-C changes between the two trials is related to the degree of TG elevation at baseline and resulting lipid metabolism following ApoC-III inhibition. The clinical impact of these changes in the FCS population is uncertain, in part because despite increases in LDL-C, the value remains low (i.e., &lt;70 mg/dL) for most study subjects. LDL-C is monitorable and could be mitigated with lipid-modifying therapy.
L1405: ## 7.7.7. Imbalance in Cardiovascular Events
L1406: ## Issue
L1407: In the phase 2 trial, CS2, a higher number of serious cardiac disorders were observed in the olezarsen treatment group compared to the placebo group in a non-FCS population with moderately elevated TG at high risk for CVD. The effect of olezarsen on cardiovascular risk is unknown.
L1408: ## Background
L1409: Genetic studies of loss of function variants in the *apoC-III* gene showed a decrease in TG levels and risk of atherosclerotic disease (Tg et al. 2014; Wulff et al. 2018). This observation has made ApoC-III a pharmaceutical target for reduction of cardiovascular risk. However, it is unknown whether a pharmacologic reduction in ApoC-III will translate into a clinical benefit of CV risk reduction. Recent trials that have focused on lipid endpoints other than LDL-C illustrate the limitations of lipid parameters as surrogates. A notable recent example is the PROMINENT trial, a cardiovascular outcome trial of pemafibrate, in statin-treated subjects with type 2 diabetes and moderate HTG which failed to show a cardiovascular benefit despite a placebo-adjusted 26% reduction in TG, and placebo-subtracted decreases in ApoC-III of 28%, but not ApoB (4.8%) (Das Pradhan et al. 2022).
L1410: ## Assessment
L1411: In CS3, CS8, CS7, and CS13 all AEs and SAEs that were consistent with a MACE were adjudicated by a blinded, independent committee. Trial CS2, an early phase study of olezarsen did not have a MACE adjudication committee.
L1412: In Trial CS2, a dose-finding study of olezarsen (10 mg to 50 mg) in subjects with mixed dyslipidemia there was a higher incidence of cardiac disorders in the pooled olezarsen group (13.3%) compared to the placebo group (4.2%), including a fatal cardiac arrest in the 15 mg every 2 weeks group. Serious cardiovascular events also occurred in a higher proportion of olezarsen-treated subjects (n=6 [6.7%]) compared to placebo-treated subjects (n=1 [4.2%]). A listing of the cardiovascular SAEs is listed below. Review of the narratives for these subjects was notable for a significant medical history of CVD which confounds a causality assessment.

L1445: ## Conclusion
L1446: In the olezarsen clinical development program there was a higher number of deaths, serious cardiac events, and adjudicated MACE with olezarsen treatment compared to placebo treatment. There were no deaths or MACE in the placebo treatment groups. Most of the serious cardiac events and MACE occurred in a non-FCS population with underlying cardiovascular comorbidities. It is this reviewer's opinion that the number of MACE and duration of exposure is too small to provide a definitive causality assessment for olezarsen. Ongoing larger studies in subjects without FCS with either sHTG or moderate HTG at risk for or with established ASCVD may provide further information to inform this safety review issue. At this time there is no clear signal of cardiovascular harm in the FCS population, and the small imbalance in serious cardiac events associated with olezarsen treatment in the non-FCS population is likely the play of chance.
L1447: ## 7.7.8. Immunogenicity
L1448: ## Issue
L1449: Treatment with olezarsen results in the development of ADA, therefore an immunogenicity assessment to evaluate the potential impacts of olezarsen ADA on safety is a review issue.
L1450: ## Assessment
L1451: The immunogenicity of olezarsen was evaluated in all clinical trials. In CS3 and CS8, ADA were collected at baseline (Day 1 pre-dose), Weeks 2, 5, 9 (CS3 only), 13, 25, 37, 53, and during the follow-up period (Weeks 4, 8, 13). Subjects who experienced persistent or increasing constitutional symptoms were to have additional testing for ADA status.
L1452: Subjects were given 'positive' ADA status if they had at least one confirmed positive sample at any time during the treatment or post-treatment evaluation periods. Subjects were given 'negative' ADA status if all evaluated immunogenicity sample results during the treatment and post-treatment evaluation periods were negative, and they had at least one evaluable immunogenicity sample postdose. Otherwise, study subjects were given 'unknown' ADA status.
L1453: In CS3, 24 (36.4%) of the 66 subjects at baseline were positive for anti-olezarsen ADA across treatment groups. The placebo group had the highest percentage of subjects positive for ADA at baseline (43.5%) compared to the olezarsen 50 mg (38.1%) and olezarsen 80 mg (27.3%) groups. Of note, 26 out of the 66 subjects had been previously treated with volanesorsen. Of those previously treated with volanesorsen, 14 (53.8%) were positive for ADA at baseline in Trial CS3.
L1454: No placebo-treated subjects developed treatment-emergent ADA compared to 38.1% of subjects in the olezarsen 50 mg group and 45.5% of subjects in the olezarsen 80 mg group. All subjects with induced treatment-emergent ADA had a persistent response. Of the 42 subjects who were ADA negative at baseline, 29 (69%) remained negative throughout the study. The median time to onset of treatment-emergent ADAs were similar in treatment groups that received olezarsen (30.0 days and 29.5 days in the olezarsen 80 mg group and olezarsen 50 mg group, respectively). The median time to peak titer was greater in the olezarsen 80 mg group than in the olezarsen
L1455: NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L1456: 50 mg group (218.5 days vs. 29.5 days, respectively). The median (range) peak titer was 200 (50, 25,600) in the olezarsen 80 mg group, and 75 (50, 25,600) in the olezarsen 50 mg group.
L1457: In the OLE study, CS13, treatment-emergent ADAs were detected in 30%, 52.9%, and 88.2% of subjects for the CS3-placebo, CS3-olezarsen 50 mg, and CS3-olezarsen 80 mg groups, respectively, suggesting the incidence of treatment-emergent ADA may increase with treatment duration. At least one ISR was experienced by 26.7% of patients with treatment-emergent ADAs in comparison with 6.3% and 12.5% of patients with negative ADA status and treatment-unaffected ADA, respectively. Hypersensitivity events were only experienced by patients with treatment-emergent ADAs (23.3%). Two subjects (CS3 [REDACTED](b)(6) and CS3 [REDACTED](b)(6)) experienced serious hypersensitivity events in CS13 and also had high ADA titers. See Section 7.7.1 for narratives of these subjects.
L1458: In Trial CS8, more olezarsen-treated subjects developed treatment-emergent ADA compared to the placebo group (olezarsen 50 mg 41.4%; olezarsen 80 mg 40.4%; placebo 12.8%). Of the 125 subjects that were negative/unknown for ADA at baseline, 84 (67%) remained negative. In subjects who developed treatment-emergent ADAs, the median time to onset was 83 days in the olezarsen 50 mg group, 29 days in the olezarsen 80 mg group, and 169 days in the placebo group. The median duration for treatment-emergent ADA was 298 days, 281, and 84 days, in the olezarsen 50 mg, olezarsen 80 mg, and placebo groups, respectively.
L1459: Although there did not seem to be a clear association with ADA and AEs in either Trial CS3 or CS8, in the OLE CS13 study, there was a higher proportion of subjects with ADA that had ISRs and hypersensitivity events.
L1460: ## Conclusion
L1461: Treatment with olezarsen results in development of treatment-emergent ADA in approximately 40% of treated subjects. The incidence of ADA may increase with longer drug exposure as demonstrated in subjects that enrolled in the OLE study, CS13. Review of immunogenicity data from events during CS3 and CS8 did not demonstrate a clear relationship between AEs, including hypersensitivity events, and ADA status. In CS13, however, ISRs and hypersensitivity events were more frequent in subjects with treatment-emergent ADAs compared to subjects with ADA negative or treatment-unaffected status, although the numbers were small and therefore limit definitive causality assessments.
L1462: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L1463: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L1464: ## Renal Impairment
L1465: No dedicated renal impairment study was conducted for olezarsen. The ultimate elimination of unconjugated olezarsen is likely a combination of slow nuclease metabolism within tissues and subsequent excretion of shortened metabolites (as well as any remaining full-length oligonucleotide) into urine.
L1466: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L1467: Clinical studies conducted in this program enrolled participants with normal renal function as well as those with mild to moderate renal impairment. The range of post hoc estimates of steady-state exposure, including C_{max}, area under the concentration-time curve (AUC), and C_{trough}, were overlapping between participants with normal renal function (N=221) and participants with mild (N=48) or moderate (N=3) renal impairment (Figure 32). Consistent with PK exposure metrics, the range of post hoc estimates of steady-state percent change from baseline for ApoC-III and TG were overlapping between participants with normal renal function (N=219) and participants with mild (N=48) or moderate (N=3) renal impairment (Figure 33 and Figure 34).
L1468: The review team agrees with the Applicant's labeling recommendation that no dose adjustment is needed in patients with mild or moderate renal impairment (\ge 30 mL/min).

L1481: ## **Hepatic Impairment**
L1482: No dedicated hepatic impairment study was conducted for olezarsen. The Applicant has proposed the following language for use of olezarsen in patients with hepatic impairment (HI):
L1483: Trials for olezarsen enrolled sixteen participants with mild HI based on the National Cancer Institute Organ Dysfunction Working Group criteria. Two subjects were from the healthy subject single ascending dose/multiple ascending dose study (ISIS 678354-CS1), one subject from healthy Japanese subject single ascending dose/multiple ascending dose study (ISIS AKCEA-CS1), six patients from dose-ranging study in patients with HTG and established or at high risk for CVD (ISIS 678354-CS2), four patients from confirmatory efficacy phase 2b study in patients with HTG (ISIS 678354-CS8) and three patients were from phase 3 study in patients with FCS (ISIS 678354-CS3 or Balance). One subject with moderate HI was enrolled in healthy subject study AKCEA-CS1. No patients with moderate or severe HI were enrolled in the studies.
L1484: The population PK analysis did not find baseline AST, ALT, or bilirubin as statistically significant covariates impacting olezarsen PK. Furthermore, the range of post hoc estimates of steady-state exposure, including C_{max}, AUC, and C_{trough}, were overlapping between participants with normal hepatic function (N=332) and participants with mild (N=16) or moderate (N=1) HI (based on National Cancer Institute Organ Dysfunction Working Group classification category) (Figure 35). Consistent with this, steady-state ApoC-III and TG reduction were similar between participants with normal hepatic function (N=330) and participants with mild (N=16) or moderate (N=1) HI (Figure 36 &amp; Figure 37).
L1485: The review team recommends including the following labeling language:
L1486: *"No dose adjustment is (b) (4) in patients with mild hepatic impairment.
TRYNGOLZA has not been studied in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment".*

L1499: ## **Race**
L1500: Japanese origin (from Study AKCEA-CS1) was identified as a statistically significant covariate on Vc/F in the population PK analysis. However, there were no clinically meaningful differences in olezarsen exposure metrics among categories of race (White, Black or African American, Asian, Japanese [from Study AKCEA-CS1], American Indian or Alaska Native, Native Hawaiian or Pacific Islander, Other or Unknown), given that exposures are still within the overall range of exposures seen in the population. The review team agrees that no dose adjustment is warranted on the basis of race.
L1501: ## **Age and Sex**
L1502: Age (adults aged &lt;65 years [N=223], 65 to 74 years [N=41], and \ge 75 years [N=8]) and sex were not identified as a statistically significant covariate in the population PK analysis. Slight differences in median C_{max,ss} and AUC_{\tau,ss} are attributable to differences in body weight between male (median body weight 86.2 kg) and female participants (median body weight of 70.6 kg) and are not considered clinically meaningful, given that exposures are still within the overall range of exposures seen in the population. Therefore, no dose adjustment is required on the basis of age and sex.
L1503: ## **Body Weight**
L1504: The population PK analysis identified body weight as a statistically significant covariate and as the most influential factor on olezarsen PK. Increased body weight was associated with increased apparent clearance (CL/F), Q/F, Vc/F, and Vp/F and k_a. Compared with a reference subject with a body weight of 70 kg, the median olezarsen exposure (AUC_{\tau,ss}, C_{max,ss}, C_{trough,ss}) for a participant at the 10th percentile of the body weight distribution, i.e., one with a weight of 61 kg, is expected to have a 16%, 28%, and 9% increase, respectively. A participant at the 90th percentile of the body weight distribution, i.e., one with a weight of 105 kg, is expected to have a 35% lower AUC_{\tau,ss}, 55% lower C_{max,ss}, and 23% lower C_{trough,ss} compared with a reference subject with weight of 70 kg. It is noted that the differences in C_{trough,ss} between participants at the 10th and 90th percentiles of the body weight distribution were found to be smaller than the differences in C_{max,ss} and AUC_{\tau,ss} for these participants.
L1505: A comparison of individual exposure metrics by body weight quartile was consistent with the magnitudes of differences found for the median exposure (Figure 38). While there was a trend of declining exposure with increasing body weight for C_{max,ss} and AUC_{\tau,ss}, no such apparent trend was identified for C_{trough,ss}. Consistent with the C_{trough,ss}, steady-state ApoC-III and TG reductions (percent change-from-baseline) were similar across different body weight groups.
L1506: The impact of body weight on olezarsen PK was, therefore, not considered clinically meaningful, and a fixed dose of 80 mg monthly is appropriate, regardless of body weight.
L1507: Figure 38. Post Hoc Pharmacokinetic Metrics at Steady State vs. Covariates: Body Weight
L1508: Source: Figure 171 of 678354-ppk02-study-report.
The horizontal dashed lines represent the median of the data across all categories.

L1509: ## 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L1510: ## Drug Interactions
L1511: The drug-drug interaction potential of olezarsen involving cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzymes and human transporter proteins was investigated in vitro. Based on the in vitro evaluation, no clinically relevant drug-drug interactions are expected for olezarsen, and no drug-drug interaction-based dose adjustment is necessary.
L1512: ## **Interaction With Highly Plasma Protein-Bound Drugs**
L1513: No drug-drug interaction was observed in vitro with regard to plasma protein-binding displacement between olezarsen and highly plasma protein-bound drugs, warfarin, and ibuprofen in vitro, and thus olezarsen is not expected to have drug-drug interactions with highly plasma-bound drugs.
L1514: ## **Metabolism and Transporter-Based Drug-Drug Interaction**
L1515: Oligonucleotides like olezarsen are not substrates for the classic CYP metabolic pathways and thus their PK are unlikely to be impacted by other drugs that are CYP inhibitors or inducers. Olezarsen did not inhibit major CYP isoforms (CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1, or CYP3A4). Olezarsen up to 10 µM did not induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4. At 10 and 100 µM, olezarsen was not a substrate or inhibitor, respectively, of OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, bile salt export pump (BSEP), breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), or P-gp. It is unlikely that olezarsen would impact the PK of compounds that are substrates of CYPs or transporters.
L1516: ## **8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development**
L1517: Olezarsen was granted orphan drug designation for the FCS indication. The Applicant was exempt from the Pediatric Research Equity Act requirement. Olezarsen has not been studied in pediatric patients. The Applicant has an agreed Pediatric Investigation Plan with the European Medicine Agency that is deferred (b) (4) to determine pediatric dosing (EMA 2023).
L1518: ## **8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential**
L1519: The Applicant conducted a fertility and early embryonic development study in male and female mice with olezarsen. The Agency agreed that embryofetal development and pre-/postnatal development evaluations conducted with the unconjugated active ASO component of olezarsen in mice and/or rabbits were adequate to support a marketing application.
L1520: **Table 86** summarizes the outcomes and conclusions of these studies. The identified multiples are based on a comparison of the BSA at the no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) dose converted to a monthly dose relative to the monthly clinical dose of olezarsen, even though for some of the studies it was the unconjugated ASO that was administered. Regardless, as the unconjugated ASO is the active substance for both test articles administered, the comparison is considered acceptable. Refer to Section 13 for further details of the reproductive toxicity studies conducted with the conjugated and unconjugated ASO. Specific labelling recommendations will be addressed separately.

L1522: Labeling Section	Nonclinical Data
8.1 Pregnancy	
Combined fertility and embryo-fetal development study in mice with the unconjugated (lacking GalNAc) ASO.
Unconjugated ASO administered to male and female mice by subcutaneous injection at doses of 3, 10, or 25 mg/kg every other day (10.5, 35, and 87.5 mg/kg/wk) prior to mating and through the completion of organogenesis on gestation day 15
No adverse developmental outcomes were noted at doses up to 87.5 mg/kg/wk (highest dose administered)
Low concentrations of the unconjugated ASO were measurable in placental tissue (<10% of maternal plasma levels) while levels in fetal tissue were below the limit of detection
The NOAEL was 87.5 mg/kg/wk (approximately 21x the MRHD based on BSA comparison of the unconjugated ASO)
Embryo-fetal development study in pregnant rabbits with the unconjugated ASO.
Unconjugated ASO administered by subcutaneous injection at doses of 3, 6, or 15 mg/kg every other day (10.5, 21, and 52.5 mg/kg/wk) during the period of organogenesis on gestation days 6 to 18
A maternally toxic dose of 52.5 mg/kg/wk was associated with abortions and early delivery but was not associated with adverse developmental outcomes
Low concentrations of the unconjugated ASO were measurable in placental tissue (~7% of maternal plasma levels), whereas levels in fetal tissue were below the limit of detection
The NOAEL was mid dose of 21 mg/kg/wk (approximately 20x the MRHD based on BSA comparison of the unconjugated ASO)
Pre- and postnatal study in mice with the unconjugated ASO.
Unconjugated ASO administered to dams by subcutaneous injection at doses of 3, 10, or 25 mg/kg every other day (10.5, 35, and 87.5 mg/kg/wk) from gestation day 6 through lactation day 21 (weaning)
Decreases in offspring body weights were associated with delays in the development of morphological landmarks at 87.5 mg/kg/wk
The maternal NOAEL was the high dose of 87.5 mg/kg/wk (approximately 21x the MRHD based on comparison of the active substance dose). The NOAEL for effects on offspring was the mid dose of 105 mg/kg/wk, (approximately 9x the MRHD based on BSA comparison of the unconjugated ASO).

8.2 Lactation	In milk from lactating mice from the pre- and postnatal toxicity study given subcutaneous doses of the unconjugated ASO at 87.5 mg/kg/wk, very low levels (<0.01% of maternal liver levels) were detected.

13.1 Impairment of Fertility	Olezarsen was administered every other week to males for 14 weeks and every other week to females prior to mating followed by every other day after mating until gestation day 6 at doses of 0, 5, 10, or 20 mg/kg.
No effect on fertility in mice administered olezarsen at doses up to 20 mg/kg every other week (approximately 2 times the clinical dose based on BSA comparison).

L1546: ## 11. Advisory Committee Summary
L1547: An advisory committee meeting was not convened for this application.
L1548: The application for the related drug volanesorsen for treatment of FCS was discussed at a meeting of the Endocrinologic and Metabolic Drugs Advisory Committee on May 10, 2018. The committee voted 12 to 8 in favor of approval.
L1549: The primary safety risk for volanesorsen was severe thrombocytopenia.
L1550: Olezarsen has the same nucleotide sequence and backbone chemical composition as volanesorsen and differs from it only through its inclusion of triantennary N-acetyl galactosamine (GalNAc3), which allows for lower dosing and injection volume than with volanesorsen.
L1551: The safety and effectiveness concerns with volanesorsen have been largely addressed or resolved with the olezarsen product. Olezarsen has a mild signal for thrombocytopenia but not a signal for bleeding. Olezarsen 80 mg statistically significantly reduces TG to a clinically meaningful degree. In Study CS3, for the total olezarsen group (80 mg + 50 mg groups), a decrease in the adjudicated pancreatitis event rate was observed compared with placebo. Safety concerns with olezarsen are monitorable and can be addressed in labeling. These issues do not rise to the level of requiring advisory committee input.
L1552: ## III. Additional Analyses and Information
L1553: ## 12. Summary of Regulatory History
L1554: ISIS 678354 (also referred to as olezarsen) is being developed for treatment of familial chylomicronemia syndrome (FCS).
L1555: ## Notable Regulatory Milestones
L1556: On December 14, 2017, the initial IND 136692 for olezarsen was submitted (b) (4). After review, the trial was determined safe to proceed and FDA issued non-hold clinical and nonclinical comments in an advice/information request letter on January 16, 2018.

On December 22, 2022, a request for Fast Track designation for the treatment of FCS was submitted. On January 19, 2023, the Fast Track designation was granted for olezarsen to reduce triglycerides (TG) in adults with FCS.

On September 13, 2023, a request for review of the proposed proprietary name Tryngolza was submitted to IND 136692. On March 11, 2024, a Proprietary Name Request Conditionally Acceptable letter was issued for Tryngolza.

On January 18, 2024, a request for Breakthrough Therapy designation was submitted for the treatment of FCS. The Breakthrough Therapy designation was granted on February 20, 2024, for use of olezarsen as an adjunct to diet to reduce TG in patients with FCS.

On February 14, 2024, orphan drug designation was granted for olezarsen for treatment of FCS.

On April 19, 2024, the original NDA 218614 for olezarsen was submitted for the proposed indication:

*As an adjunct to diet to reduce triglycerides in adults with FCS.*

On June 18, 2024, the application was filed, and the Filing Communication was issued conveying no filing review issues were identified and the Applicant was notified that the application received priority review.
L1557: ## Milestone Meetings
L1558: On September 1, 2017, the pre-investigational new drug application file 136692 was opened for olezarsen with the submission of a Type B pre-investigational new drug meeting request. The purpose of this meeting was to discuss the adequacy of nonclinical and clinical development plans to support an IND (b) (4). Written Responses were issued on October 31, 2017.

On April 17, 2020, a Type B End-of-Phase 2 (EOP2) meeting was requested to discuss the development plan for treatment of FCS. On April 28, 2020, a Meeting Granted letter was issued informing the Applicant that an EOP2 meeting request was premature and that Type C Written
L1559: Responses would be issued in lieu of a meeting. The May 15, 2020, meeting package contained a protocol for Trial ISIS 678354-CS3, titled, *A Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Phase 3 Study of AKCEA-APOCIII-LRX (ISIS 678354) Administered Subcutaneously to Patients with Familial Chylomicronemia Syndrome (FCS)*. The FDA informed the Applicant that we had several concerns about the adequacy of this program to enter phase 3 development and that we were providing general preliminary comments on the planned phase 3 program and recommendations for obtaining additional data. The Written Responses were issued on July 1, 2020.
L1560: On December 7, 2021, a Type C meeting was requested to discuss the proposed bioequivalence (BE) study (protocol number ISIS 678354-CS20, titled, *A "Single Dose", Randomized, Open-Label, Two-Period Crossover, Bioequivalence Study Comparing Two Subcutaneous Formulations: Vial and Autoinjector (AI) with Olezarsen, at Two Dose Levels, in Healthy Adult Participants*) to transition from the currently used vial drug product to an AI presentation. On December 17, 2021, a Meeting Request Granted Written Responses Only letter was issued. The FDA informed the Applicant that we did not agree on the proposed design for the BE Study ISIS 678354-CS20, we did not agree with the Applicant's rationale for bridging the 50 mg and 80 mg doses, and we did not agree that the overall bridging strategy was adequate to support NDA filing with the AI configuration. The Written Responses also included additional Chemistry, Manufacturing, and Controls, device, and combination product comments from the FDA. The Written Responses were issued on February 16, 2022.
L1561: On April 5, 2022, a Type C meeting was requested to discuss the proposed safety database to support an NDA submission. On April 26, 2022, a teleconference was granted and scheduled for June 13, 2022. The April 26, 2022, Meeting Granted letter contained a request for the background package to include additional information on how the Applicant intended to demonstrate substantial evidence of effectiveness to support an indication in FCS and the overall clinical pharmacology package, including plans regarding assessment of the effect of hepatic impairment, the bridging of the autoinjector, and addressing QT-IRT comments. The Preliminary Comments were issued on July 29, 2022. Relevant issues discussed at the meeting, and reflected in the August 9, 2022, Meeting Minutes, are reflected below:

L1570: ## **Pediatric Research Equity Act**
L1571: Because olezarsen for this indication was granted orphan drug designation, the Applicant was exempt from the Pediatric Research Equity Act requirement.
L1572: (b) (4)
L1573: ## **13. Pharmacology Toxicology**
L1574: Olezarsen (ISIS 678354) and/or volanesorsen (GalNAc-unconjugated form of olezarsen) were characterized in a series of nonclinical pharmacology, pharmacokinetic, and toxicology studies. The pharmacologic activity of olezarsen was evaluated in a series of in vitro and in vivo studies, receptor binding activity was evaluated in a secondary pharmacology study, and safety pharmacology studies were evaluated in cell lines and in cynomolgus monkeys.
L1575: The absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion profile was primarily characterized as a component of the repeat-dose toxicity studies. Additionally, a series of single dose studies were also performed in rats at various ages, in achondroplastic and wild-type mice, and by various routes of administration.
L1576: The toxicity profile was established in a series of in vitro and in vivo studies. Repeat-dose toxicity, fertility and early embryonic development, and genotoxicity were conducted with olezarsen, while carcinogenicity, embryofetal development, and pre-/postnatal development were evaluated in studies with volanesorsen, the unconjugated active antisense oligonucleotide (ASO). The mRNA sequence to which the ASO hybridizes is identical between humans and non-human primates (NHPs) but differs in rodents. For this reason, the Applicant used human *apoC-III*
L1577: transgenic mice to evaluate pharmacologic effects and included groups administered species-specific ASOs in many of the pivotal tox studies conducted in rodents.

L1582: ## 13.3. Review of Studies Supporting the New Drug Application
L1583: ## 13.3.1. Pharmacology
L1584: ## Pharmacology
L1585: The Applicant conducted both in vitro and in vivo studies to demonstrate pharmacological activity with olezarsen. Activity was characterized in vitro using primary hepatocytes (human and transgenic mouse) and in vivo activity was demonstrated in wild-type mice, transgenic mice expressing human *apoC-III*, and in NHPs (cynomolgus monkeys). Results from the in vitro pharmacology studies demonstrated that olezarsen reduces *apoC-III* mRNA up to 89% compared to untreated controls. Results from the in vivo studies demonstrated dose- and time-dependent reductions of liver *apoC-III* mRNA, plasma ApoC-III protein and plasma TG levels. These effects were consistent with the expected pharmacological properties of the compound. Results from these animal models showed reasonable proof-of-concept and included a clinically translatable endpoint (plasma TG). Key observations from studies demonstrating pharmacologic activity are summarized below.
L1586: ## In Vitro Pharmacological Activity of ISIS 678354 in Primary Human Hepatocytes
L1587: In a study (Study No. 20-1d52) in primary human hepatocytes (HepatoPac®), olezarsen produced a concentration-dependent reduction of ApoC-III protein with a half-maximal inhibitory concentration (IC_{50}) of 0.18 \muM and maximal efficacy of 82% knockdown in *apoC-III* mRNA levels after 48 hours of incubation followed by 48 hours of recovery. See Figure 39 below.

L1598: ## Dose Response Evaluation of ISIS 838707 in Wild-Type Mice Fed a Normal Chow Diet
L1599: An in vivo study (Study No. EX/3980_166) was conducted in wild-type CD-1 mice using a mouse-specific surrogate ApoC-III ASO (ISIS 838707, a murine GalNac3-conjugated version of olezarsen) because olezarsen is not pharmacologically active in mice due to the divergence in sequence between mouse and human ApoC-III protein.
L1600: CD-1 mice were injected once weekly by subcutaneous (SC) injection with ISIS 838707 (5 and 10 mg/kg/week or saline) for 6 weeks. Forty-eight hours following the last treatment, a dose-related reduction in liver *apoC-III* mRNA expression and plasma TG levels were noted, with the reductions reaching 88% and 76%, respectively, compared to untreated control mice at the 10 mg/kg/wk dose. See Table 87 below.

L1606: ## **Dose Response Evaluation of ISIS 678354 in Human apoC-III Transgenic mice**
L1607: An in vivo study (study No. EX/3979_73, 23-144a) was conducted in transgenic mice that express human *apoC-III* gene and have extremely high levels of TG. Human *apoC-III* transgenic mice treated with a single dose of olezarsen at 0.3, 1, 3, and 10 mg/kg showed dose-dependent reductions in liver *apoC-III* mRNA by up to 77% and plasma ApoC-III protein and plasma TG with up to 87% reduction in liver *apoC-III* mRNA and plasma TG at the 10 mg/kg dose (see Table 88 below). The median effective dose (ED_{50}) values for liver ApoC-III, plasma ApoC-III, and plasma TG reductions were identified as 1.3, 0.8, and 0.9 mg/kg, respectively.

L1612: ## **Dose Response Evaluation of ISIS 678354 in 39-Week Study in Lean Cynomolgus Monkeys**
L1613: Non-human primates (cynomolgus monkeys) are considered the most relevant nonclinical species because the pharmacologically active ASO portion of olezarsen exhibits full complementarity to the human *apoC-III* mRNA transcript sequence. Cynomolgus monkeys were administered olezarsen SC at 2, 6, 12, and 30 mg/kg/wk, once weekly for up to 39 weeks in a toxicology study (study No. 678354 – AS02) that included recovery assessments following 13- or 39-weeks of treatment. Regarding pharmacologic endpoints, there were dose-dependent reductions in liver *apoC-III* mRNA up to 90% and plasma *apoC-III* mRNA up to 77% compared to pre-dose that were maintained for the duration of treatment. A reversal of these effects was seen following recovery periods (see Figure 41 and Table 89 below). Minimal effects on plasma TG levels were noted since chow-fed cynomolgus monkeys already have very low plasma TG levels (&lt;50 mg/dL).

L1633: ## 13.3.2. Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, and Excretion/Pharmacokinetics
L1634: The nonclinical pharmacokinetic and toxicokinetic (PK/TK) parameters of olezarsen, including absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion, were characterized in the mouse following single and repeat SC dosing in 6-, 13-, and 26-week studies, and in monkeys following repeat-dosing in the 39-week study.
L1635: Olezarsen was quantified in plasma using quantitative hybridization assay coupled with electrochemiluminescence (ECL) and in tissues using high-performance liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometry. In addition, liver and kidney concentrations were measured in the chronic mouse and monkey studies and in F0 mice as part of the fertility and early embryonic development study.
L1636: ## Absorption
L1637: Across nonclinical species, the PK profiles were qualitatively comparable. Olezarsen rapidly enters plasma following SC administration and is then quickly distributed into tissues where it is metabolized by nucleases releasing the active ASO.
L1638: In mice, olezarsen was rapidly absorbed (time to maximum concentration [Tmax] = 0.5 to 1 hour). After reaching peak levels, plasma concentrations declined rapidly over the first 8 to 12 hours. Clearance was multi-phasic with an initial quick decline from plasma followed by a more prolonged decline from tissues. The mean residence time ranged from 2 to 5 hours at the doses evaluated and was similar between doses at each time point. Mean peak concentration (Cmax) and total plasma exposure (AUC0-48h) increase with dose in a greater than proportional manner. Plasma drug levels following repeated dosing were higher than after the initial dose administration suggesting accumulation. There were no sex differences in exposure. Exposure profile curves are shown in Figure 42.
L1639: Figure 42. Exposure Profile Curve of ISIS 678354 in Mice
L1640: Data presented are mean + standard deviation (N = 3)
L1641: Source: Study No. 678354-APK01PK, Figure 1
L1642: Source: Figure 1 of Applicant's Pharmacokinetic Written Summary, modified by reviewer to remove Applicant's figure title
L1643: In the monkey, rapid absorption was also seen (T_{\text{max}} = 1 to 4 hours), followed by a more prolonged clearance phase, with a half-life of approximately 30 days following repeated administration. C_{\text{max}} and \text{AUC}_{0-48\text{h}} increased greater than dose proportionally over the dose ranges evaluated, with levels generally 2- to 4-fold higher in antidrug antibody (ADA)-positive animals. In ADA-positive animals, plasma trough levels were also higher than in ADA-negative animals. There was no apparent accumulation in ADA-negative animals, but there was in ADA-positive animals, likely resulting from olezarsen binding to ADA in plasma. No sex differences in exposure were identified. Exposure profiles are shown in Figure 43 below.
