L1664: ## **Distribution**
L1665: The distribution of olezarsen was evaluated in mice and monkeys. Olezarsen is highly bound to plasma proteins (&gt;98%) in the mouse and monkey and was similar to that in human plasma. The mean blood to plasma ratios in human and monkey ranged from 0.488 to 0.98 at concentrations up to 300 \mug/mL, whereas in mouse, mean blood to plasma ratios was ~1. Distribution to tissues in mouse and monkey was responsible for the initial rapid plasma clearance, with the highest concentrations present in liver and kidney. In both species, concentrations in the liver were higher compared to kidney cortex at the lower doses evaluated, most likely attributed to more efficient uptake of GalNAcs by the asialoglycoprotein receptor in the liver. The concentration in the kidney cortex increased approximately dose-proportionally, whereas in the liver, it increased less than dose-proportionally, likely due to saturation of asialoglycoprotein receptor-mediated hepatocyte uptake at the higher doses evaluated in both species (12 and 20 mg/kg/wk in mouse and monkey, respectively) which limited further uptake into the liver. The saturation occurred at high multiples of the proposed clinical dose (80 mg/month, or 0.285 mg/kg/wk). Near steady-state tissue exposure levels were achieved after 13 weeks of once-weekly dosing in both mouse and monkey. Minimal levels were reported in both tissues following recovery periods, consistent with terminal tissue half-lives of approximately 4 weeks in both species. Data are summarized in Table 93 (mice) and Table 94 (monkey) below.
L1666: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)

L1678: ## Metabolism
L1679: Across species, the metabolism of olezarsen begins with an initial hydrolysis of the GalNAc3 to form the unconjugated ASO in tissues. The unconjugated ASO is metabolized in tissues by intracellular nucleases to produce chain-shortened oligonucleotide metabolites, consistent with endonuclease cleavage followed by sequential exonuclease cleavage of deoxyporphorothioate linkages.
L1680: In mouse and monkey plasma, fully conjugated olezarsen was the major circulating species within the first 24 hours postdose, accounting for 69% to 93% of the detected analytes. N-1 to N-15 chain-shortened metabolites and unconjugated drug accounted for the remaining analytes.
L1681: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L1682: Prior to subsequent dosing, unconjugated drug accounted for the nearly total abundance of analyte detected.
L1683: In the liver and kidney of mouse and monkey, full-length oligonucleotide primarily associated with unconjugated olezarsen was the most abundant species (&gt;80%), with multiple chain shortened oligonucleotide metabolites (N1-N15) accounting for anywhere from &lt;1% to &lt;7% of the total peak area in mice, and &lt;1% to &lt;2% in monkeys. In urine, unconjugated drug was the major component.
L1684: Eight linker-related metabolites (M5-M12) were detected in mouse plasma and 10 metabolites (M4-M12 and M14) in monkey plasma, with highest levels typically seen 2 hours after dosing. Concentrations were &lt;6% of parent levels. Levels were decreased up to 10-fold further by 24 hours and were inconsistently able to be detected prior to subsequent dose administration. In mouse urine, linker metabolites were predominantly excreted in the first 24 hours.
L1685: Human plasma was collected and analyzed for fully conjugated drug and metabolites at pre-dose and/or 2 hours postdose at various study days in clinical Trial ISIS 678354-CS1 and reported in study 678354-IS13. Full length drug was the only analyte detected, accounting for 100% of the peak area in all samples. In human urine collected in clinical Trial ISIS 678354-CS1 and reported in study 678354-IS0999, unconjugated olezarsen (i.e., volanesorsen) is the most abundant component, while various chain-shortened metabolites accounted for the remainder. Overall, the metabolite profile in plasma and urine was similar between nonclinical species (mouse and monkeys) and humans and there were no unique metabolites identified in humans compared to animal species.
L1686: ## Excretion
L1687: Excretion of the unconjugated olezarsen and its metabolites occur predominantly through urine, and to a lesser extent through feces.
L1688: In mouse urine, full-length olezarsen represented &lt;1% of the administered dose during the initial 48 hours following administration; the major component was unconjugated olezarsen. In human urine, unchanged fully conjugated olezarsen was the major oligonucleotide component, with several oligonucleotide metabolites associated with olezarsen 3'-deletion that still retained some of the linker component attached, as well as chain-shortened oligonucleotides from unconjugated olezarsen were detected.
L1689: ## Anti-Drug Antibodies
L1690: ADA levels were measured in the chronic monkey study. ADA were detected in animals from all treated groups (30-64%) and controls (22%). The plasma trough levels of olezarsen were increased approximately up to 6-fold in ADA+ animals as compared to that reported in ADA- animals following 3 months or more treatment, suggesting olezarsen binding to ADA in plasma. However, there were no notable differences in tissue levels of drug between ADA+ and ADA- animals. Furthermore, the presence of ADAs had no significant effects on the toxicity profile or pharmacologic endpoints (hepatic *apoC-III* mRNA levels).
L1691: ## Toxicokinetics
L1692: Toxicokinetic data and/or tissue drug levels were collected as a component of various repeat-dose toxicity studies. Descriptions of pertinent TK data are summarized with the results of individual toxicology studies (refer to Section 13.3.3).
L1693: ## **13.3.3. Toxicology**
L1694: The toxicology of olezarsen has been characterized in repeat-dose toxicity studies, a fertility and early embryonic developmental study, a battery of in vitro and in vivo genotoxicity studies, and an impurity qualification study. The toxicity of the active, unconjugated ASO (volanesorsen) on embryo-fetal development, pre-postnatal development, and its carcinogenic potential were previously assessed in support of the marketing application for that product. In agreement with the Agency, these studies with the unconjugated ASO were considered supportive of the current application.
L1695: ## General Toxicology
L1696: The general toxicity of olezarsen has been characterized in repeat-dose toxicity studies in CD-1 mice and cynomolgus monkeys in studies of up to 26- and 39-weeks duration, respectively, with once-weekly SC injections. These studies included 13- and 26-week recovery periods in the mouse and monkey, respectively. The no observed adverse effect levels (NOAELs), basis for the NOAELs, and the safety margins based on body surface area (BSA) are captured in Table 95 below. The summary table is followed by more detailed reviews of the pivotal toxicology studies conducted with olezarsen that support this application.
L1697: Drug-related effects consistent with the expected toxicities of an ASO with a phosphorothioate backbone were evident in the liver, kidney, and spleen. Tissue effects at the injection site were also observed. The most notable effect was thrombocytopenia, observed in both the mouse and NHP.

L1702: ## Individual Study Summaries
L1703: ## **26-Week Repeat Dose Toxicity Study of ISIS 678354 in Mice With a 13-Week Recovery Period (Study No. 678354- AS04)**
L1704: Dosages of 2, 6, or 20 mg/kg were administered once weekly by SC injection for 26 weeks followed by a 13-week recovery period (control and 20 mg/kg). An additional group was also administered a murine specific version of the same ASO (ISIS 838707) at 6 mg/kg/wk to evaluate target-related toxicity.
L1705: ## Key Study Findings
L1706: - Increases in aspartate aminotransferase (AST) (up to ~3-fold), alanine aminotransferase (ALT) (up to ~7x) at 20 mg/kg/wk dose in both sexes compared to controls. These effects were reversible.
- Increase in alkaline phosphatase (up to ~2-fold compared to control) in males at 20 mg/kg/wk that resolved during recovery.
- Reversible increase in monocyte chemoattractant protein-1 (MCP-1) (~8-fold compared to control) in both sexes at 20 mg/kg/wk, which suggests an inflammatory response because MCP-1 is a potent inflammatory cytokine.
- Immunoglobulin M (IgM) decreased in males at \ge 6 mg/kg/wk (up to 40% lower) and in females at \ge 2 mg/kg/wk (up to 58%).
- Dose-related increased absolute and relative weights for liver (1.2 to 1.6-fold) and spleen (1.6 to 1.9-fold over control) at \ge 6 mg/kg/wk in both sexes compared to controls, which correlated with microscopic findings in the liver (hepatocellular hypertrophy, cytoplasmic alteration, karyomegaly, increased mitotic figures and vacuolated/granular macrophages) and spleen (red pulp cellularity), respectively.
- Decreased absolute and relative thymus weights (-41% to -45% lower) at 20 mg/kg/wk in both sexes, which correlated with increased generalized lymphoid depletion in all animals administered 20 mg/kg/wk.
- The absolute and relative liver weight increase (1.1 to 1.2-fold); spleen weight increase (1.4 to 1.5-fold) and thymus weight decrease (-30 to -59%) and microscopic findings persisted at 20 mg/kg/wk during recovery.
- Increased absolute and relative kidney weight (~1.2-fold), cytoplasmic basophilic granules in the tubular epithelial cells and minimal tubular vacuolation.
- Decreases in hepatic *apoC-III* mRNA expression (~ -88%), plasma TG (~ -49% lower, relative to control), and mild increases in MCP-1 (~2-fold over control) with the ISIS 838707 (the mouse specific inhibitor).
- Tissue concentration (\mu\text{g/g}) of unconjugated olezarsen following multiple SC administration (48 hours after last dose) with recovery demonstrated dose-dependent increases in the liver and kidney, with more than dose-proportional increase in the kidney, and low concentrations in reproductive organs compared to kidney and liver in CD-1 mice.
L1707: The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) for the 26-week mouse study was 2 mg/kg/wk, based on the dose-related pattern, incidence, and severity of the microscopic findings in the liver and spleen, which correlated with increased absolute and relative organ weights at \ge 6 mg/kg/wk and minimal to marked increases in liver enzymes, resulting in a 0.5x safety margin based on BSA at the maximum recommended human dose of 80 mg/month. The pharmtox reviewer's identified NOAEL differs from that determined by the Applicant. The Applicant considered only liver, spleen, and thymus effects (organ weight changes and microscopic findings) at 20 mg/kg/wk to be adverse because they persisted through recovery and identified 6 mg/kg/wk as the NOAEL.

L1713: ## Dose Justification
L1714: The dosages used in the 26-week study were based on results from the 13-week study conducted in CD-1 mice administered 0, 2, 6, or 24 mg/kg once weekly by SC administration. The key finding from this study pertinent to dosage selection was a 25% reduction in platelet count (thrombocytopenia) in all 24 mg/kg/wk females which was reversible. Thrombocytopenia was also seen with the unconjugated ASO (volanesorsen).

L1727: ## 39-Week Repeat Dose Toxicity Study of ISIS 678354 in Monkey With a 13-Week Interim Necropsy and a 26-Week Recovery Period (Study No. 678354- AS02)
L1728: SC dosages of 2, 6, and 12 mg/kg were administered for 39 weeks followed by a 26-week recovery period at 12 mg/kg. These groups also included animals terminated at 13 weeks. Additionally, a dosage of 30 mg/kg was administered for 13 weeks; this group also included a 26-week recovery period.
L1729: ## Key Study Findings
L1730: - A single male administered 12 mg/kg/wk had very low platelet counts on both Day 65 and at the interim termination at Week 13.
- Increases in glutamate dehydrogenase (up to 5-fold baseline), ALT (4-fold baseline), AST (1.6x baseline) were evident at \ge 6 mg/kg/wk. Decreases in albumin (\sim 13-30% lower) in both
L1731: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L1732: sexes at \ge 6 mg/kg/wk and A:G ratio (~40% lower), and creatinine were also seen, primarily at dosages \ge 12 mg/kg/wk. Liver enzyme increases correlated with microscopic findings in the liver. These effects on serum chemistry parameters were reversible in the recovery group.

- At dosages \ge 12 mg/kg, basophilic granules were found in the liver (in hepatocytes and Kupffer cells; associated with Kupffer cell hypertrophy), kidney, lymph nodes, and multiple other tissues. Additional findings were noted in the liver (glycogen vacuolation), kidney (tubular cell vacuolation), and injection site (mononuclear cell infiltration) was observed at the injection site. There were no microscopic correlates to the heavier spleen. Basophilic granules were still present in multiple tissues following recovery.
- Dose-related reduction in hepatic *apoC-III* mRNA levels and plasma protein levels and plasma TG levels were evident. Both showed reversal following the recovery period.
- Plasma exposure increased with dose in a greater than proportional manner, there were no sex differences, and there was minimal accumulation. A similar pattern was seen in liver and kidney tissues. The half-life in both plasma and tissues ranged from 20 to 30 days.
- ADA-positive animals were identified in all groups. The overall incidence of animals testing positive for ADA's ranged from approximately 30% to 64% in those administered olezarsen for 39 weeks while the incidence in controls was 22%. Following recovery, 50% of animals administered 12 mg/kg/wk and 75% of those administered 30 mg/kg/wk remained ADA-positive.
- Olezarsen was rapidly absorbed from plasma with T_{max} occurring by 4 hours after dose administration, exposure increased with dose in a greater than proportional manner. The non-linearity was attributed to higher clearance at the lower dose levels as compared to that at the higher dosages administered. There was no sex difference in exposure and minimal accumulation.
- The plasma terminal elimination half-life (t_{1/2}) was estimated to be approximately 30 days based on data generated at 12 and 30 mg/kg/wk supporting the once-monthly dosing regimen.
- Following absorption from plasma, rapid distribution to tissues (liver and kidney), with less than 2-fold difference in levels between the tissues (liver had more than kidney).
- The calculated t_{1/2} following 13 and 39 weeks of treatment in kidney tissue was approximately 30 days and in liver was 21 to 29 days.
- Hepatic *apoC-III* mRNA and ApoC-III protein expression in plasma were reduced in a dose-dependent manner, up to 90% after up to 39 weeks of treatment at doses \ge 2 mg/kg/wk.
L1733: The NOAEL was identified as 6 mg/kg/wk, based on the thrombocytopenia observed in a single animal at 12 mg/kg/wk and the increased incidence/severity of microscopic alterations in liver and kidney that were not completely resolved following the recovery period at dosages \ge 12 mg/kg/wk. This NOAEL of 6 mg/kg/wk gives a 6x safety margin based on BSA relative to maximum recommended human dose of 80 mg/month. The lowest observable effect level of 12 mg/kg/wk at which thrombocytopenia was observed gives a 12x safety margin.
L1734: ## Additional Comments
L1735: Thrombocytopenia was a drug-related effect seen with olezarsen in a single male administered 12 mg/kg/wk on Days 65 and Day 93. In comparison, thrombocytopenia occurred in multiple animals at comparable mg/kg dosage levels that correlated with clinical signs of severe bruising and petechia leading to early terminations and/or dosing holidays in studies with the active unconjugated ASO volanesorsen.

## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L1743: **Table 100. Summary of Results, 39-Week Monkey Study**
L1744: Endpoints	Major Findings
Endpoints with notable observations	
Clinical chemistry	
Increases in glutamate dehydrogenase (GLDH) (up to 5-fold baseline), ALT (4-fold baseline), AST (1.6x baseline) were evident at \ge 6 mg/kg/wk. Decreases in ALB (\sim13-30% lower) in both sexes at \ge 6 mg/kg/wk and A:G ratio (\sim40% lower), and CREA were also seen, primarily at dosages \ge 12 mg/kg/wk. Liver enzyme increases correlated with microscopic findings in the liver. These effects on serum chemistry parameters were reversible in the recovery group. IgM was slightly increased (1.6-1.7-fold over baseline) in both sexes at \ge 12 mg/kg/wk during interim sacrifice. Slight increase in IgG in one male at 2 mg/kg/wk and 2 females in 30 mg/kg/wk group.
Dose-dependent increases in complement Bb were seen at all dosages at one or more timepoints. Up to 3-fold (females) and up to 21-fold (males) at 12 and 30 mg/kg/wk and up to 3.6-fold (males) and 5.5-fold (females) at \ge 2 mg/kg/wk. Increases in complement Bb was accompanied by decreases in complement C3 was seen at all dosages in males and at 12 and 30 mg/kg/wk in females. Effects indicative of generalized inflammation included increases in CRP, MCP-1, and/or IFN\gamma levels, primarily at dosages > 12 mg/kg. These effects on serum chemistry parameters were reversible in the recovery group.

Hematology	
A single male administered 12 mg/kg had low platelet counts (>98\% decrease) during the 13-week treatment interval but survived to scheduled interim termination.
Transient prolongation of activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT) times (up to 1.5x baseline) occurred at 30 mg/kg/wk.

Organ weight	
The liver, lymph nodes, and spleen were grossly enlarged and / or had increased weights. At terminal sacrifice, absolute liver weight increased in males at \ge 6 mg/kg/wk (\ge 1.5-fold); absolute and relative liver weight increased in females at \ge 12 mg/kg/wk (\sim 1.7-fold and \sim 1.5-fold, respectively). Spleen weight in females at 12 mg/kg/wk increased by \sim 2-fold. Effects on organ weights were reversible.

Histopathology	
At dosages \ge 12 mg/kg, basophilic granules were found in the liver (in hepatocytes and Kupffer cells; associated with Kupffer cell hypertrophy), kidney, lymph nodes, and multiple other tissues. Additional findings were noted in the liver (glycogen vacuolation), kidney (tubular cell vacuolation), and injection site (mononuclear cell infiltration). There were no microscopic correlates to the heavier spleen. Basophilic granules were still present in multiple tissues following recovery

Anti-Drug Antibodies (ADAs) / Immunogenicity	
The presence of ADA had no effect on exposures on days 1 and 91; at day 273, exposures were 2 to 4-fold higher in ADA positive animals. There were no differences in hepatic apoC-mRNA, C3 and platelet count between ADA positive and ADA negative animals after 9 months of treatment.
There was no effect on the ability of the animals to mount an immune response based on T-cell dependent antibody responses (TDAR) to KLH challenges.

Endpoints without notable observations	
No drug-related adverse effects on survival, clinical observations, body weights, food consumption, ophthalmoscopic examinations, ECGs, blood pressure, urine analysis, urine chemistry or cytokine/chemokine.

L1770: ## Carcinogenicity
L1771: Carcinogenicity studies were not conducted for olezarsen. However, carcinogenicity studies were conducted with the active unconjugated ASO volanesorsen.
L1772: Two-year carcinogenicity studies were conducted with volanesorsen in CD-1 mice (study no. 304801-AS15) and Sprague-Dawley rats (study no. 304801-AS16) to assess its carcinogenic potential. Results from these studies are summarized below.
L1773: In the 2-year mouse study with volanesorsen, dosages of 6, 25, and 40 mg/kg/wk were administered SC. There was reduced survival attributed to cardiomyocyte degeneration associated with inflammatory cell infiltrates at dosages ≥25 mg/kg/wk leading to early termination of these groups (Day 625, males at 25 mg/kg/wk; Day 617, females at 25 mg/kg/wk; Day 507, males at 40 mg/kg/wk and Day 487, females 40 mg/kg/wk). Drug-related neoplastic observations were noted at all dosages evaluated. In the liver of male mice, hepatocellular adenomas/carcinomas were increased at dosages ≥25 mg/kg/wk, and multicentric hemangiomas/hemangiosarcomas were increased at all doses. In female mice, multicentric histiocytic sarcomas were increased at all doses (including in the group dosed with the mouse-specific oligonucleotide), and pituitary gland adenomas were increased at 25 mg/kg/wk. The liver adenomas, histiocytic sarcomas, and hemangiosarcomas likely reflect the pro-inflammatory properties of the phosphorothioate ASO drug class and the greater sensitivity of rodents to these effects. Pituitary tumors are most likely a reflection of the high background rate of hyperplasia in this species, which is also a relatively common background lesion in aging mice. Thus, although the increased incidences of these tumors are drug-related, the relevance to the clinical population is likely to be limited. As a result of the reduction in survival at the mid dose/high dose (HD),
L1774: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L1775: dose-related increased incidences of cardiovascular effects, and neoplastic effects at all dosages, a NOAEL for the induction of neoplasia was not identified.
L1776: In the 2-year rat study with volanesorsen, dosages of 0.2, 1, and 5 mg/kg/wk were administered. Survival was reduced in males at 5 mg/kg/wk due to either masses at injection sites or chronic progressive nephropathy. Drug-related neoplasms observed were increases in the incidences of malignant fibrous histiocytomas (MFH) in the skin/subcutis of the injection sites at dosages \ge 1 mg/kg/wk in both sexes. The injection site MFH neoplasms with volanesorsen treatment in rats may be attributable to chronic inflammatory reactions associated with repeated SC injections into a limited number of sites and is not likely to be clinically relevant. The NOAEL for the induction of neoplasms in the 2-year rat study is considered the low dose (LD) of 0.2 mg/kg/wk (0.1x the maximum recommended human dose based on BSA comparison of administered dose) based on the increased incidence of MFH at \ge 1 mg/kg/wk.
L1777: In summary, the carcinogenicity studies identified increased incidences of neoplasms in both mice and rats. The majority of these findings were related to the increased sensitivity of these species to the proinflammatory effects of phosphorothioate oligonucleotides and/or the method of administration. As such, the relevance of these findings to the clinical population is limited. Hepatocellular carcinomas, malignant fibrous histiocytomas, hemangiosarcomas, and histiocytic sarcomas have also been reported in carcinogenicity studies with other ASOs. Thus, the presence of these tumors with the unconjugated ASO provides further support that these neoplasms may be a class effect of the phosphorothioate oligonucleotides.
L1778: ## Reproductive Toxicology
L1779: A GLP-compliant fertility and early embryonic developmental study was conducted for olezarsen in CD-1 mice (study no. 678354-AS08) because of decreased prostate/seminal vesicle weight and decreased sperm count from the combined fertility and embryo-fetal development mouse study with volanesorsen. Studies evaluating embryofetal development and pre-/postnatal development were not conducted with olezarsen as there were no effects on parameters evaluated in these studies conducted with the unconjugated ASO volanesorsen. The NOAELs from the reproductive toxicology studies conducted with olezarsen and volanesorsen are summarized in Table 104 below.
L1780: In Table 104, safety multiples are estimated between the study NOAELs and the clinical dose, based on BSA comparisons. For the estimates, the weekly BSA dose of olezarsen or volanesorsen were multiplied by 2 and 4, respectively, to equate to the once monthly clinical dosing regimen of olezarsen compared to more frequent dosing in animal studies.

L1789: ## Individual Study Summaries–Reproductive Toxicology Studies With Olezarsen
L1790: ## **A Fertility and Embryonic Developmental Study of ISIS 678354 in CD-1 Mice Via Subcutaneous Administration (Study No. 678354-AS08)**
L1791: Summary: The potential effect of olezarsen on fertility and early embryonic development was evaluated in CD-1 mice. No adverse effects of olezarsen were observed for any parameter, including effects on mating, fertility, and early embryonic development. Consequently, the NOAEL for effects on fertility and early embryonic development was considered the HD of 20 mg/kg.
L1792: ## Key Study Findings
L1793: - Slightly increased ALT (~1.7-fold over control) levels in males at 20 mg/kg
- TG levels were reduced to 64% of control levels in HD males and reduced to 59% of controls in females administered doses \ge10 mg/kg, consistent with the expected pharmacological effect of the drug.
- Increased absolute and relative liver weights (~1.2 and ~1.1-fold over control) in males at 20 mg/kg, increased absolute and relative spleen weight (~1.2-fold over control) in males at \ge10 mg/kg, increased relative kidney weight (~1.1-fold over control) in females at 20 mg/kg.
- Increases (~29% \uparrow over control) in fertility and fecundity indices observed at \ge10 mg/kg.

L1810: ## Reproductive Toxicology Studies With the Active Unconjugated ASO (Volanesorsen) and/or ISIS 440670
L1811: A battery of GLP-compliant reproductive and developmental toxicity studies was conducted to assess the potential effects for the active unconjugated ASO (volanesorsen).
L1812: ## Combination Fertility and Embryofetal Development Study in Mice
L1813: In a combined fertility and embryofetal development study in CD-1 mice (study number 304801-AS07), volanesorsen was administered SC at dosages of 3, 10, or 25 mg/kg once every other day (equivalent to 10.5, 35, or 87.5 mg/kg/wk). Males were treated for 4 weeks prior to cohabitation with treated females and continued to be treated for a total of 10 weeks; females were treated for 2 weeks prior to cohabitation and continued treatment until gestational day 15/16. An additional group was administered a murine specific version of this ASO (ISIS 440670) at a dose of 3 mg/kg every other day. In males, treatment with volanesorsen did not result in any adverse effects on fertility following 4 weeks of treatment. However, following 10 weeks of treatment there was a reduction in sperm count (33% lower) and decreased prostate/seminal vesicle weights (\downarrow ~20%) at 87.5 mg/kg/wk. It is not known if there would have been an effect on fertility endpoints following a longer treatment period. Other effects observed included slight increases in monocytes (up to 4-fold), decreased platelets (reduced up to 32%), and increased ALT (up to 10-fold) and AST (up to 6-fold) in both sexes at 25 mg/kg/wk; the increase in liver enzymes was associated with increased liver weights. Increased spleen weights (up to 1.7-fold) were also evident at this dose.
L1814: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L1815: In females, no effects on fertility endpoints were noted. Slight increases in the litter incidences of rib variations were noted at the HD, but the increases were not statistically significant. No effects were reported for ISIS 440670. The identified NOAEL for male and female fertility and embryofetal development was 87.5 mg/kg/wk.
L1816: A combined fertility and developmental study in CD-1 mice with ISIS 440670, an antisense inhibitor of the mouse *apoC-III* mRNA (study number 304801-AS08) administered SC every other day at dosages of 0, 10, or 25 mg/kg (equivalent to 0, 35, or 87.5 mg/kg/wk), effects were limited to decreases in platelet counts (reduced up to 22% at 25 mg/kg), decreased TG levels (reduced up to 67% at 25 mg/kg), and reductions in hepatic *apoC-III* mRNA (reduced up to 36% at 25 mg/kg) were observed in both sexes at both dosages. The latter two effects were related to the pharmacology of the compound. Postmortem examination revealed increases in spleen weights (up to 1.6-fold), increased kidney weights (up to 1.2-fold, and liver weights (up to 1.3-fold) at the 25 mg/kg dose. As with volanesorsen, slight increases in the incidence of rib variations were seen at both dose levels that did not reach statistical significance. There were no effects on fertility endpoints. Overall, under the conditions of the experiment, ISIS 440670 was well-tolerated up to 25 mg/kg (87.5 mg/kg/wk) following 10 weeks of treatment in males and 4 to 6 weeks of treatment in females. No adverse effects on male or female fertility or embryofetal development were observed at up to the top dose of 25 mg/kg (87.5 mg/kg/wk).
L1817: ## Embryofetal Development Study in the Rabbit
L1818: In an embryofetal development study, rabbits (study no. 304801-AS09) were administered volanesorsen SC at dosages of 3, 6, 15 mg/kg once every other day (equivalent to 10.5, 21, or 52.5 mg/kg/wk). Reduced feed consumption (18% lower during treatment period) with associated clinical observations of reduced/absent feces and reduced weight gain during the treatment (47% lower) and post-treatment (33% lower) periods in maternal animals at 52.5 mg/kg/wk. Postmortem examination revealed tan discoloration of the liver at 52.5 mg/kg/wk and increased weights of the kidney, liver, and spleen at all dosages (ranging from ~5% to 26%, although the effects were considered significant only at \ge21 mg/kg/wk. Abortions/delivery of non-viable fetuses were evident at 52.5 mg/kg/wk. At this dose, fetal weights were slightly lower (decreased up to 14%). There were no effects on gross, visceral, or skeletal development. The identified maternal NOAEL was 21 mg/kg/wk based on the reduced feed consumption, reduced weight gain, and abortion/reduced viable fetuses at 52.5 mg/kg/wk.
L1819: ## Pre- and Postnatal Toxicity Study in Mice
L1820: In a pre-/postnatal study, female CD-1 mice (study no. 304801-AS17) were administered volanesorsen SC at dosages of 3, 10 or 25 mg/kg every other day (equivalent to 10.5, 35, or 87.5 mg/kg/wk) during gestation followed by once weekly administration from parturition through postpartum day 21. An additional group was administered ISIS 440670 at 87.5 mg/kg/wk. Minimal effects were seen on maternal animals (body weights &lt;6% lower; kidney, liver, and spleen weights increased &lt;2-fold). The offspring at 87.5 mg/kg/wk weighed up to 15% less than concurrent controls and weights remained lower throughout the lifespan which contributed to delays in the age offspring reached various developmental landmarks. Delays of up to ~3 days occurred in the appearance of evidence of sexual maturity in females at all dosages of volanesorsen and with ISIS 440670. There were no effects on reproductive performance of F1 animals. The weights of various organs were also reduced in the F1 generation animals, but these
L1821: effects are considered secondary to the lighter body weights. The HD of 87.5 mg/kg/wk was considered the NOAEL for maternal animals based on the absence of an effect on reproductive function or the ability to raise a litter. For the F1 generation, 35 mg/kg/wk was considered the NOAEL based on the reduced weights observed throughout their lifespan at 87.5 mg/kg/wk.
L1822: ## 13.3.4. Other Studies
L1823: ## Impurities/Degradants
L1824: Impurities were qualified in a 13-week impurity qualification study in mice and the 39-week repeat-dose toxicity study in monkeys.
L1825: ## **13-Week Repeat-Dose Toxicity and Impurity Qualification Study in ICR Mice (Study No. 678354-AS06)**
L1826: Olezarsen impurities were qualified in a 13-week repeat-dose toxicity study in Institute for Cancer Research mice in which the toxicity profile of two batches of olezarsen that had enriched impurity levels were compared to a representative batch of olezarsen that had not been enriched with impurities. The treatment groups (10/sex/group) received weekly SC doses of test article. Two dose levels (2.33 and 7 mg/kg/wk) of each formulation were administered. Animals in the olezarsen control group were dosed with test article prepared from drug substance CA678354-002 (purity, 91.3%) which was also used in Clinical Trial 3 (CS3). A vehicle control group was administered saline. Animals were terminated on Day 87. Mortality, clinical signs, body weight, food consumption, ophthalmic examinations, clinical pathology, and microscopic findings were evaluated to assess potential impurity-related toxicity.
L1827: ## **Key Study Findings**
L1828: - Target organs included liver, spleen, and injection site for all drug-treated groups.
- Microscopic findings were evident in the liver (single cell necrosis, mononuclear cell infiltration, nuclear degeneration/inclusions, cytomegaly / karyomegaly), spleen (extramedullary hematopoiesis), and injection site (mononuclear cell infiltration and basophilic granules). These effects were correlated with elevated liver enzymes (increases in ALT/AST of up to approximately 2-fold) and/or increase organ weights.
- In general, the incidences and severities were comparable between groups at the same dose, although the incidence and severity of single cell necrosis in the liver and extramedullary hematopoiesis in the spleen were slightly higher in the groups administered impurity-enriched olezarsen relative to that seen with olezarsen.
- Liver drug levels were comparable between all three test articles.
L1829: The drug-related effects observed in groups administered olezarsen with enhanced levels of impurities was considered similar to that observed in the group administered olezarsen without enhanced levels of impurities at each dose. Overall, there were no meaningful differences in the severity or incidence of the various findings at the evaluated dose levels. In conclusion, enhanced levels of impurities did not affect the established toxicity profile of olezarsen.

## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)

L1846: ## 14. Clinical Pharmacology
L1847: ## 14.1. In Vitro Studies
L1848: ## Plasma Protein Binding
L1849: ## **In Vitro Evaluation of the Extent of Plasma Protein Binding of ISIS 678354 in Human, Monkey, and Mouse Plasma by Ultrafiltration (Trial 678354-IS04)**
L1850: The plasma protein binding of olezarsen was evaluated by an ultrafiltration method. Olezarsen at two nominal concentrations (5 and 150 µg/mL) in human, monkey, and mouse plasma was preincubated at 37°C for approximately 30 minutes without adjusting pH (pH in whole plasma ranged from 7.3 to 7.5) prior to ultra-centrifugation. The concentrations in plasma and ultrafiltrate were determined using a nuclease-dependent hybridization enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA). Olezarsen is found to be highly bound to human plasma proteins (&gt;99% bound) at concentrations of 5 and 150 µg/mL. The extent of plasma protein binding was similar across all species and between the two concentrations tested and determined to be ≥98%.
L1851: ## Distribution in Blood Cells
L1852: ## **In Vitro Evaluation of Whole Blood and Plasma Concentration Ratio of ISIS 678354 (Olezarsen) in Human, Monkey, and Mouse (Trial 678354-IS11)**
L1853: The blood to plasma distribution ratio of olezarsen was evaluated neat and at six different nominal concentrations (0, 0.01, 0.03, 0.1, 0.3, 1, and 3 µg/mL) in human whole blood, neat and at eight different nominal concentrations (0, 0.1, 0.3, 1, 3, 10, 30, 100, and 300 µg/mL) in monkey whole blood, and at seven different nominal concentrations (0, 0.1, 0.3, 1.00, 3.00, 10.0, and 30.0 µg/mL) in mouse whole blood. The blood to plasma ratio for olezarsen ranged from 0.73 to 0.98, 0.49 to 0.64, and 0.83 to 0.98 at concentrations tested in the human, monkey, and mice, respectively, suggesting that olezarsen distributes mostly in the plasma compartment.
L1854: ## 14.1.1. Drug-Drug Interaction Studies
L1855: ## Study Related to Plasma Protein Binding Interactions
L1856: ## **In Vitro Evaluation of Human Plasma Protein Binding of ISIS 678354 and its Interactions with Warfarin and Ibuprofen by Ultrafiltration (Trial 678354-IS06)**
L1857: Warfarin and ibuprofen were selected for the evaluation of plasma protein-binding displacement with olezarsen because they are highly bound to albumin binding site I and site II, respectively. The substrate and displacer concentrations tested in this trial were 1 and 10 µg/mL for olezarsen, 3 and 30 µg/mL for warfarin, and 30 and 300 µg/mL for ibuprofen. The concentrations of
L1858: olezarsen in plasma and ultrafiltrate were determined using a hybridization ECL assay, and concentrations of warfarin and ibuprofen were determined by high-performance liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometry.
L1859: The extent of plasma protein binding of olezarsen was similar with and without the presence of warfarin or ibuprofen, and the protein binding of warfarin and ibuprofen was similar with and without the presence of olezarsen. The percent displacement of olezarsen (as a substrate) by warfarin and ibuprofen (as displacers) or the percent displacement of warfarin or ibuprofen (as substrates) by olezarsen (as a displacer) was found to be negligible (&lt;1%). These data suggest that no plasma protein-binding displacement is expected between olezarsen and other highly plasma protein-bound drugs.
L1860: ## **Trials Related to Potential Involvement of Cytochrome P450 Isoforms**
L1861: ## **Cellular Uptake and In Vitro Evaluation of ISIS 678354 as Inhibitors of Cytochrome P450 Expression in Human Hepatocytes (Trial 678354-IS10)**
L1862: Cytochrome P450 (CYP) inhibition study was performed by preincubating olezarsen for 30 minutes at concentrations ranging from 0.1 to 50 \muM in suspensions of cryopreserved cultured primary human hepatocytes, followed by the addition of a probe substrate for each CYP enzyme to initiate the metabolic reaction for 45 minutes for 1A2, 2B6, 2C9, 2D6, 3A4, and 90 minutes for 2C8, 2C19, 2E1.
L1863: Evaluations of the cellular uptake of olezarsen in cryopreserved cultured human primary hepatocytes was conducted with olezarsen at concentrations of 1, 10, and 50 \muM and an incubation time up to 120 minutes, where concentrations of olezarsen in cell pellets were determined using an LC-MS/MS method.
L1864: The results indicate that olezarsen up to 50 \muM does not inhibit the activities of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1, or CYP3A4. Under the same experimental conditions, known inhibitors of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1, and CYP3A4 showed expected inhibition of enzyme activities, demonstrating that the hepatocyte inhibition experiment was appropriately performed.
L1865: Hepatocyte uptake of olezarsen occurred throughout the 120-minute incubation period, with an average intracellular concentration of 78.1 \muM after 60-minute incubation and 72.8 \muM after 120-minute incubation at 50 \muM of olezarsen in the incubation medium. These intracellular concentrations associated with hepatocytes are approximately 10-fold higher than the estimated steady-state level in the liver at the clinical dose of 80 mg once Q4W.
L1866: Taken together, results from this study demonstrate the lack of potential for olezarsen to inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1, and CYP3A4.
L1867: ## **In Vitro Assessment of Cytochrome P450 Induction Potential of ISIS 678354 in Plated Cryopreserved Human Primary Hepatocytes (Trial 678354-IS07)**
L1868: The potential for olezarsen to act as an inducer of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4 was evaluated in cryopreserved primary human hepatocytes using both the enzyme activities and corresponding mRNA levels. Human cryopreserved primary hepatocytes were treated with olezarsen at a concentration range of 0.1 to 50 \muM for 48 hours.
L1869: The results of the study demonstrated that olezarsen did not cause induction of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 at mRNA level in three donors, [REDACTED](b)(6), up to 50 µM except for CYP3A4 for donor [REDACTED](b)(6). In donor [REDACTED](b)(6), the maximal induction fold change in mRNA was 3.27-fold in CYP3A4 (5.41% of positive control rifampicin response) at 50 µM, with determined induction 50% effective concentration (EC_{50}) of 20 µM and maximum effect of 3.60-fold. At 10 µM, it showed less than 2-fold increase, considered as no effect. At the olezarsen media concentrations of 10 and 50 µM, the corresponding mean hepatocyte concentrations over the three time points (30, 60, and 120 minutes) were 35.6 \pm 3.98 µM and 72.2 \pm 13.5 µM, respectively. These concentrations are significantly higher than the expected liver concentrations in patients (predicted based on data from the chronic monkey Study 678354-AS02) approximately by 4- and 9-fold, respectively. Thus, olezarsen is not an inducer of CYP3A4 at clinically relevant doses.
L1870: Taken together, results from this study demonstrate the lack of potential for olezarsen to induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 at clinically relevant doses.
L1871: ## **Trial Related to Potential Involvement of Major Drug Transporters**
L1872: ## **Assessment of ISIS 678354 as an Inhibitor or Substrate of Human Organic Anion Transporter (OAT)1, OAT3, Organic Cation Transporter (OCT)1, OCT2, Organic Anion Transporting Polypeptide (OATP)1B1, OATP1B3, Multidrug and Toxin Extrusion Protein (MATE)1, MATE2-K, Breast Cancer Resistance Protein (BCRP), P-glycoprotein (P-gp), and Bile Salt Export Pump (BSEP)-Mediated Transport (Trial 678354-IS08)**
L1873: The potential for olezarsen to act as a substrate or inhibitor of human OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BSEP, BCRP, P-gp was evaluated at 10 µM and 100 µM of olezarsen, respectively. The transport of each probe substrate was determined by radiometric detection, while concentrations of olezarsen were determined using an LC-MS/MS method.
L1874: At a concentration of 100 µM of olezarsen, the mean percent inhibition ranged from -31.1% to 45.3% for all the transporters evaluated. Therefore, olezarsen is not considered an inhibitor of OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BSEP, BCRP, or P-gp. In the substrate assays at an olezarsen concentration of 10 µM, less than a 2-fold difference of uptake was observed in transporter-transfected cells compared with control cells for OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, and BSEP, suggesting that olezarsen is not a substrate for these transporters. For BCRP and P-gp, the permeability value and the efflux ratio were not determinable due to olezarsen concentrations in all receiver wells being below the lower limit of quantitation (LLOQ). However, given the extremely low B→A permeability (&lt;0.0287 \times 10^{-6} cm/s), olezarsen does not appear to be a substrate for BCRP or P-gp.
L1875: Taken together, results from this study demonstrate that olezarsen is not a substrate or inhibitor of OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BCRP, P-gp, and BSEP.
L1876: ## 14.2. In Vivo Studies
L1877: ## A Placebo-Controlled, Dose-Escalation Study to Assess the Safety, Tolerability, Pharmacokinetics, and Pharmacodynamics of Single and Multiple Doses of ISIS 678354, a GalNAc3 Conjugated 2'-MOE Chimeric Antisense Oligonucleotide Targeting ApoC-III, Administered Subcutaneously to Healthy Volunteers with Elevated Triglycerides (Trial ISIS 678354-CS1)
L1878: ## Objectives
L1879: The objectives of the study were to evaluate the safety and tolerability, PK, and effects on pharmacodynamics (PD), including plasma ApoC-III, TG, total cholesterol (TC), low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C), high-density lipoprotein cholesterol (HDL-C), non-HDL-C, and very low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (VLDL-C), of single and multiple SC doses (both weekly and Q4W dosing regimens) of olezarsen in healthy volunteers with elevated TG.
L1880: ## Study Design
L1881: This was a Phase 1, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, single-center, dose-escalation study. There were five ascending single-dose cohorts (N=8 per cohort, total randomized 6 active:2 placebo) with olezarsen doses of 10, 30, 60, 90, and 120 mg. The posttreatment evaluation period was Day 2 through Day 150 for the single-dose cohorts.
L1882: Volunteers in the multiple-dose cohorts were evaluated on weekly dosing for 6 weeks and Q4W for 3 months. The olezarsen doses were 15 mg (Cohort AA) and 30 mg (Cohort BB) for the weekly dosing cohorts (N=8 per cohort, total randomized 6 active:2 placebo) and 60 mg for the Q4W dosing cohort (Cohort CC; N=10, total randomized 6 active:4 placebo). The post-treatment evaluation period was Day 37 through Day 155 for the weekly multiple-dose cohorts and Day 86 through Day 204 for the Q4W multiple-dose cohort.
L1883: ## Results
L1884: ## PK
L1885: Following SC administration, olezarsen was rapidly absorbed into the systemic circulation, with median T_{max} ranging from 1 to 3 hours. Plasma olezarsen concentrations then declined over time in a multiphasic manner, with an initial rapid distribution phase dominating the plasma clearance followed by a much slower elimination phase with apparent terminal half-life ranging from 3 to 6 weeks (Figure 44). Plasma C_{max} and area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) increased slightly greater than dose proportionally over the dose range of 10 to 120 mg of olezarsen. No increase in C_{max} or AUC was observed after repeated weekly or Q4W administration (Figure 45). These data support time-invariant plasma kinetics for olezarsen.
L1886: Rapid distributional clearance from plasma and limited recovery of full-length ASO in urine (&lt;1% of administered dose over a 24-hour period) suggests that clearance from plasma occurred primarily by distribution to tissues, where the drug is metabolized to chain-shortened oligonucleotide metabolites prior to renal excretion.

L1893: ## PD
L1894: PD results showed dose-dependent sustained reductions in fasting plasma ApoC-III and TG levels, with particularly marked, statistically significant, reductions across the higher single-dose and all multiple-dose cohorts. Among the single-dose cohorts, reductions of 12%, 11%, 43%, 68%, and 77% (TG) and 4%, 32%, 65%, 78%, and 91% (ApoC-III) for the 10, 30, 60, 90, and 120 mg groups, respectively, were seen at Day 15, 2 weeks after dosing (Figure 46). The effects were sustained at Day 30, with reductions of 38%, 60%, and 72% (TG) and 58%, 72%, and 87% (ApoC-III) for the 60, 90, and 120 mg groups, respectively.

L1898: Among the weekly multiple-dose cohorts, reductions of 61% and 71% (TG) and 65% and 84% (ApoC-III) for the 15 and 30 mg groups were seen at Day 43 (one week after the last dose), respectively (Figure 47). The effects were sustained at Day 64, with reductions of 56% and 59% (TG) and 63% and 79% (ApoC-III) for the 15 and 30 mg groups. Similarly, increases in HDL-C concentrations of 50% to 56% from baseline were observed at Day 43 in the 15 and 30 mg weekly multiple-dose groups, respectively.

The effects were sustained at Day 113, with reductions of 58% (TG) and 79% (ApoC-III) for the 60 mg group. In addition, increases in HDL-C concentrations of 76% from baseline were observed at Day 92 in the 60 mg monthly multiple-dose group. Among the multiple-dose cohorts, reductions in ApoB levels of approximately 20 to 30% were achieved, with no notable increases in any treatment group.

L1905: ## A Randomized Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Non-Confirmatory Study to Assess Safety, Tolerability, PK, and PD of Single Ascending and Multiple Doses of ISIS 678354 in Healthy Japanese Participants (Trial AKCEA-CS1)
L1906: ## Objectives
L1907: The objectives of the study were to assess the safety and tolerability (primary), PK (secondary), effects on fasting serum TGs and ApoC-III levels (exploratory), and immunogenicity of single and multiple SC dose administration of olezarsen in healthy Japanese participants.
L1908: ## Study Design
L1909: This was a randomized, placebo-controlled, participant and investigator-blinded, single ascending dose and multiple-dose study in healthy Japanese male and female participants conducted in the USA. In the single ascending dose portion, participants were randomized to olezarsen or placebo at each of three serial dose levels (30, 60, or 90 mg). For the multiple-dose part of this study, 60 mg of olezarsen or placebo was administered SC Q4W on Days 1, 29, 57, and 85.
L1910: ## Results
L1911: ## PK
L1912: Following single- or multiple-dose SC administration in healthy Japanese participants, olezarsen was absorbed rapidly into the systemic circulation, with median Tmax ranging from 2 to 3 hours. After reaching Cmax, plasma concentrations of olezarsen declined in a multi-phasic fashion, with a rapid disposition phase that dominated the plasma clearance, followed by a slower elimination phase with terminal elimination half-life of 8.2 to 27.5 days for single-dose cohorts and 30.8 days for the multiple-dose cohort. Of note, the half-life of 8.2 days observed following a single 30-mg dose of olezarsen is likely underestimated due to a majority of samples in the elimination phase being below the LLOQ. Plasma Cmax and AUC increased slightly greater than dose proportionally over a dose range of 30 to 90 mg. There was no increase in plasma Cmax or AUC after repeated Q4W administration of 60 mg olezarsen for 13 weeks.
L1913: The fraction of olezarsen excreted over 24 hours ranged from 0.0264% to 0.0741%, suggesting negligible renal elimination. Renal clearance over the first 24 hours also increased with dose and ranged from 6.24 mL/h to 12.0 mL/h.
L1914: ## PD
L1915: ## *Following Single SC Administration of 30 mg, 60 mg, or 90 mg ISIS 678354*
L1916: Following single SC doses of olezarsen at 30 mg, 60 mg, and 90 mg, mean percent decreases in TG levels from baseline were observed beginning on Day 3 and continued through Day 120. The most considerable effect of the administration of olezarsen on plasma TG levels compared with placebo appeared to occur on Day 15 postdose; the percentage differences (p-values) were -62.36% (0.124), -97.24% (0.021), and -83.78 (0.046) for the 30 mg, 60 mg, and 90 mg treatment groups, respectively. The percentage change in TG levels from baseline does not appear to be dose-dependent; the effect on TG levels appeared similar following single administration of either 60 mg or 90 mg ISIS 678354, and smaller following administration of 30 mg ISIS 678354.
L1917: Significant percentage decreases in ApoC-III levels following administration of ISIS 678354 compared with placebo were observed beginning on Day 8 in the 60 mg and 90 mg treatment groups and beginning on Day 15 in the 30 mg treatment group, with percentage differences (p-values) -61.02% (0.0004), -64.77% (0.0002), and -39.40 (0.0103), respectively. Significant decreases in ApoC-III levels continued to Day 90 postdose in the 60 mg and 90 mg treatment groups, and to Day 30 postdose in the 30 mg treatment group, with percentage differences (p-values) -39.84% (0.0023), -22.35% (0.0303), and -41.09 (0.0092), respectively. The most considerable effect of the administration of 30 mg, 60 mg, and 90 mg ISIS 678354 on plasma ApoC-III levels compared with placebo appeared to occur on Day 30, Day 8, and Day 15 postdose, respectively.
L1918: ## *Following Multiple SC Administration of 60 mg ISIS 678354 Every 4 Weeks for a Total of Four Doses (Day 1, Day 29, Day 57, Day 85)*
L1919: Following four SC doses (Day 1, Day 29, Day 57, and Day 85) of 60 mg olezarsen Q4W, mean percent decreases from baseline in TG levels were consistently observed from Day 2 to Day 204, 119 days after the last dose. The percentage change in TG from baseline ranged from -73.8% on
L1920: NDA 218614
Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L1921: Day 92 to -31.7% on Day 204. The most considerable effect of the administration of ISIS 678354 on plasma TG levels compared with placebo appeared to occur on Day 113 where the percentage difference (p-value) was -71.96% (0.004).
L1922: Percentage decreases in ApoC-III levels from baseline results and differences in ApoC-III levels compared with placebo were consistently decreased from Day 2 to Day 204. The percentage change in ApoC-III from baseline ranged from -81.6% on Day 92 to -26.3% on Day 204. The most considerable effect of the administration of ISIS 678354 on plasma ApoC-III levels compared with placebo appeared to occur on Day 92 postdose where the percentage difference (p-value) was -81.63% (0.0002).
L1923: ## Immunogenicity
L1924: In single-dose cohorts, all participants who received placebo treatment had negative ADA results during the study. Overall, in the single-dose cohorts, three (15.0%) participants were assessed as ADA positive during the study (two [40.0%] participants in the 60 mg ISIS 678354 treatment group and one [16.7%] participant in the 90 mg ISIS 678354 treatment group). There was no association or trend between the incidence of ADA and dose level.
L1925: In multiple-dose cohorts, two (100%) participants who received placebo treatment had positive ADA results at Day 1 (titer of 50 for both participants), and three (50.0%) participants in the 60 mg ISIS 678354 treatment group were assessed as ADA positive at postdose time points during the study. There was a trend of increased ADA incidence with increased number of doses. No participants who tested ADA positive reported any TEAEs during the study.
L1926: ## A Single-Dose, Randomized, Open-Label, Two-Period Crossover Bioequivalence Study Comparing Two Subcutaneous Formulations: Vial and Autoinjector With Olezarsen, at Two Dose Levels, in Healthy Adult Participants (Trial ISIS 678354-CS20)
L1927: ## Objectives
L1928: ## Primary Objective
L1929: The primary objective of this study was to assess the bioequivalence of olezarsen between two SC formulations at two dose levels: (1) autoinjector ((b)(4) mg/mL) and vial (100 mg/mL) at the 50 mg dose; and (2) autoinjector (100 mg/mL) and vial (100 mg/mL) at the 80 mg dose, in healthy adult participants.
L1930: The primary endpoint was to assess whether the bioequivalence at each dose level calculated at the 90% CI for the ratio of geometric means (GMs) for AUC0-336h and Cmax for olezarsen fell within 80.00% to 125.00%.
L1931: ## Exploratory Objective
L1932: The exploratory objective of this study was to assess serum ApoC-III reduction from baseline over time (pre-dose through 91 days post-last dose).
L1933: ## Study Design
L1934: This was a Phase 1, single-dose, randomized, open-label, 2-period, 2-sequence, 2-treatment, 2-way crossover, BE study that evaluated two dose levels of olezarsen (50 mg and 80 mg).
L1935: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L1936: Following an up to 28-day screening period, 104 eligible participants were randomized to two dose groups (50 mg or 80 mg). There was a 28- to 42-day washout period between Day 1 of treatment period 1 and Day 1 of treatment period 2 to avoid PK carry over effects of the preceding treatment. Blood samples for PK analyses were collected at pre-dose (0 hour) and at 0.5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 12, 24, 36, 48, 96, 168, and 336 hours postdose in each of the two treatment periods and during the post-treatment follow-up period (28, 56, and 91 days post-last dose). Blood samples for PD analysis (i.e., serum ApoC-III and serum TGs) were collected during treatment periods 1 and 2 on Day -1, Day 1 (pre-dose), Day 8, and Day 15 and the post-treatment follow-up period (28, 56, and 91 days post-last dose).
L1937: ## Results
L1938: ## PK
L1939: The PK exposures after administration of the four treatments are presented in Figure 49 (olezarsen 50 mg, semi-log scale), Figure 50 (olezarsen 80 mg, semi-log scale), and are summarized in Table 110. The geometric least square (LS) means and ratios for olezarsen AUC_{0-336h} and C_{max} comparing the autoinjector to the vial at the 50 and 80 mg dose levels are summarized in Table 111.

L1966: ## Conclusions
L1967: - The primary endpoint of BE between the autoinjector and the vial presentations based on AUC_{0-336h} was achieved for both olezarsen 50 mg and 80 mg dose levels.
- Bioequivalence between the autoinjector and the vial presentations based on C_{max} was achieved for the olezarsen 50 mg dose level. For the olezarsen 80 mg dose level, the geometric mean ratio 90% CI for C_{max} was slightly above the standard upper CI limit for BE (80.00% to 125.00%), by 4.99%; however, this small difference is not clinically relevant due to the short duration around C_{max} during which plasma concentration versus time profiles
L1968: showed a small difference, and the rest of the concentration-time profiles were nearly superimposable.

- The PD effect of olezarsen on ApoC-III and TG reduction was similar between autoinjector and vial presentations at either the olezarsen 50 mg or 80 mg dose, which also supports that the small difference in C_{max} at the olezarsen 80 mg dose is not clinically relevant.
- There were no clinically meaningful differences in safety profile and ISRs between the autoinjector and vial presentations for olezarsen at either olezarsen 50 mg or 80 mg dose.
L1969: ## 14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and Performance
L1970: ## Quantification of Olezarsen Concentrations in Human Plasma
L1971: Briefly, olezarsen was quantitatively measured directly from human plasma using a hybridization ECL assay. In this assay, samples were treated with proteinase K to mitigate potential interference from ADAs. Olezarsen in samples was hybridized to a complementary capture probe, which was biotinylated on the 5'-end. The hybridized olezarsen was then immobilized on a streptavidin-coated MesoScale Discovery (MSD) plate. A detection probe labeled with ruthenium (MSD-Tag) on the 3'-end is added to the plate to bind to the free portion of olezarsen. After addition of read buffer, the plate was read in an MSD Sector PRTM 600 plate reader.
L1972: The method validation evaluated the quantification range, accuracy, precision, potential interference, and cross-reactivity to selected putative metabolites, hook effect, dilutional linearity, selectivity, matrix effect, hemolysis and lipemia effects, potential ADA interference, quantification of unconjugated olezarsen (ISIS 304801, volanesorsen) and total full-length ASO, and stability. The validation range was 0.015 nM (0.13 ng/mL) to 20 nM (173.7 ng/mL), with the low end of the range defining the LLOQ.
L1973: Validation results demonstrated that the method was suitable for quantification of olezarsen and unconjugated olezarsen. The method performance over the validation is provided in Table 114.

L1982: ## **Quantification of Olezarsen Concentrations in Human Urine**
L1983: Olezarsen in human urine was quantitatively measured using a partially validated hybridization ELISA. Olezarsen was hybridized to a complementary cutting probe (678354LNARP), which was biotinylated at the 5'-end of the sequence and had digoxigenin at the 3'-end. The hybridized solution was then placed in a NeutrAvidin-coated ELISA plate. After binding had taken place, S1 nuclease was added to digest any unhybridized probes. An anti-digoxigenin antibody conjugated to alkaline phosphatase was used to detect the hybridized analyte by binding to the digoxigenin label. AttoPhos was used as a substrate for fluorometric readout. Fluorescence intensity was determined using a fluorescent plate reader. The hybridization ELISA is capable of quantifying olezarsen and unconjugated olezarsen (ISIS 304801) equivalently; therefore, the assay is capable of quantifying total full-length ASOs of olezarsen, using olezarsen for calibration. The method performance over the validation is provided in Table 115.

L1991: ## 14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment—Impact of PK/PD, Efficacy, and Safety
L1992: A validated qualitative ELISA method was used for the detection and assessment of anti-olezarsen antibodies in human plasma in a multi-tiered approach: (1) screening assay, which identifies initial positive or negative samples; (2) confirmation assay, which assesses specificity of the positive screen samples by free-drug competition; and (3) titration assay, which estimates the level of antibody for the confirmed positive samples. Assay sensitivity was determined using affinity purified rabbit anti-olezarsen IgG and was defined as 9.65 ng/mL and 39.1 ng/mL in neat human plasma for the screening and confirmatory assays, respectively. The assay's ability to
L1993: ## NDA 218614
## Tryngolza (olezarsen sodium)
L1994: detect anti-olezarsen antibodies in the presence of circulating olezarsen (drug tolerance) was determined to be 39.1 ng/mL of antibody in the presence of up to 4000 ng/mL of olezarsen. No neutralizing assay was developed.
L1995: ADA sampling was obtained from patients with FCS from Trial ISIS 678354-CS3 on Days 1, 15, 29, 57, 85, 169, 253, 365, and follow-up visit. Treatment-emergent (treatment-induced and treatment-boosted) ADAs against olezarsen were detected in 38.1% (8/21) of patients in the 50 mg olezarsen group, 45.5% (10/22) of patients in the 80 mg olezarsen group, and 0% (0/23) of patients who received placebo. As a reference, the proportion of patients with ADAs detected at baseline were 38.1% (8/21), 27.3% (6/22), and 43.5% (10/23) of patients in the 50 mg olezarsen, 80 mg olezarsen, and pooled placebo groups, respectively; an overall baseline positive rate of 36.4% (24/66). Of note, 26 out of the 66 patients in the study had previous treatment with volanesorsen. The baseline positive rate was 53.8% (14/26) and 25.0% (10/40) in patients with and without previous volanesorsen treatment, respectively.
L1996: ## **Impact of Immunogenicity on Pharmacokinetics**
L1997: Higher plasma olezarsen C_{\text{trough}} levels were consistently observed in ADA-positive patients (either treatment-unaffected or treatment-emergent ADA) compared with ADA-negative patients across olezarsen clinical studies. In Trial ISIS 678354-CS3 specifically, mean trough plasma olezarsen concentrations in the olezarsen 80 mg group were elevated up to approximately 10-fold in FCS patients with treatment-unaffected and treatment-emergent ADA relative to ADA-negative patients, and the corresponding elevations for patients in the olezarsen 50 mg group were up to approximately 5-fold relative to ADA-negative patients.
L1998: The mean (+SD) olezarsen trough plasma concentrations over time stratified by ADA category in patients with FCS in Trial ISIS 678354-CS3 are presented in Figure 51.

L2022: ## Impact of Immunogenicity on Safety
L2023: In Trial ISIS 678354-CS3, there does not appear to be any association of patient ADA status (negative, treatment-unaffected, or treatment-emergent) with the frequency of TEAEs, their severity, or their relationship to treatment, nor are there any such trends for serious TEAEs.
L2024: For other adverse events of interest (OAEIs), there was no apparent correlation between ISRs, bleeding, thrombocytopenia, renal, or hepatic TEAEs and ADA status. With respect to hypersensitivity OAEIs, there was no obvious relationship between hypersensitivity reactions
L2025: and ADA status, though small sample sizes limit interpretation of the data. For more details, please refer to Section 7.7.8.
L2026: ## 14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
L2027: The population PK, exposure-ApoC-III, and exposure-TG modeling was considered reasonable for describing olezarsen, ApoC-III, and TG concentrations following a single or multiple dose administration of olezarsen and for performing PK and PD simulations to predict steady-state olezarsen exposure metrics and the effect on ApoC-III and TG levels. The PK and PD modelling were utilized to support the current submission as outlined below.
L2028: **Table 116. Utility of the Population PK Modeling**
L2029: Utility of the Final Model	Reviewer's Comments
The PK model included weight as a covariate on CL/F, Vc/F, Q/F, Vp/F, and k_a. Japanese participants had 29% higher Vc/F. Both k_a and relative F were higher for the autoinjector compared to the vial by 13% and 5%, respectively. ADA positive status with ADA titer of 100 was associated with 30% decrease in CL/F.
The model-predicted PK was used to develop 2 independent exposure-response models, describing the relationship between olezarsen concentrations and either apoC-III or triglyceride (TG) concentrations.
The exposure-apoC-III model estimated an IC_{50} (concentration at half maximum inhibition effect on apoC-III production rate) of 0.44 ng/mL, in patients with FCS. The Applicant's model estimated a 30% increase in IC_{50} in ADA positive subjects with an ADA titer of 50. The exposure-TG model estimated an EC_{50} (concentration at half maximum effect on TG elimination rate constant) of 1.48 ng/mL, in patients with FCS. The Applicant's model estimated an increase in EC_{50} with an ADA titer above 50.
The PK and exposure-response models were used to perform population simulations for olezarsen 50 mg or 80 mg, administered every 28 days (Q4W) or 30 days (Q1M), to support dose selection in patients with FCS. The Applicant's simulations indicated that the PK, apoC-III, and TG profiles for Q4W and Q1M dosing overlap following first dose and at steady-state with only marginal difference in median olezarsen concentrations, percentage change from baseline in apoC-III and TG for the respective doses and dosing regimens.
The PK model was considered reasonable in predicting median and individual observed concentrations of olezarsen and their variability in healthy volunteers, patients with FCS and patients with HTG and CVD.
ADA titer was found to be the most influential covariate on AUC and Ctrough. ADA titer did not affect C_{max}. About 97% of subjects and PK samples collected had ADA titer below or equal to 3200, which was estimated to result in 194% and 400% increase in median steady-state AUC (AUC_{ss}) and Ctrough (C_{trough,ss}), compared to ADA negative subjects. At the mean ADA titer of 800, median AUC_{ss} and C_{trough,ss} are predicted to increase by 120% and 250% compared to ADA negative subjects. Although ADA titers are expected to increase C_{trough,ss} and AUC_{ss}, the estimated C_{trough,ss} remain well below C_{max,ss}.
The Applicant's exposure-apoC-III and exposure-TG models are acceptable and adequately describes the median and individual observed apoC-III and TG concentrations and their variability in healthy volunteers, patients with FCS and patients with HTG and CVD.
The Applicant did not provide the rationale and physiological explanation for including ADA status and titer on IC_{50}. The effect of ADA status/titer could be confounded by another unexplored covariate and looks driven only by study ISIS 678354-CS3 in FCS patients. The model without ADA status and titer effect did not affect the PD model parameters, except for the effect of FCS disease on IC_{50}, suggesting that FCS patients are estimated to IC_{50} of 0.63 ng/mL).
L2030: Utility of the Final Model	Reviewer's Comments
The Applicant performed exploratory analysis to evaluate the relationship between the change from baseline (CFB) in platelet count versus cumulative AUC (cAUC) and C_{max} in studies ISIS 678354-CS3 and -CS8, concluding that there were no substantial trends in the observed change from baseline or the maximum decrease from baseline in platelet count across the entire range of cumulative AUC or C_{max}.
The proposed doses of 80 mg olezarsen administered Q4W or Q1M are acceptable and estimated to achieve, at the end of the dosing interval (at C_{trough,ss}), a percent reduction from baseline in apoC-III of 74% for Q4W or 71% for Q1M, and percent reductions from baseline in TG of 32% for Q4W and 30% for Q1M, in patients with FCS.
The reviewer's assessment of the relationship between olezarsen exposure metrics and the maximum reduction in platelet count is relatively shallow, with most patients experiencing less than grade 1 thrombocytopenia, and most patients with highest maximum decrease in platelets counts having no ADA or maximum ADA titer lower or equal 800. Similar pattern was observed for percent maximum decrease from baseline in platelets counts and exposure-metrics.
L2031: Source: FDA reviewer

L2033: ## 14.5.1. Applicant's PK Analysis
L2034: The Applicant developed a population PK model for olezarsen to characterize the PK properties of olezarsen administered SC. The data for PK modeling were collected from three Phase 1 studies in healthy subjects (ISIS 678354-CS1, ISIS 678354-CS20, and AKCEA-CS1), one Phase 2 study (ISIS 678354-CS2) in patients with HTG and established CVD or at high risk for CVD, and two Phase 3 studies (ISIS 678354-CS3 and -CS8) in patients with FCS and in patients with CVD and HTG, respectively (Table 117).
L2035: The PK dataset consisted of 6535 quantifiable PK samples from 415 subjects. Concentrations below the limit of quantification (n=419, &lt;0.13 ng/mL) were excluded from the analysis dataset as they represented 6% (less than 10%) of all the data.
L2036: Table 118 summarizes the demographic characteristics of subjects included in the PK analyses, stratified by study. Table 119 summarizes the ADA titers by study. Most patients (85%, n=122) had ADA titers between 50 and 800, 12% of patients (n=17) had ADA titers between 1600 and 3200, 3.4% of patients (n=5) had ADA titers between 6400 and 25600, among whom one patient had a single transient peak ADA at 51200.

L2063: ## **Final Population PK Model**
L2064: The PK model for olezarsen consisted of a two-compartment disposition model with first-order absorption and a linear elimination from the central compartment. Between subject variability (BSV) terms were included on apparent clearance (CL/F), apparent central volume (Vc/F), apparent inter-compartmental clearance (Q/F), apparent peripheral volume (Vp/F), and the absorption rate (k_a). Between-occasion variability was included on CL/F and Vp/F, where occasion is the time-varying ADA positivity status (binary variable, with ADA positive status defined from time of first ADA appearance). The proportional residual unexplained variability (RUV) was implemented as an additive residual error on the log-scale. The parameter estimates from the final octreotide PK model are listed in Table 120.
L2065: Weight was included as a covariate on CL/F, Vc/F, Q/F, Vp/F, and k_a. Japanese participants (i.e., Trial AKCEA-CS1) had 29% higher Vc/F compared to the other ethnicities across the studies. Both k_a and relative F were higher for the autoinjector compared to the vial by 13% and 5%, respectively. ADA-positive status with ADA titer of 100 was associated with 30% decrease in CL/F.
L2066: The goodness of fit (GOF) plots from the final PK model are shown in Figure 55 and the prediction-corrected visual predict check (pcVPC) plots are shown in Figure 56.
L2067: ## **Reviewer Comment and Assessment of the Population PK Model**
L2068: Overall, the Applicant's PK model is acceptable and adequately describes the individual observed concentrations of olezarsen and their variability in healthy volunteers, patients with FCS and patients with HTG and CVD.
L2069: - The PK parameters from the PK model were estimated with an acceptable precision (&lt;23% relative standard error [RSE]). The estimated interindividual random error (eta-) shrinkage was low (&lt;30%) and acceptable for the reliable graphical assessment of covariate effects, except for k_a (eta-shrinkage =55%). The residual error (epsilon-) shrinkage was low (9%), indicating the informativeness of the GOF plots to diagnose structural and residual error model misspecifications. The GOF plots show that the PK model fitted the observed data well with no critical trends or bias in the predicted versus the observed concentrations of olezarsen and in the conditional weighted residuals (CWRES) versus predictions or time from all studies and olezarsen dose levels.
- The pcVPC plots, stratified by ADA status, indicate that the Applicant's PK model reasonably captures and describes the observed median olezarsen concentrations and their variability from all studies with minimal bias.
- ADA titer was found to be the most influential covariate on CL/F, AUC and C_{\text{trough}}. Figure 57 summarizes the predicted effect of ADA titer on the steady-state exposure metrics (AUC_{\text{ss}}, C_{\text{max,ss}} and C_{\text{trough,ss}}).

L2095: About 97% of subjects and PK samples collected had ADA titer below or equal to 3200, which was estimated to result in 194% and 400% increase in median AUCss and Ctrough,ss compared to ADA-negative subjects, respectively. At the mean ADA titer of 800, median AUCss and Ctrough,ss are predicted to increase by 120% and 250% compared to ADA-negative subjects. Although ADA titers are expected to increase Ctrough,ss and AUCss, the estimated Ctrough,ss are expected to remain well below Cmax,ss, which are not affected by ADA titer effect.
L2096: ## 14.5.2. Applicant's Exposure-Response Analyses
L2097: The Applicant used the model-predicted PK to develop two exposure-response models, using sequential PK-PD modeling. The exposure-response models described the relationship between olezarsen concentrations and either ApoC-III concentrations or TG concentrations.
L2098: The exposure-ApoC-III dataset consisted of 4518 quantifiable ApoC-III concentrations paired with PK information from 415 subjects. ApoC-III concentrations below the limit of quantification (n=196, &lt;0.94 mg/dL) were excluded from the analysis dataset as they represented less than 10% of all the data. The exposure-TG dataset consisted of 4728 TG concentrations paired with PK information from 415 subjects. No trends in ApoC-III or TG levels over time were observed for placebo subjects, hence, placebo subjects were excluded from the analyses.
L2099: ## 14.5.2.1. Exposure-ApoC-III Model
L2100: The exposure-ApoC-III model consisted of an indirect response model with an inhibitory drug effect on ApoC-III production rate (K_{in}). The drug effect on K_{in} was described as an inhibitory E_{max} function with maximum inhibition (I_{max}) and a concentration at half maximum inhibition (IC_{50}). BSV terms were included on the ApoC-III removal rate constant (K_{out}) and IC_{50} parameters. The RUV in ApoC-III concentrations was implemented using a proportional residual error model, with separate RUV estimated by ADA status.
L2101: Table 121 summarizes the parameter estimates of the final exposure-ApoC-III model. The model identified disease status as a covariate on K_{out} and IC_{50}, with FCS patients having 51% lower K_{out} and 80% increase in IC_{50} compared to healthy volunteers (HV). The Applicant's model estimated a 30% increase in IC_{50} in ADA-positive subjects with an ADA titer of 50.

L2117: ## Reviewer Comment and Assessment of the Exposure-ApoC-III Model
L2118: The Applicant's exposure-ApoC-III model is acceptable and adequately describes the individual observed ApoC-III concentrations and their variability in healthy volunteers, patients with FCS and patients with HTG and CVD.
L2119: - The PD parameters were estimated with an acceptable precision (&lt;30% RSE). The estimated interindividual random error (\eta-) shrinkage was low (\le 30\%) and acceptable for the reliable graphical assessment of covariate effects. The residual error (\epsilon-) shrinkage was low (&lt;6%), indicating the informativeness of the GOF plots to diagnose structural and residual error model misspecifications. The GOF plots show that the exposure-ApoC-III model fitted the observed data well with no critical trends or bias in the predicted versus the observed concentrations of ApoC-III and in the CWRES versus predictions or time from all studies and olezarsen dose levels.
- The pcVPC plots, stratified by disease status, indicate that the Applicant's exposure-ApoC-III model reasonably captures and describes the observed median ApoC-III concentrations and their variability from all studies with minimal bias.
- The Applicant did not provide the rationale and physiological explanation for including ADA status and titer on IC_{50}. The effect of ADA status/titer could be confounded by another unexplored covariate and appears to be driven only by Trial ISIS 678354-CS3 in FCS patients. The removal of ADA status and titer on IC_{50} from the exposure-ApoC-III model significantly increased the objective function value by 129 points (for 2 degrees of freedom [df]). The model without ADA status and titer effect did not change the estimated BSV for IC_{50} or the estimated PD model parameters, except for the effect of FCS disease on IC_{50}
L2120: which increased from 1.8 to 2.54 (i.e., 41% difference in the parameter estimate), suggesting that FCS patients are estimated to have 154% increase in IC50 (IC50 of 0.63 ng/mL) compared to HV (IC50 of 0.247 ng/mL).
L2121: ## 14.5.2.2. Exposure-TG Model
L2122: The exposure-TG model consisted of an indirect response model with a stimulatory drug effect on TG removal (elimination) rate constant (Kout). The drug effect on Kout was described as an Emax function with maximum effect (Emax) and a concentration at half maximum effect (EC50). BSV terms were included on the Kout, Emax and EC50 parameters. A proportional error model was used to describe the RUV of TG concentrations, with separate RUV estimated for patients with FCS and subject without FCS (including HV).
L2123: Table 122 summarizes the parameter estimates of the final exposure-TG model. The model identified disease status as a covariate on Kout and EC50. Patients with HTG or CVD experienced an approximate 61% reduction in Kout compared to HV or FCS patients. Patients with FCS have 619% increase in EC50 compared to HV or patients with HTG or CVD. The Applicant's model estimated an increase in EC50 with an ADA titer above 50.
